The Airy line ensemble at the rough-smooth boundary
Sunil Chhita, Duncan Dauvergne, Thomas Finn

Abstract
. We study the rough-smooth boundary in the two-periodic Aztec diamond, a random domino tiling model exhibiting three types of macroscopic regions. We show that the height function at this boundary converges to an independent sum of an Airy surface and an i.i.d. noise field with fluctuations governed by the full-plane smooth phase. Going further, we prove convergence of a family of Temperleyan backbone paths to the Airy line ensemble. This gives the first convergence result for a family of undirected paths converging to the Airy line ensemble, as well as Airy convergence at a noisy boundary.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
60K35, 82B23, 82B20

Figure 1
.
Temperleyan paths for the two-periodic Aztec diamond (
n=1200,a=0.5
).

1.
Introduction

The two-periodic Aztec diamond with bias parameter
a\in(0,1)
is a domino tiling model of a certain diamond shaped region of the square grid, known as an Aztec diamond. Each domino (
1\times 2
or
2\times 1
rectangle) has weight
1
or
a
which depends on its location in a doubly periodic fashion. Large two-periodic Aztec diamonds exhibit three types of macroscopic regions, as characterized for random tiling models in
[
KOS06
]
. These regions are known as
frozen
, where tiles form a deterministic brickwork pattern;
rough
, where there is polynomial decay of correlations between tiles; and
smooth
, where there is exponential decay of correlations between tiles. In the simulation in
Figure

2
, we can identify four frozen regions covering the corners of the Aztec diamond, a smooth region sitting in the center of the Aztec diamond (the region with no long structures), and a doubly-connected rough region which occupies the rest of the Aztec diamond. There are other random tiling models which exhibit all three types of macroscopic regions, such as the three-periodic lozenge tilings
[
Kui25
]
, as well as other statistical mechanical models, such as the six vertex model with domain-wall boundary conditions and a certain choice of parameters
[
AR05
]
. The two-periodic Aztec diamond is one of the simplest such models.

Figure 2
.
A simulation of a two-periodic Aztec diamond with
n=200
and
a=0.7
with the
a
-dominoes shaded darker than the
b
-dominoes. Note the underlying square grid is rotated by
\pi/4
, as is our coordinate convention in this paper.

One of the original motivations for studying the two-periodic Aztec diamond as articulated in
[
CY14
,
CJ16
]
was to understand the probabilistic behavior at the rough-smooth boundary and to compare to the known probabilistic behavior at the frozen-rough boundary. Indeed, universality at the frozen-rough boundary has been recently achieved for a large class of lozenge tilings
[
AH25
]
, see also
[
OR03
,
FS03
,
Joh05
,
BKMM07
,
Pet14
,
DM18
]
for earlier tiling results and
[
Gor21
]
for more references and background.
In tiling models, there is usually a set of lattice paths that combinatorially defines the boundary between the frozen and rough regions. In the microscopic limit at the interface, these lattice paths converge to the
Airy line ensemble
, a random sequence of continuous functions
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathcal{A}_{i}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},i\in\mathbb{N}\}
with the ordering
\mathcal{A}_{1}>\mathcal{A}_{2}>\cdots
believed to govern edge fluctuations of many statistical mechanical models. The Airy line ensemble also appears as a scaling limit in random matrix theory
[
Mac94
,
FNH99
,
AVM05
]
and for growth models in the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang universality class, e.g. see
[
PS02
,
IS07
,
CH13
,
DNV23
,
Wu23
,
ACH24
]
.

The Airy line ensemble is also expected to govern the fluctuations at the rough-smooth boundary in random tilings. However, here the picture is much murkier than at the rough-smooth boundary. One of the main difficulties is that unlike at the frozen-rough boundary, the rough-smooth boundary features mixing between two non-deterministic regions. This makes the boundary noisy, and means that there is no local combinatorial description of a boundary or a canonical ensemble of paths. Moreover, when one does try to define natural families of paths they are undirected, i.e. they cannot be written as graphs of one-dimensional functions and may feature significant backtracking or winding.

Our goal in this paper is to overcome these challenges and understand precisely what is happening at the rough-smooth boundary in the two-periodic Aztec diamond. Our first theorem shows convergence of the height function at the rough-smooth boundary. For this theorem, we define the
Airy surface

\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\{0,1,\dots\}
by

\mathcal{A}(t,x)=\#\{i\in\mathbb{N}:\mathcal{A}_{i}(t)\geq x\},

which should be viewed as the height function for the Airy line ensemble.

Theorem 1

(Informal version of
2.1
)
.

Fix
a\in(0,1)
. Let
\mathcal{H}_{n}
be the height function of the two-periodic Aztec diamond of size
n
, and let
H_{n}
be the average of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
in the smooth region. Let
[\cdot]_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a scaling map which takes limiting coordinates to an
O(n^{2/3})\times O(n^{1/3})
-sized box where the rough-smooth boundary meets the antidiagonal in the two-periodic Aztec diamond of size
n
. Then, as
n\to\infty
, for any finite set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

(H_{n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}[u]_{n},u\in S)\implies(4\mathcal{A}(u)+X_{u},u\in S)

where
\mathcal{A}
is the Airy surface and
X=(X_{u},u\in F)
is an i.i.d. vector, independent of
\mathcal{A}
, whose individual entries are given by the height of a face in the full-plane smooth phase.

1
makes precise the idea that the rough-smooth boundary is given by an Airy line ensemble
\mathcal{A}
sitting on top of a smooth background, represented by the
X
. The central height
H_{n}
itself converges in the limit to a discrete Gaussian random variable, see the definition (
3
) and surrounding discussion.

While
2.1
confirms the idea that the height profile at the rough-smooth boundary looks like an Airy surface sitting in a smooth background, it says nothing about the existence of a family of paths converging to the Airy line ensemble.
Most of the paper is devoted to understanding such a family. In particular, we are able to identify a ‘top path’ which converges to the Airy process. The paths we consider are
south and north backbone paths
, introduced briefly in
[
CJ16
]
, which arise from a version of Temperley’s bijection. See
Section

2.3
for details and
Figure

3
for an example. Let
\{\mathcal{S}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n/2}
be the collection of south backbone paths starting on the bottom boundary and labelled from left to right. We let
1\leq I\leq n
be the (random) index such that
\mathcal{S}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{S}_{I}
terminate on the left boundary and
\mathcal{S}_{I+1},\dots,\mathcal{S}_{n}
terminate on the right. We can now state our main theorem.

Figure 3
.
The same simulation as in
Figure

2
, with an overlay of south (
\mathcal{S}
) and north (
\mathcal{N}
) backbone paths from the bottom and top of the Aztec diamond respectively.

Theorem 2

(Informal version of
2.2
)
.

Fix
a\in(0,1)
. Around the main anti-diagonal of the Aztec diamond, as
n\to\infty
, the collection
\{\mathcal{S}_{I+1-i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
converges after rescaling to the Airy line ensemble, in the sense of finite dimensional distributions (FDD). This convergence can be taken jointly with the convergence in
2
.

Note that since the curves
\mathcal{S}_{i}
are general plane curves whereas the Airy line ensemble consists of one-dimensional functions, certain projections need to be taken to make sense of the FDD convergence above. Note that the splitting point
I
retains randomness even in the limit, and ends up corresponding with high probability to the central height
H_{n}
from
1
.

To our knowledge, our results are the first to show Airy process or Airy line ensemble convergence for a model in a noisy background, or a model whose boundary is given by genuinely two-dimensional lattice paths. Indeed, without these difficulties the kind of FDD convergence in
1
and
2
would be immediate from the extended Airy kernel convergence established in
[
BCJ18
]
, as is the case for other models. In our setting we need to worry about even the most basic properties of the model, such as establishing the presence of a last path at the boundary, controlling global or mesoscopic backtracks and path winding, and teasing out the differences between the local and global contributions to the height function. What saves us is that we are working with a highly integrable model which is amenable to refined computations, and we have a Temperley-type correspondence with certain biased spanning forest measures, which allows us to import probabilistic tools coming from Wilson’s algorithm and biased loop-erased random walks. We discuss our analysis in much greater detail below after the precise statement of results.

1.1.
Previous work on the model

Recent progress on the two-periodic Aztec diamond and its generalizations has hinged on an inherent algebraic structure. This algebraic structure yields exact expressions for the determinantal point process correlations associated to the two-periodic Aztec diamond in three different ways
[
CY14
,
DK21
,
BD19
]
which rely on the same structure
[
CD23
,
KP25
]
. Moreover, this algebraic structure has also been exploited to introduce bias on the orientation of dominoes
[
BD23
]
, consider higher periodicities where the model has multiple smooth regions
[
Ber21
,
BB25
]
, split weights where the model has two different adjacent smooth phases
[
She25
]
,
as well as completely general weights
[
BdT24
]
. One feature of all of the aforementioned papers is that the correlation kernel associated to the determinantal process is amenable to precise asymptotic analysis, which gives access to limit shape curves and certain local statistics. Indeed, this has been exploited for the two-periodic Aztec diamond to find local limit laws
[
CJ16
,
DK21
]
, Airy kernel asymptotics at the rough-smooth boundary
[
CJ16
,
BCJ18
]
, sine-Gordon asymptotics in the
a\to 1
regime
[
Mas22
]
, precise dimer-dimer correlations at this boundary
[
JM23b
]
, and Gaussian free field fluctuations
[
BN25
]
. This final result also holds for more general periodicities. More recently, this integrable structure has been to shown to hold for certain randomly weighted domino tilings of the Aztec diamond
[
DP25
]
.

An alternate way to try to define the rough-smooth boundary is to introduce contractions to certain faces, a process called squishing
[
BCJ22
]
, which results in a set of lattice paths known as squished paths. From simulations, these squished paths seem to determine the rough-smooth boundary and it was shown there that the squished paths are responsible, in a certain sense, for producing the Airy kernel point process found in
[
BCJ18
]
provided that
0<a<1/3
.
Part of the reason is that the squished paths are the level lines of the height function when restricted to certain faces of the Aztec diamond. Furthermore, if
a=n^{-\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in(2/3,1)
, then as
n\to\infty
the squished path separating rough and smooth interfaces converges to the Airy process
[
JM23a
]
. This result relies on the fact that the squished path, although combinatorially is a two-dimensional path, becomes a one-dimensional path with high probability if
a
is sufficiently small given
n
, which makes analysis much easier than the setup considered here. We note that for all
a
, the squished and backbone paths should differ on a scale which is
o(n^{1/3})
and hence converge to the same Airy limit. However, even our most optimistic ideas did not show this for
a
greater than some small constant
a_{0}
. Indeed, we ran into many of the same issues around crude loop and path counting which lead to the upper bound of
1/3
in
[
BCJ22
]
, and significant new ideas would be needed to prove a version of
2
for squished paths in the full range of
a
.

1.2.
Boundaries beyond tilings and vertex models

Compared to the frozen-rough boundary, the rough-smooth boundary in random tilings is much closer in character to interfaces that appear in less integrable random surface models. In particular, there are many models where we expect Airy line ensemble convergence at noisy interfaces and where natural families of boundary paths are undirected, e.g. contour lines in the 3D Ising model or the
(2+1)
-dimensional SOS model and variants in certain parameter ranges, see
[
IST15
,
IV16
,
FS23
]
and references therein. Note that in many settings, the predicted limit laws are slightly different objects
(e.g. Dyson-Ferrari-Spohn diffusions and related objects
[
IVW18
,
BRCCG25
]
) which exhibit their own limit transition to the Airy line ensemble
[
FS23
,
DS25b
]
.

Because of the many extra difficulties in these and related models, establishing Airy line ensemble convergence in any regime seems incredibly difficult. However, there has nonetheless been substantial recent progress studying level lines and establishing limit laws in non-Airy regimes, e.g.  see
[
CLM+16
,
CKL24
,
CL25
]
. It would be interesting to see if ideas used in those settings could have relevance in random tiling or vertex models, where there is extra integrability but rough-smooth boundaries are still difficult to understand.

1.3.
Outline of the paper

The next two sections are essentially an extended introduction.
Section

2
gives precise definitions and statements of our two main theorems.
In
Section

2.1
, we introduce the two-periodic Aztec diamond, in
Section

2.2
we introduce the height function and in
Section

2.3
, we introduce Temperley’s bijection. We state our main results in
Section

2.4
.
Section

3
contains a detailed proof sketch and includes two auxiliary results on the model which we believe may be of particular interest.

The remainder of the paper is devoted to the proofs.
Very roughly, after an initial combinatorial section (
Section

4
) containing a proof of Temperley’s bijection for the Aztec diamond and some auxiliary results, the paper is divided into an essentially probabilistic part (
Section

5
through to
Section

9
) which contains the meat of the paper and an integrable part (
Section

10
to
Section

14
) whose proofs are mostly standard asymptotic analysis or modifications of previous arguments for the model (the exception here is the symmetry in
3.1
).

Section

5
records the basic tools that we need for working with the model. Many results in this section have integrable proofs for this section are postponed until
Section

10
and beyond. In
Section

6
we prove
1
.
In
Section

7
,
Section

8
, and
Section

9
build to the proof of
2
.
Section

7
establishes global path control and one of our auxiliary results (
3.2
),
Section

8
establishes the local path regularity and our other auxiliary result (
3.3
), and
Section

6
completes the proof of
2.2
.

2.
Definitions and main results

2.1.
The two-periodic Aztec diamond

First, define vertex and face lattices in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
:

\displaystyle{\mathtt{\tilde{V}}}=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:(i+j)\text{ mod }2=1\},

\displaystyle{\mathtt{\tilde{F}}}=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:(i+j)\text{ mod }2=0\}.

We let the edge set
{\mathtt{\tilde{D}}}
consist of all pairs
\{v,w\}\subset{\mathtt{\tilde{V}}}
such that
v-w\in\{\pm e_{1},\pm e_{2}\}
where
e_{1}=(1,1)
and
e_{2}=(-1,1)
. This gives an infinite bipartite graph
{\mathtt{\tilde{G}}}:=({\mathtt{\tilde{V}}},{\mathtt{\tilde{D}}})
, consisting of white and black vertices

\displaystyle{\mathtt{White}}

\displaystyle=\{(i,j)\in{\mathtt{\tilde{V}}}:i\text{ mod }2=1,j\text{ mod }2=0\},

\displaystyle{\mathtt{Black}}

\displaystyle=\{(i,j)\in{\mathtt{\tilde{V}}}:i\text{ mod }2=0,j\text{ mod }2=1\}.

The set
\tilde{F}
gives the faces of
\tilde{G}
, where a face is labelled by its centre. Next, fix a parameter
a\in(0,1)
. If
(i,j)\in{\mathtt{\tilde{F}}}
,
i,j\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
, and
i+j\in 4\mathbb{Z}+2
, then we label
(i,j)
as an
a-face
. If
i,j\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
, and
i+j\in 4\mathbb{Z}
, then we label
(i,j)
as an
b-face
. If an edge is incident to an
a
-face it has weight
a
and if it is incident to a
b
-face it has weight
1
. We call the resulting weighted graph the
two-periodic lattice
with parameter
a
. This weighting splits the white and black vertex sets into two pieces, which in total partitions
{\mathtt{\tilde{V}}}
into four pieces, which we label as
{\mathtt{\tilde{N}}},{\mathtt{\tilde{S}}},{\mathtt{\tilde{E}}},{\mathtt{\tilde{W}}}
and are defined as follows:

•

First, every vertex
v\in{\mathtt{\tilde{V}}}
is adjacent to exactly one
a
face
f(v)
.

•

If
v
is south of
f(v)
(i.e.
v-f(v)=(0,-1)
), let
v\in{\mathtt{\tilde{S}}}
. Similarly, if
v
is north, east, or west of
f(v)
(i.e.
v-f(v)=(0,1),(1,0),(-1,0)
, respectively), let
v\in{\mathtt{\tilde{N}}},{\mathtt{\tilde{E}}},{\mathtt{\tilde{W}}}
.

It is straightforward to check that
{\mathtt{White}}={\mathtt{\tilde{N}}}\cup{\mathtt{\tilde{S}}}
and
{\mathtt{Black}}={\mathtt{\tilde{E}}}\cup{\mathtt{\tilde{W}}}
.

For
n\in 4\mathbb{N}
, the
two-periodic Aztec diamond

{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
of size
n
is the subgraph of the two-periodic lattice induced by the vertices in the box
[-n,n]^{2}
. We write
{\mathtt{V}}={\mathtt{\tilde{V}}}\cap[-n,n]^{2}
and similarly define
{\mathtt{N}},{\mathtt{S}},{\mathtt{E}},{\mathtt{W}}
for vertex classes in the two-periodic Aztec diamond. We let
{\mathtt{D}}
be the edge set of
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
. The bounded faces of the Aztec diamond are given by
{\mathtt{F}}={\mathtt{\tilde{F}}}\cap(-n,n)^{2}
. It will also be useful to consider a larger set which includes boundary faces:

{\mathtt{\bar{F}}}=\{f\in{\mathtt{\tilde{F}}}:\text{ there exists }v\in{\mathtt{V}}\text{ with }\|v-f\|_{2}=1\}.

The size parameter
n
is suppressed from the above notation; its value will always be clear from context.
See
Figure

4
for an illustration of the two-periodic Aztec diamond.

Figure 4
.
The two-periodic Aztec diamond of size
4
. The edges around each face labeled
a
have weight
a
while the edges around each face labeled
b
have weight
1
.

A
dimer configuration

D
on
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
is a perfect matching of
{\mathtt{V}}
, i.e. a subset of
{\mathtt{D}}
so that each vertex is incident to exactly one edge. Edges in a dimer configuration are called
dimers
. The law of the
two-periodic Aztec diamond with parameter
a
is the probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
on dimer configurations of
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
, where each configuration
D
has probability proportional to the product of the edge weights.
Note that if we had started with any weighting that assigned an edge weight
a
to
a
-faces and
b
to
b
-faces, then as long as
a\neq b
, the resulting measure would be equivalent to
\mathbb{P}_{a^{\prime},n}
for some
a^{\prime}\in(0,1)
, possibly after a reflection.

Figure 5
.
The relationship between dimers and dominoes is shown on the figure on the left while the figure on the right shows the height function for this dimer covering.

2.2.
Height functions

Dimer coverings of bipartite graphs have an alternative
height function
representation, an idea dating back to Thurston
[
Thu90
]
. For a dimer configuration
D
on
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
, we define its height function
h=h(D):\mathtt{\bar{F}}\to\mathbb{Z}
with the following rules:

•

h(-n,-n)=-n
.

•

As we traverse counterclockwise on the faces around each white vertex, we increase the height by
1
provided that there is no dimer covering the shared edge between faces. If there is a dimer covering the shared edge between faces, we decrease the height by 3.

•

As we traverse clockwise on the faces around each black vertex, we increase the height by
1
provided that there is no dimer covering the shared edge between faces. If there is a dimer covering the shared edge between faces, we decrease the height by 3.

The height function on the boundary
\partial\mathtt{F}:=\mathtt{\bar{F}}\setminus\mathtt{F}
is deterministic since there are no dimers outside of
\mathtt{V}\cap[-n,n]^{2}
. We can easily check that:

•

If
f\in\partial\mathtt{F}
is of the form
(i,-n)
or
(i,-n-1)
, then
h(f)=i
.

•

If
f\in\partial\mathtt{F}
is of the form
(-n,i)
or
(-n-1,i)
, then
h(f)=i
.

•

If
f\in\partial\mathtt{F}
is of the form
(i,n)
or
(i,n+1)
, then
h(f)=-i
.

•

If
f\in\partial\mathtt{F}
is of the form
(n,i)
or
(n+1,i)
, then
h(f)=-i
.

In other words, as we traverse the boundary
\partial\mathtt{F}
clockwise, we increase along the west boundary, decrease along the north boundary, increase along the east boundary, and then decrease along the south boundary. See
Figure

5
for an example. In the two-periodic Aztec diamond, the limit shape of the height function has maximal gradient in the frozen regions, and is flat throughout the smooth region. The rough region transitions between these two extremes.

The above correspondence gives a bijection between the set of dimer configurations on
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
and the set of allowable height functions. These are functions
h:\mathtt{\bar{F}}\to\mathbb{Z}
with the above boundary values, and such that around every vertex
w\in\mathtt{White}
heights increase by
1
as we move around counterclockwise except for between a single pair of faces, and for every vertex
w\in\mathtt{Black}
heights increase by
1
as we move around clockwise except for between a single pair of faces.

2.3.
Temperley’s bijection

Dimer coverings of
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
can also be understood through a version of Temperley’s bijection. The classical Temperley’s bijections maps dimer coverings of bipartite graphs to spanning trees of an associated graph in the case when the boundary of the graph has constant height, see
[
Tem74
,
KPW00
]
. In the Aztec diamond, the height changes as we move around the boundary, and so if we construct a Temperley-type correspondence in this setting we get a bijection with certain classes of spanning forests. There are four versions of this bijection, which correspond to the north, south, east, and west vertices, but in the present paper we will only use the north and south bijections.

Figure 6
.
The left figure shows a dimer tiling, the right shows both the north and south Temperleyan forests. Backbone paths in the north and south forests are drawn in red and blue lines, respectively, as is our convention for all figures.

To define the south bijection, define a graph
{\mathtt{G}}(\mathtt{S})
whose vertex set is a small extension of
\mathtt{S}
. Define

(1)

\mathtt{\bar{S}}=\mathtt{\tilde{S}}\cap([-n-1,n+1]\times[-n,n]).

We let
\mathtt{\bar{S}}
be the vertex set of
{\mathtt{G}}(\mathtt{S})
, and let
\{v,w\}\subset\mathtt{\bar{S}}
be an edge whenever
v-w\in\{\pm 2e_{1},\pm 2e_{2}\}
. Now, given a dimer configuration
D
on
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
, we can associate to it a subgraph
F_{D}\subset{\mathtt{G}}(\mathtt{S})
with the same vertex set
\mathtt{\bar{S}}
, by including an edge
\{v,v+e\}
in
F_{D}
for
e\in\{\pm 2e_{1},\pm 2e_{2}\}
whenever
\{v,v+e/2\}
is a dimer in
D
. We call
F_{D}
the
south Temperleyan forest
for
D
. See
Figure

6
for an example of this correspondence.

The map
D\mapsto F_{D}
is one-to-one, and we can identify its image as the set of
dimer-compatible forests
of
{\mathtt{G}}(\mathtt{S})
, which we now define. First, divide the outer boundary of
{\mathtt{G}}(\mathtt{S})
into four pieces:

(2)

\begin{split}\partial_{E}{\mathtt{S}}&=(\{n+1\}\times[-n,n])\cap{\mathtt{\bar{S}}},\quad\partial_{W}{\mathtt{S}}=(\{-n-1\}\times[-n,n])\cap{\mathtt{\bar{S}}},\\
\partial_{S}{\mathtt{S}}&=([-n,n]\times\{-n\})\cap{\mathtt{\bar{S}}},\quad\partial_{N}{\mathtt{S}}=([-n,n]\times\{n\})\cap{\mathtt{\bar{S}}}.\end{split}

We call
\partial^{{\mathtt{x}}}{\mathtt{S}}:=\partial_{E}{\mathtt{S}}\cup\partial_{W}{\mathtt{S}}={\mathtt{\bar{S}}}\setminus{\mathtt{S}}
the
sink boundary
for
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
, we call
\partial^{{\mathtt{o}}}{\mathtt{S}}=\partial_{N}{\mathtt{S}}\cup\partial_{S}{\mathtt{S}}
the
source boundary
for
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
, and let
\partial{\mathtt{S}}
be the union of all four parts of the boundary.

Definition 2.1
.

A subgraph
F\subset{\mathtt{G}}(\mathtt{S})
is a
dimer-compatible forest (DCF)
if:

(i)

F
is a spanning forest, i.e. its vertex set is
{\mathtt{\bar{S}}}
.

(ii)

Every component of
F
contains exactly one element of
\partial^{{\mathtt{x}}}\mathtt{S}
.

(iii)

For every vertex
v\in\partial^{{\mathtt{o}}}\mathtt{S}
, let
\theta_{F}(v)
denote the unique vertex in
\partial^{{\mathtt{x}}}\mathtt{S}
in the same component as
v
. Then
\theta_{F}(v)
is contained in the cross-shaped region
\{v+(n,\pm n):n\in 2\mathbb{Z}\}
. This region intersects
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}
at exactly two points, once in
\partial_{E}{\mathtt{S}}
and once in
\partial_{W}{\mathtt{S}}
.

We will typically think of all edges in each component of a DCF
F
as being oriented towards the sink boundary
\partial^{{\mathtt{x}}}{\mathtt{S}}
. It is easy to include this orientation in the map
D\mapsto F_{D}
by including the oriented edge
(v,v+e)
in
F_{D}
whenever
\{v,v+e/2\}
is a dimer in
D
.

The
north Temperleyan forest
is essentially the planar dual of the south forest, see
Section

4
for the precise setup. Alternately, it can be obtained through the same south forest procedure after rotation by
\pi
.

We can reconstruct of a dimer configuration the height function directly from any of its Temperleyan forests. Very roughly, the height of a vertex
v
in a Temperleyan forest
F
is equal to the height of the sink vertex
s_{v}
in the component containing
v
plus four times the winding number of the path from
v
to
s_{v}
. Here the height of a vertex is the average height of its incident faces. Because of this correspondence, we can think different components of a Temperleyan forest as wanting to live at distinct heights, and Definition
2.1
(iii) as a height-matching condition.

The two-periodic Aztec diamond plays particularly well with Temperley’s bijection. Indeed, if we let
\mathbb{Q}_{{\mathtt{S}},a,n}
be the pushforward of the measure
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
under the Temperley map
D\mapsto F_{D}
, then the probability of a DCF
F
under
\mathbb{Q}_{{\mathtt{S}},a,n}
is simply proportional to
a^{2}
times the number of directed edges which point northwest or northeast. In other words, we bias towards edges which point south. Given this structure, we can imagining sampling such a forest
F\sim\mathbb{Q}_{{\mathtt{S}},a,n}
in two steps:

•

First, sample all of the paths connecting the source and sink boundaries
\partial^{{\mathtt{o}}}\mathtt{S}
and
\partial^{{\mathtt{x}}}\mathtt{S}
. We call these the
backbone paths
.

•

Given the first step, the corridors between backbone paths are conditionally independent, and each region can individually be sampled using Wilson’s algorithm for sampling spanning trees on a weighted graph. In particular, the law of the path from any vertex until it hits a backbone path is just that of the loop-erasure of a south-biased random walk.

Because of this two-step process, most interesting information in the forest
F_{D}
is carried by the backbone paths, which exhibit long-range correlations. The biased loop-erased random walks which fill in the corridors have correlations which decay exponentially fast. Moreover, it is unlikely that the loop-erasure of a biased random walk has a high winding number, and so components of
F_{D}
are essentially flat.

Figure
1
is a simulation of the north and south Temperleyan forests for a two-periodic Aztec diamond with
n=1200,a=0.5
. South backbone paths are given in blue, and north in red; this is our convention through the paper. A selection of paths off of the backbone moving through the smooth region are also included. These paths show a strong north/south drift prior to hitting the backbone, as we expect from the bias.

2.4.
Main results

Figure 7
.
A simulation of the south forest in the two-periodic Aztec diamond with
n=800
and
a=0.7
, with only a portion of the Aztec diamond around the smooth region pictured. The
\mathcal{S}
-backbone paths are pictured in blue, and the (dual)
\mathcal{N}
-backbone paths are red.

Recall from the overview that our first main result concerns convergence of the height functions to the Airy surface after rescaling the underlying lattice. This perspective avoids having to directly work with paths.

To state
1
precisely, let
\mathcal{H}_{n}
denote the height function for the two-periodic Aztec diamond of size
n
, let
\zeta_{n}
be the set of all faces with
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq n^{3/4}
, and set

(3)

H^{\prime}_{n}=\frac{1}{|\zeta_{n}|}\sum_{f\in\zeta_{n}}\mathcal{H}_{n}(f).

Define the
central height

H_{n}
to be the unique integer in the interval
(H^{\prime}_{n}-1/2,H_{n}^{\prime}+1/2]
. The central height
H_{n}
is the height at which the flat smooth region sits on average. In the definition, it is not important the exact set we are averaging over to define this height, as long as it grows to infinity with
n
and is contained in the smooth region. The random variable
H_{n}
itself is converging to a discrete Gaussian random variable, see
[
BN25
]
and Remark
5.1
.

Next, we define scaling parameters. Following
[
BCJ22
]
, define

(4)

c=\frac{a}{(1+a^{2})},

and fix

\xi_{c}=-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1-2c}
. The point
(\xi_{c},\xi_{c})
is the unique point in the third quadrant on the rough-smooth limit curve. Set

(5)

c_{0}=\frac{(1-2c)^{\frac{2}{3}}}{(2c(1+c))^{\frac{1}{3}}},\hskip 14.22636pt\lambda_{1}=\frac{\sqrt{1-2c}}{2c_{0}}\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\lambda_{2}=\frac{(1-2c)^{\frac{3}{2}}}{2cc_{0}^{2}}.

For this theorem, some of the limiting objects come from the
full-plane smooth phase

\mathbb{P}_{a}
. There are multiple ways of defining this object (see Section
4.3
), but for now, we simply note that the measure
\mathbb{P}_{a}
on dimer configurations of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is the limit of the measures
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
as we take
n\to\infty
, i.e. the local limit of the two-periodic Aztec diamond in the center of the smooth region. As there are no boundary conditions in the full-plane smooth phase, the height function is naturally defined only up to constant shift. We choose this shift in the most natural way, so that the height function is ergodic and mean
0
on
a
- and
b
-faces.

Theorem 2.1
.

For
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, define
[t,x]_{n}
to be the nearest
a
-face in
{\mathtt{\tilde{F}}}
to the point

n(\xi_{c},\xi_{c})+t\cdot 2^{1/3}\lambda_{2}n^{2/3}(1,-1)+(x-t^{2})\cdot 2^{5/3}\lambda_{1}n^{1/3}(1,0).

Then for any finite set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have the convergence

(6)

\begin{split}(H_{n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}[u]_{n}:u\in S)\implies(4\mathcal{A}(u)+X_{u}:u\in S),\end{split}

where
\mathcal{A}
is the Airy surface, and the random variables
X_{u},u\in S
are i.i.d. and independent of
\mathcal{A}
. Each of these random variables individually is distributed as the height of (any)
a
-face in the full-plane smooth phase with parameter
a
.

We can write
X_{u}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}4Y
, where
Y
is the winding number of the loop-erasure of a biased nearest-neighbour random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, which takes up and right steps with probability
a^{2}/(2+2a^{2})
and left and down steps with probability
1/(2+2a^{2})
.

Remark 2.2
.

The law of each of the random variables
X_{u}
should be a discrete Gaussian, but we have not pursued this direction here. More remarkably, we believe that the law of these random variables is the same as the limit law of the central height. This latter fact seems to be a special symmetry of the two-periodic Aztec diamond, and we do not expect it to hold for smooth phases and rough-smooth boundaries in other tiling models.

Remark 2.3
.

2.1
has the following two variants, which lend more credence to the idea that the limit of the height function is an Airy line ensemble sitting in a smooth background.

First, with the same setup as in that theorem, we have that

\begin{split}(H_{n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}(v-(1,1)+[u]_{n}):u\in S,v\in{\mathtt{\tilde{F}}})\implies(4\mathcal{A}(u)+\mathcal{H}^{u}(v):u\in S,v\in{\mathtt{\tilde{F}}}),\end{split}

where now each function
\mathcal{H}^{u}:{\mathtt{\tilde{F}}}\to\mathbb{Z}
is an independent copy of the height function for the full-plane smooth phase. The convergence above is in the product topology.

Moreover, if we consider a sequence of sets
\phi_{n}=[-r_{n},r_{n}]^{2}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}
for some
r_{n}=o(n^{1/24})
, then letting
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
, we can couple
D|_{[u]_{n}+v},u\in S,v\in\phi_{n}
with the full-plane smooth phase so that the two objects are equal with probability
1-o(1)
. Because of the presence of the Airy lines in an
n^{2/3}\times n^{1/3}
scaling window, the optimal result here would allow for any sets
\phi_{n}
that are lower order than this scale.

The above results, together with ergodicity of the full-plane smooth phase, says that if we take small local averages of the height function then we converge to the Airy surface. Setting up some notation, with
\phi_{n}
as above, define the mollified height function

\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}([u]_{n}):=\frac{1}{|\phi_{n}|}\sum_{v\in\phi_{n}}\mathcal{H}_{n}(v+[u]_{n}).

Then

\begin{split}\tfrac{1}{4}(H_{n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}([u]_{n}):u\in S)\implies(\mathcal{A}(u):u\in S).\end{split}

Both
2.1
and the various strengthenings above are shown in Section
6
, where they all follow as corollaries of the more technical
6.8
.

Next, we state a precise version of
2
on backbone path convergence. While this theorem is stated for south backbone paths only, the proof will show the result for the south and north paths together. Setting some notation, for a two-periodic Aztec diamond of size
n
, we let
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-},\mathcal{S}_{2}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-},\dots,\mathcal{S}_{n/2}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{n/2}^{-}
denote the
n/2
backbone paths in the associated south and north Temperleyan forest that start on the lower boundary, with the ordering from left to right. Then there is a unique split point
I=I_{n}\in\{0,\dots,n/2\}
where for
i\leq I_{n}
, the path
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
ends on the west boundary, and for
i>I_{n}
, the path
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
ends on the east boundary. To help parse the definitions, it may be illustrative to consider the third panel in
Figure

5
where
n=12
, and we have six south forest paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
and six north forest paths
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{-}
. In that example,
I_{n}=3
. We similarly use notation
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{+},\mathcal{N}_{i}^{+}
for south and north backbone paths starting on the north boundary.

By considering simulations (e.g. see
Figure

3
) we should expect that the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}:=\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}}^{-}
delineates the rough-smooth boundary.
Now, the Airy paths are one-dimensional in the limit whereas the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
are plane curves which may backtrack (e.g. see
Figure

7
). Therefore in order to consider the convergence of the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
to the Airy line ensemble, we should consider the smallest and largest points where these curves intersect each vertical line. First, let
R_{\theta}
denote counterclockwise rotation by
\theta
, and define the set

{\mathtt{Meso}}_{n}=n(\xi_{c},\xi_{c})+R_{-\pi/4}([-n^{3/4},n^{3/4}]\times[-n^{1/2},n^{1/2}]),

which is a mesoscopic enlargement of the
O(n^{2/3}\times n^{1/3})
scaling window at the rough-smooth boundary.

Define paths
\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,+}(t)

\displaystyle=\sup\{x\in\mathbb{R}:[t,x]_{n}\in\mathcal{S}_{I+1-i}\cap{\mathtt{Meso}}_{n}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,-}(t)

\displaystyle=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:[t,x]_{n}\in\mathcal{S}_{I+1-i}\cap{\mathtt{Meso}}_{n}\}.

Theorem 2.2
.

First,

\mathbb{P}(H_{n}=4I_{n}-n-1)=1-o(1)

as
n\to\infty
. In other words, with high probability the path split point is equal to the central height (after shifting the indexing appropriately).

Next, the two line ensembles
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
converge to the parabolic Airy line ensemble
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
in the sense of finite dimensional distributions (FDDs). That is, for any finite set
T\subset\mathbb{R}
we have

(7)

(\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}|_{T}:i\in\mathbb{N})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{A}_{i}|_{T}:i\in\mathbb{N}).

Moreover, with all assumptions as in
2.1
and Remark
2.3
, we have the joint FDD convergence

(8)

\begin{split}(H_{n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}[u]_{n},\;\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}(t))&\implies(-4\mathcal{A}(u),\;\mathcal{A}_{i}(t)).\end{split}

Here the joint convergence is over
u\in S,t\in T
, and
i\in\mathbb{N}
, where
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{2},T\subset\mathbb{R}
are arbitrary finite or countable sets.

Remark 2.4
.

It is not particularly important that we intersect with the enlarged boundary region
{\mathtt{Meso}}_{n}
in the definitions
\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}
. This could be removed with some extra asymptotic formula analysis, but we have not pursued this here for brevity. The exact scaling of
n^{3/4}\times n^{1/2}
in the definition of
{\mathtt{Meso}}_{n}
is essentially arbitrary, and only important in that it is larger than the
n^{2/3}\times n^{1/3}
Airy scale.

Remark 2.5
.

A version of
2.2
also holds using the north Temperleyan backbone paths, see
9.1
. Indeed, an important part of the proof is showing that the naturally paired north and south backbone paths stay close together as they move through the rough-smooth boundary region. Our exact estimate shows that the vertical distance between these paths is
O(n^{1/4}\log^{2}n)
, whereas the fluctuation scale is
O(n^{1/3})
, see Corollary
8.2
for details. We expect that the true distance between these pairs of paths is of much lower order, i.e. logarithmic (or at least polylogarithmic) in
n
.

We can also give a slight strengthening
2.2
by replacing the paths
\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}(t)
with versions of the form

t\mapsto\sup_{|u|\leq n^{-1/3-\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}(t+u),\qquad t\mapsto\inf_{|u|\leq n^{-1/3-\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}(t+u),

where
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, see
9.1
. Note that if
n^{-1/3}
is replaced by
n^{-2/3}
, then this result becomes essentially immediate from
2.2
. Of course, the optimal result in this direction would be to show that the paths
\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}
actually converge uniformly to the Airy line ensemble. We state this as a conjecture.

Conjecture 2.3
.

The two line ensembles
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
converge in the uniform-on-compact topology to the Airy line ensemble
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

Almost all approaches towards understanding uniform convergence to the Airy line ensemble go through studying a Gibbs property in the prelimit that converges to the Brownian Gibbs property in the limit. This approach was pioneered by Corwin and Hammond
[
CH13
]
in the setting of non-intersecting Brownian motions, and extended and refined to prove uniform tightness for a range of one-dimensional non-intersecting or softly non-intersecting path models, e.g. see
[
CH16
,
DNV23
,
Wu23
,
BCD23
,
DFF+21
,
CD18
,
ACH24
,
Ser23
,
DS25a
,
DS25b
]
. The Brownian Gibbs property for the Airy line ensemble also leads to a complete probabilistic characterization of the object, recently proven in
[
AH26
]
(see also
[
DM21
]
for an earlier result), which presents the tantalizing possibility of proving convergence to the Airy line ensemble without an appeal to formulas.

In all of these papers, an underlying Gibbs property for non-intersecting paths is crucial for propagating pointwise path control to control on entire sets. In our setting, a natural Gibbs property for the backbone paths should exist. Indeed, because the backbone paths are non-crossing paths coming out of a weighted spanning forest, we expect that there is a tractable Gibbs property related to the study of non-crossing loop erased random walks, coming out of Fomin’s theory
[
Fom01
]
. However, even given this property, we expect that proving uniform convergence of the paths
\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}
would require a new suite of ideas, since in the present setting our paths are two-dimensional, whereas all of the prior Gibbsian line ensemble literature deals with one-dimensional paths.

3.
Proof sketch and some auxiliary results

As the paper is quite long, we have opted to give a detailed proof sketch in order to help guide the reader. Along the way, we will present two auxiliary results about the behaviour of the backbone paths in the two-periodic Aztec diamond.

Two challenges and three tools

As discussed previously, there are two central challenges in studying the rough-smooth boundary that are not present at the rough-frozen boundary in tiling models, or in other models where Airy line ensemble convergence has been previously established.

I.

The presence of a noise (in our case, from the smooth phase) which blurs the exact boundary.

II.

The natural collections of paths defining the boundary are undirected.

There are many models where we expect Airy line ensemble convergence in the presence of effects I and II, see
Section

1.2
for more details. Of these, the two-periodic Aztec diamond is particularly tractable, and we have many refined integrable and probabilistic tools available for understanding the rough-smooth boundary. In the present paper, the tools we use fit into three main categories:

1.

Height function estimates.
While we cannot understand absolutely everything about the height function directly from formulas, we can essentially compute the expectation of the height function throughout the Aztec diamond with precise asymptotics. We can also gain some control over how the height function concentrates and how it decorrelates. Finally, a more refined analysis at the rough-smooth boundary reveals that certain averages of the height function converge the Airy surface; this was established in
[
BCJ18
]
.

2.

Smooth phase couplings.
The full-plane smooth phase measure
\mathbb{P}_{a}
with parameter
a\in(0,1)
is equivalent (through Temperley’s bijection) to a biased spanning forest on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, where edges pointing in the
(1,1)
and
(1,-1)
directions have weight
a^{2}
and edges pointing in the
(-1,-1)
and
(-1,1)
have weight
1
. In
[
BCJ22
]
, the authors used the determinantal structure of the two-periodic Aztec diamond measure
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
to prove the following smooth phase coupling result: for a box of with side length
L
at the rough-smooth boundary, the total variation distance between
\mathbb{P}_{a}
and
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
is
O(n^{-1/3}L^{8})
. We use both this estimate, and a couple of variants which give smooth phase couplings on larger sets as we move further away from the rough-smooth boundary and into the central smooth region. The smooth phase itself can be understood through Wilson’s algorithm for sampling spanning trees.

3.

Spanning tree estimates and interlacing.
If we condition on the backbone paths in one of the four Temperleyan forests, then we can fill in the remainder using Wilson’s algorithm. In particular, individual paths in any of the Temperleyan forests simply follow a biased loop-erased random walk path until they hit the backbone, and so remain in a certain parabola with high probability. This observation starts to give a lot of structure to the spanning forests when we combine it with the fact that the south and north forests are dual to each other. For example, in the corridor between two adjacent south backbone paths, the trajectory of the dual north backbone path cannot position itself in a way that blocks a south loop-erased random walk path from reaching either of the south backbone paths, see
Figure

8
.

Figure 8
.
A low-likelihood configuration of backbone paths. The dual north backbone path
\mathcal{N}_{i-1}^{-}
is positioned relative to the south backbone paths
\mathcal{S}_{i-1}^{-},\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
in such a way that makes it very unlikely that a south-drifted random walk from any of the three blue sites will hit
\mathcal{S}_{i-1}^{-}
or
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
before hitting
\mathcal{N}_{i-1}^{-}
. Parabolic random walk range constraints are included for two of the blue sites.

The first two categories above are highly reliant on the specific integrable structure of the two-periodic Aztec diamond, whereas the third is based on more a general probabilistic/combinatorial framework and holds for two-periodic tilings on more general domains. Beyond establishing the tools above, we do not use the exact formulas for the two-periodic Aztec diamond and all arguments are probabilistic or geometric in nature.

Most of the tools described above have standard proofs and will not surprise readers familiar with the model. The only result we highlight here is the following symmetry, recorded again in the body of the text as
5.1
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
a\in(0,1]
, and let
(i,j)\in{\mathtt{F}}
be such that
i,j\in 2\mathbb{Z}
. Then

\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(i,j)=-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(-i,j)=-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(i,-j)=\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(-i,-j)

In particular,
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(2i,0)=\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(0,2i)=0
for all
-n/2\leq i\leq n/2
.

This lemma is an obvious symmetry when
a=1
, but beyond this case we do not have a combinatorial explanation. We executed a by-hand verification of the equality
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(0,0)=0
for small values of
n
, but could not discern a pattern. Note that the expected height at the origin is not an integer in the full-phase smooth phase, so even this symmetry is not coming from a simpler property of the infinite-volume model.

The overview of proof of
2.1

2.1
is significantly easier than
2.2
, and in the context of the present paper can be viewed as a warm-up to the latter statement. Indeed, by analyzing convergence to the Airy surface rather than finding paths that converge to the Airy line ensemble, we circumvent effect II described above. Moreover, the framework of random surface convergence in
2.1
can easily incorporate the idea of an Airy line ensemble sitting in a background noise, whereas convergence of line ensembles struggles to capture this directly.

2.1
follows uses the main theorem of
[
BCJ18
]
, together with a few of the auxiliary tools described in
1
and
2
above and some probabilistic understanding of the smooth phase. Translated into our language, the main theorem of
[
BCJ18
]
essentially says the following; a stronger and more precise version is recorded as
6.1
.

Consider a finite collection of times
T\subset\mathbb{R}
and a finite collection of disjoint intervals
(x_{i},y_{i}),i\in I
. Then for a particular choice of mollifying sequence
\phi_{n}
(see Remark
2.3
), we have:

(9)

\begin{split}(\mathcal{H}^{\phi_{n}}_{n}[t,x_{i}]_{n}&-\mathcal{H}^{\phi_{n}}_{n}[t,y_{i}]_{n}\;;\;(i,t)\in I\times T)\\
&\implies(4\mathcal{A}(t,y_{i})-4\mathcal{A}(t,x_{i})\;;\;(i,t)\in I\times T).\end{split}

There are two key differences between (
9
) and (
6
): the presence of the mollifying sequence in (
9
) versus the presence of the i.i.d. random variables
X_{u}
in (
6
), and the fact that (
9
) looks at differences of the height function, rather than the height function itself. Note that in
6.1
, the scaling of the plane with
n
differs slightly, but this is a minor effect and easily resolved.

In
[
BCJ18
]
, statement (
9
) is proven by asymptotic analysis of exact formulas. In that context, the mollifying sequence
\phi_{n}
is crucial since it renders the limiting object determinantal. The asymptotic analysis yields a limit result for height differences rather than for the heights themselves because the underlying exact formulas are for the dimer configuration, which is a discrete derivative of the height function. To remove the mollifiers
\phi_{n}
, we will use that locally around each point
[t,x]_{n}
, the dimer configuration is well-coupled to a smooth phase (tool
2
). Fast decay of correlations in the smooth phase guarantees that the mollifying sequence must have had the effect of simply averaging out this smooth phase. It also guarantees that any noise we see from this smooth phase at a fixed point is independent of the limiting heights. By making this argument rigorous, we can replace (
9
) with a statement of the following form:

(10)

\begin{split}(\mathcal{H}^{\phi_{n}}_{n}[t,x_{i}]_{n}&-\mathcal{H}^{\phi_{n}}_{n}[t,y_{i}]_{n}\;;\;(i,t)\in I\times T)\\
&\implies(4\mathcal{A}(t,y_{i})+Y_{i,t}-4\mathcal{A}(t,x_{i})-X_{i,t}\;;\;(i,t)\in I\times T),\end{split}

where the
X_{i,t},Y_{i,t}
are i.i.d. random variables, independent of
\mathcal{A}
, each equal in law to the height at any
a
-face in the full-plane smooth phase. Next we remove the height differences. First, it is not difficult to reduce this problem to the case when
|I|=1
, and it will be easier to work in the mollified framework (
9
). If we take the point
y_{1}
in (
10
) to be very large then with high probability the right-hand side (
9
) is simply equal to
\{\mathcal{A}(x_{1},t):t\in T)
. This suggests that it will be useful to allow
y_{1}
to tend to
\infty
with
n
. In
6.7
, we extend the analysis of
[
BCJ18
]
to prove a version of their main theorem that allow us to do this with
y_{1}=\log^{3}n
. Given this, it is enough to show that for fixed
t
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}[t,\log^{3}n]_{n}=H_{n})=1.

Heuristically, this follows from the fact that the two-periodic Aztec diamond should agree with the full-plane smooth phase on the entire smooth region if we are sufficiently far away from the boundary. In practice, we prove this by patching together a mesoscopic smooth phase coupling near the boundary of the smooth phase that deals with regions at least distance
n^{1/3}\log^{2}n
away from the rough-smooth boundary, and a macroscopic smooth phase coupling that kicks in the moment we are distance
n^{6/7}
away from the boundary.

The overview of the proof of
2.2

2.2
is significantly more involved, and the bulk the paper is devoted to its proof. Before discussing the proof, recall that to recover the height at a vertex
v
along a path in one of the Temperleyan forests, we simply take the height at the source or sink vertex and add four times the winding number of the path connecting
v
to its source or sink. The upshot of this is that if paths do not typically wind (as is suggested by the simulations) then the height function should stay relatively stable as we move along a backbone path. Moreover, the different components of the Temperleyan forests can be viewed as essentially flat, decorated with local height fluctuations which come from small windings of the tree.

Focusing now on the south Temperleyan forest, recall also that off the backbone paths, Wilson’s algorithm guarantees that the forest paths are simply south-biased loop-erased random walks. In summary, a simplistic picture of the components in the south forest near the rough-smooth boundary suggests that each component sits almost entirely
above
its corresponding backbone path (up to a logarithmic error) and has height function equal to a deterministic shift of the path index (again, up to a logarithmic error). To reconcile this picture with
2.1
, we expect that there is a sequence of south backbone paths indexed by
J_{n},J_{n}-1,J_{n}-2,\dots
with
J_{n}:=\lceil H_{n}/4+n/2\rceil
which follow the Airy lines. See
Figure

9
for a cartoon, and
Figure

7
for a simulation.

Figure 9
.
A cartoon of the rough-smooth boundary. The top three prelimiting Airy paths
\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}-i}^{-}
are pictured, with associated typical corridor heights. Two types of forest paths that can change heights within a corridor are highlighted: red whorls, which change height by winding, and green paths, which connect up to upper backbone path despite the southward random walk drift.

Controlling global path behaviour

To establish this picture rigorously, we need to gain a strong level of control over the backbone paths. The principal difficulties arise from the fact that we are working with undirected, rather than directed, paths.

Before we can address the rough-smooth boundary, we must first rule out pathological
global
behaviour.
For purposes of illustration, consider the single path
\mathcal{S}_{J}^{-}=\mathcal{S}^{n}_{J_{n}}
. In order to later conduct an analysis of the rough-smooth boundary, we will need to rule out the following four global phenomena:

(i)

\mathcal{S}_{J}^{-}
travels macroscopically into the rough or frozen regions.

(ii)

\mathcal{S}_{J}^{-}
travels macroscopically into the smooth region
{\mathtt{Smooth}}
, rather than staying confined to a small window around the rough-smooth boundary
{\mathtt{RS}}
.

(iii)

\mathcal{S}_{J}^{-}
stays in a small window around the rough-smooth boundary, but winds around the smooth region or backtracks, rather than simply following the rough-smooth boundary from south to west.

(iv)

\mathcal{S}_{J}^{-}
traces the rough-smooth boundary from south to west, but it is not the last path to do so. In other words,
J_{n}\neq I_{n}
, where
I_{n}
is the split point defined prior to
2.2
.

See
Figure

10(a)
for a picture of the regions above.
The tools
1
-
3
introduced above are sufficient to rule out each of these scenarios.

(a)

Regions in the two-periodic Aztec diamond.

(b)

A potentially bad trajectory for a path
\mathcal{S}_{i}
starting in the region
{\mathtt{RS}}
.

We can address scenario (i) with expectation and concentration estimates on the height function (tool
1
). Such estimates give that
H_{n}=O(1)
, and outside of a small, simply connected window around the central smooth region and the rough-smooth boundary
{\mathtt{RS}}
, the height deviates by more than
O(1)
away from
0
. Since the path
\mathcal{S}_{J}
starts out at a height of
H_{n}+O(1)
by definition, it cannot exit this window without winding. On the other hand, a path that starts on the
boundary of a simply connected domain must have winding number zero upon first exit. This prevents
\mathcal{S}_{J}
from exiting this window, ruling out (i).

We address (ii) and (iii) together. Consider a single path
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
, which starts on the part of the south boundary in the set
{\mathtt{RS}}
, and ends on the west boundary. First, if we can couple the whole smooth region with the full-plane smooth phase, then this path behaves as a south-drifted random walk whenever it is in the region
{\mathtt{Smooth}}
. At a global scale, this means that it can only move directly south while in this region. This greatly restricts the kind of trajectories that the path can have, see
Figure

10(b)
for an example. From here, the rough idea for proving (ii) and (iii) is that if the path
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
does not simply follow the rough-smooth boundary curve from south to west, then we can find vertices in the north forest from which a loop-erased random walk would have to behave atypically, i.e. breaking the parabolic bounds in
Figure

8
.

These points are easy to find if only a single path
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
has a bad trajectory. Two such points are shown in red in
Figure

10(b)
. If multiple paths have bad trajectories, then we can create nested
onions
of bad paths.

7.3
systematically extends the path-blocking idea in
Figure

8
to give us quantitative control on onions in terms of their heights, widths, and number of layers. Together with the height estimates used in establishing (i), and the drift estimates in the smooth region, this gives sufficient control to eliminate (ii) and (iii).

Once (i)–(iii) are ruled out for
\mathcal{S}_{J}^{-}
and its neighboring south backbone
paths, we can conclude that there exists a topmost path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
tracing the
rough-smooth boundary from south to west within the region
\operatorname{RS}
.
By standard random walk estimates, all south forest paths that start above

\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
within the smooth region
\operatorname{Smooth}
must eventually coalesce with

\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
. By the smooth phase coupling, these south forest paths start at height
H_{n}
on average. Therefore the starting height
of
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
must coincide with the central height
H_{n}
, unless
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
itself
winds around the smooth region—a possibility ruled out by (i)–(iii).
This forces
I_{n}=J_{n}
, resolving (iv).

While a full implementation of the argument eliminating (i)–(iv) is possible using the techniques developed in this paper, doing so would require height function estimates on the whole Aztec diamond and smooth phase couplings that hold throughout the entire smooth region. To avoid some of these technicalities, we will instead prove a weaker statement which still suffices for our study of the rough-smooth boundary. The weak version replaces each region with a kind of topological skeleton and focuses on whether paths cross barriers imposed by this skeleton. A precise version is as follows.

First, let
\alpha>0
be the unique point such that
(-\alpha,0)
and
(0,-\alpha)
lie on the rough-smooth boundary curve. Let
\xi_{0}:[-\alpha,\alpha]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the rough-smooth boundary curve in the third quadrant, parametrized so that for all
a\in[-\alpha,\alpha]
, the point
\xi_{0}(a)
is on the line
\{{\bf v}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mathbf{v}\cdot e_{2}=a\}
(here recall that
e_{1}=(1,1),e_{2}=(-1,1)
). Define the coordinate change
\beta_{n}:[-\alpha n,\alpha n]\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\beta_{n}(t,x)=n\xi_{0}(t/n)+e_{1}x.

In words,
\beta_{n}(t,x)
gives a point at location
t
along the rough-smooth boundary curve, at distance
x
away from the boundary. Note that the spatial scaling in
\beta_{n}
does not change with
n
; the scaling
n\xi_{0}(\cdot/n)
simply puts the limit curve back in unscaled coordinates. Next, define regions

\displaystyle{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}

\displaystyle:=\beta_{n}([-2n^{3/4},2n^{3/4}]\times[-n^{1/2}\log^{3/2}n,2n^{1/3}\log^{2}n]),

\displaystyle{\mathtt{PRS}}_{n}^{*}

\displaystyle:=\beta_{n}([-2n^{3/4},2n^{3/4}]\times[-n^{1/2}\log^{2}n,n^{3/4}]),

We view the set
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
as an enlarged version of the region of the rough-smooth boundary where we will eventually see Airy fluctuations,
and
{\mathtt{PRS}}_{n}^{*}
as a padded version. The exact exponents in the definitions of
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*},{\mathtt{PRS}}_{n}^{*}
are not so important. What is important is that the
O(n^{2/3}\times n^{1/3})
-region where we are studying fluctuations is lower order compared to
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}
, whose height is lower order compared to
{\mathtt{PRS}}_{n}
. Next, let
{\mathtt{X}}=\{{\bf v}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:v_{1}=\pm v_{2}\}
. See
Figure

11
for a sketch of these regions, and an illustration of the upcoming theorem.

Theorem 3.2
.

Recall that
I_{n}
is the label of the last south backbone path
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
to move from the south boundary to the west boundary. Then with probability
1-o(1)
as
n\to\infty
, the following events hold:

1.

(Boundary paths avoid the skeleton and are funnelled through the boundary) The paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
with
I_{n}-n^{1/4}\leq i\leq I_{n}
do not intersect the region
({\mathtt{X}}\cup{\mathtt{PRS}}_{n}^{*})\setminus{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
.

2.

(
\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}}^{-}
is the top path) The path
\mathcal{N}_{I_{n}}^{-}
does not intersect the region
{\mathtt{PRS}}_{n}^{*}
. By interlacing, neither do any of the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i},i>I_{n}
.

3.

(Height matching)
H_{n}=4I_{n}-n-1
.

Figure 11
.
Theorem
3.2
gives global control of backbone paths. One of the main upshots of the theorem is that the path
\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}}^{-}
avoids the region
({\mathtt{X}}\cup{\mathtt{PRS}}_{n}^{*})\setminus{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
, whereas the path
\mathcal{N}_{I_{n}}^{-}
avoids the region
{\mathtt{PRS}}_{n}^{*}
entirely before meeting a path started on the north side of the Aztec diamond. This establishes that
\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}}^{-}
is the top path moving through the rough-smooth boundary

Point
1
in the above theorem should be viewed as a weak resolution to problems (i)-(iii) above, point
2
a resolution to problem (iv), and point
3
an upshot. Note that the precise powers of
n
in the definition of
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}
are not particularly important, beyond the fact that
n^{5/6}\gg n^{2/3}
and
n^{1/2}\gg n^{1/3}
.
3.2
is restated with more detail in the sequel as
7.1
.

Path regularity at the rough-smooth boundary.

Given the weak global control in
3.2
, we have a starting point for understanding the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}
at the rough-smooth boundary. If we can additionally prove a kind of weak path regularity locally, then we can deduce
2.2
. To this end, we will prove the following estimate as part of
8.1
.

Theorem 3.3
.

The following estimates holds simultaneously with probability
1-o(1)
as
n\to\infty
. Let
k\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor\}
, and suppose that
v=(v_{1},v_{2}),w=(w_{1},w_{2})
are two vertices in
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}\cap\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
where
v
occurs
before

w
on the path
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
. Then

(11)

w_{1}-v_{1}<(2k+1)n^{1/4}\log^{2}n.

3.3
is a kind of
backtrack estimate
, which gives quantitative control on how far the undirected paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}
are from being directed as they move through the rough-smooth boundary. The estimate shows that for paths of index
I-k
with
k=O(1)
, the maximum size of a backtrack in the rough-smooth boundary region is of order
O(n^{1/4}\log^{2}n)
, which is lower order than the Airy scaling.
3.3
is not expected to be sharp, and indeed, the true backtrack bound above should be polylogarithmic.

In order to illustrate how
3.3
feeds into
2.2
, let us temporarily assume we can show (
11
) with a stronger bound of size
\alpha_{n}=o(n^{1/24})
.

Fix
x,t\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
v=[x,t]_{n}
. Let
\pi
be the south forest path starting incident to
v
, and ending at a sink vertex
s
. Let
w
be the first vertex on a backbone path
\mathcal{S}_{i}
visited by the path
\pi
, and let
w^{\prime}
be the first vertex visited by
\pi
on
\mathcal{S}_{i}
whose height
\mathcal{H}_{n}(w^{\prime})
equals the sink height
\mathcal{H}_{n}(s)
. The height
\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)
can be written as a sum of the sink height
\mathcal{H}_{n}(s)=\mathcal{H}_{n}(w^{\prime})
, and the relative height
\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)-\mathcal{H}_{n}(w^{\prime})
. Now, we can use a local smooth phase coupling around
v
(tool
2
) to say that in a box
B
of width
4\alpha_{n}
around
v
, the dimer configuration is close to the smooth phase (since
n^{-1/3}\cdot\alpha_{n}^{8}=o(1)
).
Because any south forest path entering a smooth region experiences a strong downward drift, this makes it highly unlikely that
\mathcal{S}_{i}
will enter a slightly smaller box
B^{\prime}
of width
2\alpha_{n}
centered around
v
. In particular, we expect
w^{\prime}
to lie outside of
B^{\prime}
, and the height difference
\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)-\mathcal{H}_{n}(w^{\prime})
should be given by the height of
v
in the smooth phase coupling on
B^{\prime}
, plus a contribution if the path
\pi
ever winds around the box
B^{\prime}
. By our backtrack estimate, winding cannot happen around boxes of width larger than
\alpha_{n}
, and so
\pi
cannot wind around the box
B^{\prime}
. This allows us to identify
\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)-\mathcal{H}_{n}(w^{\prime})
as the smooth term
X
in
2.1
. The sink height
\mathcal{H}_{n}(w^{\prime})
(equivalently, the path index
i
after a deterministic shift) then gives the Airy term. Applying this idea on a fine mesh of vertices
v
and using some of the weak global path control from
3.2
then translates
2.1
into
2.2
.

Now, the above argument fails if we directly apply the estimate (
11
), since
n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
is much larger than the
o(n^{1/24})
-scale on which we have a high-probability smooth phase coupling. In order to work around this issue, rather than executing the above argument with a single smooth phase coupling, we construct a ring of boxes of width
\log^{3}n
which will act as a path barrier. By tool
2
above, the smooth phase coupling succeeds each box with probability at least
1-O(n^{-1/3}\log^{24}n)
, and so by a union bound, we can create a ring containing
n^{1/3-\varepsilon}
overlapping boxes, and couple each box to a different copy of the smooth phase so that all couplings simultaneously succeed with probability at least
1-n^{-\varepsilon/2}
. Now, the box width we have chosen is large enough so that drift estimates on smooth phase imply that the probability of a backbone path passing through any box in this ring is smaller than any polynomial in
n
. Therefore with high probability, we will only have a winding contribution to
\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)-\mathcal{H}_{n}(w^{\prime})
if one of the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}
winds around the entire ring, which has width at least
n^{1/3-\varepsilon}
. For
\varepsilon<1/12
, we have
n^{1/3-\varepsilon}\gg n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
, and so
3.3
can guarantee that this does not happen.

The overview of the proof of
3.3
.

We start with the bound on the top path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
. From
3.2
,
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
moves through the region
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
without entering the region
{\mathtt{X}}\cup{\mathtt{PRS}}_{n}^{*}
, whereas
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
never enters the region
{\mathtt{PRS}}_{n}^{*}
. In other words, the picture will resemble
Figure

11
. Now consider a north forest path
\pi
starting just above
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
in the region
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
. By Wilson’s algorithm, the path
\pi
is simply the loop-erasure of biased random walk, stopped when it hits
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
. In particular, it will move as a biased loop-erased walk until it exits
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
, and so with high probability, it will stay in a parabola opening up vertically while it sits in this region. Since the region
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
has height
O(n^{1/2}\log^{3/2}n)
, the parabola has maximum width given by the square root of this:
O(n^{1/4}\log^{3/4}n)
. Now, if the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}
has backtracks which are much larger than this scale, it will trap some of these loop-erased walk paths, contradicting Wilson’s algorithm.

Now, we can apply a similar idea to bound the backtrack size of the top north backbone path
\mathcal{N}_{I-1}^{-}
. Unlike the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
, as this path moves through
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
it in a corridor between
\mathcal{S}_{I-1}^{-}
and
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
, rather than in a region which is unbounded above. However, the backtrack estimate on
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
guarantees that the upper boundary of this corridor is still regular, and so a version of the argument still applies. We can continue moving through the lines
\mathcal{S}_{I-1}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{I-2}^{-},\dots
inductively, sacrificing only an additive factor with each successive line index.

4.
Trees, forests, and Temperley’s bijection

In this section, we describe and prove Temperley’s bijection for the Aztec diamond, and describe its relationships to the height function and dimer representations. Along the way, we will prove some basic combinatorial and probabilistic properties of these correspondences (e.g. symmetries, interlacing, Wilson’s algorithm).

Before discussing Temperley’s bijection, we record three basic symmetries of the two-periodic Aztec diamond. Here we let
f_{*}\mu
denote the pushforward of a measure
\mu
under a map
f
. The following lemma is immediate.

Lemma 4.1
.

The measure
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
is invariant under the following three operations sending the south vertex set
{\mathtt{S}}
to
{\mathtt{E}},{\mathtt{W}}
, and
{\mathtt{N}}
, respectively.

•

\mathbb{P}_{a,n}=(\rho_{\mathtt{E}})_{*}\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
where
\rho_{\mathtt{E}}(x,y)=(-y,-x)
. The map
\rho_{\mathtt{E}}
sends
{\mathtt{S}}\mapsto{\mathtt{E}},{\mathtt{E}}\mapsto{\mathtt{S}}
and
{\mathtt{N}}\mapsto{\mathtt{W}},{\mathtt{W}}\mapsto{\mathtt{N}}
.

•

\mathbb{P}_{a,n}=(\rho_{\mathtt{W}})_{*}\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
where
\rho_{\mathtt{W}}(x,y)=(y,x)
. The map
\rho_{\mathtt{W}}
sends
{\mathtt{S}}\mapsto{\mathtt{W}},{\mathtt{W}}\mapsto{\mathtt{S}}
and
{\mathtt{N}}\mapsto{\mathtt{E}},{\mathtt{E}}\mapsto{\mathtt{N}}
.

•

\mathbb{P}_{a,n}=(\rho_{\mathtt{N}})_{*}\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
where
\rho_{\mathtt{W}}(x,y)=(-x,-y)
. The map
\rho_{\mathtt{N}}
sends
{\mathtt{S}}\mapsto{\mathtt{N}},{\mathtt{N}}\mapsto{\mathtt{S}}
and
{\mathtt{W}}\mapsto{\mathtt{E}},{\mathtt{E}}\mapsto{\mathtt{W}}
.

4.1.
A generalization of Temperley’s bijection

In this subsection, we prove the generalized Temperley’s bijection introduced in Section
2.3
, along with a handful of other combinatorial properties of this correspondence. First, recall from Section
2.3
the definition of the extended south-vertex set
{\mathtt{\bar{S}}}
, the south-vertex graph
{\mathtt{G(S)}}
, the source and sink boundaries
\partial^{{\mathtt{o}}}{\mathtt{S}},\partial^{{\mathtt{x}}}{\mathtt{S}}
, and the notion of a dimer-compatible forest
F
on
{\mathtt{G(S)}}
.

We give similar definitions for the north vertices using the rotation
\rho_{\mathtt{N}}
is
4.1
. Define
{\mathtt{\bar{N}}}=\rho_{\mathtt{N}}{\mathtt{\bar{S}}},{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{N}})=\rho_{\mathtt{N}}({\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}}))
and say that a spanning forest
F
of
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{N}})
is dimer-compatible if
F=\rho_{\mathtt{N}}F^{\prime}
for a dimer-compatible spanning forest
F^{\prime}
on
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
. We define
\partial_{N}{\mathtt{N}},\partial_{S}{\mathtt{N}},\partial_{E}{\mathtt{N}},\partial_{W}{\mathtt{N}}
exactly as in (
2
) but with
{\mathtt{N}}
in place of
{\mathtt{S}}
, and set
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{N}}=\partial_{E}{\mathtt{N}}\cup\partial_{W}{\mathtt{N}},\partial^{\mathtt{o}}{\mathtt{N}}=\partial_{N}{\mathtt{N}}\cup\partial_{S}{\mathtt{N}}
, and
\partial N=\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{N}}\cup\partial^{\mathtt{o}}{\mathtt{N}}
.

Note that we could give similar definitions for the east and west vertices using the other two symmetries in
4.1
, and develop Temperley’s bijection in these settings as well. We will not need these bijections in the present paper.

The wiring structure for paths starting on the source boundary in a DCF is quite rigid. We specify this structure in the next lemma. We state it for DCFs on
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
, but a symmetric version holds for
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{N}})
by applying the map
\rho_{\mathtt{N}}
. See
Figure

12
for an example of
4.2
.

Figure 12
.
A dimer configuration on an Aztec diamond with
n=4
, together with the associated pair of north (red) and south (blue) Temperleyan forests. Boundary vertex labelling is as in
4.2
,
4.3
. Here
I=2
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Label the vertices of
\partial{\mathtt{S}}
as follows, moving clockwise in a cycle around the boundary from the northeast corner
(n+1,n)
:

v_{1}^{E},\dots,v_{n/2+1}^{E},v^{S}_{n/2},\dots,v^{S}_{1},v^{W}_{1},\dots,v^{W}_{n/2},v^{N}_{n/2},\dots,v^{N}_{1}.

Now, consider a DCF
F
on
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
, and recall the map
\theta_{F}:\partial^{\mathtt{o}}{\mathtt{S}}\to\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}
from Definition
2.1
(iii). Then

1.

There is a split point
I=I(F)\in\{0,\dots,n/2\}
such that for
j\leq I
we have
\theta_{F}(v_{j}^{S})\in\partial_{W}{\mathtt{S}}
, and for
j>I
we have
\theta_{F}(v_{j}^{S})\in\partial_{E}{\mathtt{S}}
.

2.

Given
I=I(F)
, the map
\theta_{F}
satisfies:

\theta_{F}(v_{j}^{S})=\begin{cases}v_{j}^{E},\quad&j>I\\
v_{j}^{W},\quad&j\leq I\end{cases},\qquad\theta_{F}(v_{j}^{N})=\begin{cases}v_{j+1}^{E},\quad&j<I\\
v_{j}^{W},\quad&j>I\end{cases}

If
I>0
, then
\theta_{F}(v^{N}_{I})\in\{v^{W}_{I},v^{E}_{I+1}\}
.

Proof.

Point
1
holds by planarity. For point
2
, observe that we can translate Definition
2.1
(iii) into the condition that for
j\in\{1,\dots,n/2\}
we have

\theta_{F}(v^{S}_{j})\in\{v_{j}^{E},v^{W}_{j}\},\qquad\theta_{F}(v^{N}_{j})\in\{v_{j+1}^{E},v^{W}_{j}\}.

The choice of
\theta_{F}
is determined as in point
2
by this constraint, the definition of
I
, and planarity.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

The labelling on the boundary vertices match the height function, up to an affine shift. For example, for every south vertex
v=v^{S}_{i}\in\partial_{S}{\mathtt{S}}
, let
f_{v}=v-(1,0)
. The face
f_{v}
is always in
\partial{\mathtt{F}}
so it has a deterministic height, which satisfies

h(f_{v_{i}^{*}})/4+n/4=i-1.

Similar affine relationships hold for the vertices on the other three boundaries. Note the similarity between the relationship here and the relation between
I_{n}
and
H_{n}
in
2.2
. The takeaway in all settings is that
path index equals height-over-
4
(up to shifting the labels). However, here the relationship is straightforward and deterministic, whereas in
2.2
the relationship only holds with high probability, and is a consequence of our global control on path behaviour.

Now, the graphs
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}}),{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{N}})
are duals of each other, in the following sense. Let
E_{\mathtt{S}}
and
E_{\mathtt{N}}
denote the edge sets of
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
and
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{N}})
, represented as line segments in the plane. Then the map taking every edge in
E_{\mathtt{S}}
and rotating by
\pi/2
about its midpoint is a bijection from
E_{\mathtt{S}}
to
E_{\mathtt{N}}
.
In particular, every edge in
E_{\mathtt{S}}
crosses exactly one edge in
E_{\mathtt{N}}
, and we refer to such edges as duals.
This dual relationship extends to dimer-compatible forests.

Lemma 4.3
.

Consider a DCF
F
on
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
. Define the dual
\check{F}
of
F
to be the subgraph of
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{N}})
with vertex set
{\mathtt{\bar{N}}}
and edge set consisting of all edges in
E_{\mathtt{N}}
that do not cross an edge in
F
. Then
\check{F}
is a DCF on
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{N}})
.

Moreover, if the split point
I
is given as in
4.2
, we have the following description for
\theta_{\check{F}}
. Extend the labelling of
\partial{\mathtt{S}}
in
4.2
to all vertices of
\partial{\mathtt{N}}\cup\partial{\mathtt{S}}
as follows, so that moving clockwise in a cycle around the boundary from the northeast corner
(n+1,n)
we have:

\displaystyle v_{1}^{E},w_{1}^{E},v_{2}^{E},\dots,v^{E}_{n/2},w_{n/2}^{E},v_{n/2+1}^{E},w^{S}_{n/2},v^{S}_{n/2},\dots,w^{S}_{1},v^{S}_{1},

\displaystyle w^{W}_{1},v^{W}_{1},w^{W}_{2},\dots,w^{W}_{n/2},v^{W}_{n/2},w^{W}_{n/2+1},v^{N}_{n/2},w^{N}_{n/2},\dots,v^{N}_{1},w^{N}_{1}.

Then

\theta_{\check{F}}(w_{j}^{S})=\begin{cases}w_{j}^{E},\quad&j>I\\
w_{j+1}^{W},\quad&j<I\end{cases},\qquad\theta_{\check{F}}(w_{j}^{N})=\begin{cases}w_{j}^{E},\quad&j\leq I\\
w_{j}^{W},\quad&j>I\end{cases}

If
I>0
, then
\theta_{\check{F}}(w^{S}_{I})=w_{I}^{E}
if
\theta_{F}(v^{N}_{I})=v^{W}_{I}
and
\theta_{\check{F}}(w^{S}_{I})=w_{I+1}^{W}
if
\theta_{F}(v^{N}_{I})=v^{E}_{I+1}
.

Figure

12
may help with parsing the labelling in
4.3
.

Proof.

First observe that
\check{F}
cannot contain a cycle. Indeed, any cycle in
\check{F}
would surround a component in
F
. On the other hand, every component in
F
contains a vertex from
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}
, and there are no cycles in
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{N}})
which surround a vertex in
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}
. Hence
\check{F}
is a forest, giving Definition
2.1
(i). Next,

|E_{\mathtt{N}}|=|{\mathtt{E}}|+|{\mathtt{W}}|=|{\mathtt{S}}|+|{\mathtt{N}}|.

This follows since the edge set
E_{\mathtt{N}}
is in bijection with the east/west vertex set
{\mathtt{E}}\cup{\mathtt{W}}
under the map taking an edge
e
to its midpoint. Now,
|E(F)|=|{\mathtt{S}}|
, as
F
is a forest with
|\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}|
-many components and vertex set
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}\cup{\mathtt{S}}
. Therefore
|E(\check{F})|=|{\mathtt{N}}|
since
|E(F)|+|E(\check{F})|=|E_{\mathtt{N}}|
by duality. Since
\check{F}
is a forest with vertex set
{\mathtt{N}}\cup\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{N}}
, it contains
|\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{N}}|
-many components.

Suppose a component
C
of
\check{F}
contains no vertices in
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{N}}
. Then there must be two components
D,D^{\prime}
(possibly the same) of
F
that separate
C
from the east and west boundaries
\partial_{E}{\mathtt{N}},\partial_{W}{\mathtt{N}}
, respectively. Component
D
must contain vertices in
\partial_{E}{\mathtt{S}},\partial_{S}{\mathtt{S}},\partial_{N}{\mathtt{S}}
, whereas component
D^{\prime}
must contain vertices in
\partial_{W}{\mathtt{S}},\partial_{S}{\mathtt{S}},\partial_{N}{\mathtt{S}}
. Part 2 of
4.2
shows that it is not possible for both these components to exist. Together with the component count for
\check{F}
, this yields Part (ii) of
2.1
.

Given Parts (i) and (ii) of
2.1
, we can define the map
\theta_{\check{F}}
mapping vertices in
\partial^{\mathtt{o}}{\mathtt{N}}\to\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{N}}
. The planar dual relationship between
F,\check{F}
allows us to determine
\theta_{\check{F}}
from
\theta_{F}
(given by
4.2
) as in the statement of the lemma. This also guarantees Part (iii) of
2.1
.
∎

Next, we state Temperley’s correspondence for the Aztec diamond. Given a dimer configuration
D
on
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
, for any choice of
{\mathtt{C}}\in\{{\mathtt{N}},{\mathtt{S}},{\mathtt{E}},{\mathtt{W}}\}
we can construct a directed subgraph
F_{{\mathtt{C}}}(D)
of
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{C}})
as follows. For every vertex
w\in{\mathtt{C}}
, there is a unique dimer in
D
of the form
\{w,w+e\}
where
e\in\{\pm e_{1},\pm e_{2}\}
. Add the directed edge
(w,w+2e)
to the graph
F_{{\mathtt{C}}}(D)
.

Proposition 4.4
.

For every even value of
n\in 4\mathbb{N}
and
{\mathtt{C}}\in\{{\mathtt{N}},{\mathtt{S}}\}
, the map
D\mapsto F_{{\mathtt{C}}}(D)
is a bijection between dimer configurations on
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
and dimer-compatible forests on
{\mathtt{G}}(\mathtt{C})
, directed towards the sink boundary
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}\mathtt{C}
.

The inverse map
D_{\mathtt{C}}
of
F_{{\mathtt{C}}}
is given as follows. Consider a DCF
F
on
{\mathtt{G}}(\mathtt{C})
, and construct the dual forest
\check{F}
on
{\mathtt{G}}(\mathtt{\check{C}})
, where
{\mathtt{\check{S}}}={\mathtt{N}},{\mathtt{\check{N}}}={\mathtt{S}}
by including an edge
e
in
\check{F}
if and only if
e
does not cross an edge in
F
. Then
\check{F}
is a DCF in
{\mathtt{G}}(\mathtt{\check{C}})
. Orient
F
towards
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{C}}
and
\check{F}
towards
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{\check{C}}}
, and let

D_{\mathtt{C}}(F)=\{(v,v+e):(v,v+2e)\text{ is a directed edge in }F\text{ or }\check{F}\}.

Finally, for a dimer configuration
D
, the dual forest
\check{F}_{{\mathtt{C}}}(D)
to
F_{{\mathtt{C}}}(D)
is
F_{{\mathtt{\check{C}}}}(D)
.

To prove
4.4
, we will need to track height changes along forest paths. This is taken care of by the next two results.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
D
be a dimer configuration on
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
and let
P=(s_{0},s_{1},\dots,s_{k})
be a directed path of vertices in the graph
F_{{\mathtt{S}}}(D)
. For
\varepsilon\in\{(0,\pm 1),(\pm 1,0)\}
, define the face-path

F^{\varepsilon}(P)=(f_{0}^{\varepsilon},\dots,f_{k}^{\varepsilon})=(s_{0}+\varepsilon,\dots,s_{k}+\varepsilon).

Then the height function
h
for
D
evolves along
F^{v}(P)
as follows. Here for two points
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we write
[a,b]
for the line segment from
a
to
b
, oriented in that way.

•

For
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, if
[s_{i},s_{i+1}]
does not cross
[f_{i-1}^{\varepsilon},f^{\varepsilon}_{i}]
, then
h(f_{i-1}^{\varepsilon})=h(f_{i}^{\varepsilon})
.

•

For
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, if
[s_{i},s_{i+1}]
crosses
[f_{i-1}^{\varepsilon},f^{\varepsilon}_{i}]
from right-to-left, then
h(f_{i-1}^{\varepsilon})+4=h(f_{i}^{\varepsilon})
.

•

For
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, if
[s_{i},s_{i+1}]
crosses
[f_{i-1}^{\varepsilon},f^{\varepsilon}_{i}]
from left-to-right, then
h(f_{i-1}^{\varepsilon})-4=h(f_{i}^{\varepsilon})
.

The same height evolution holds for
F_{{\mathtt{N}}}(D)
.

4.5
is readily verified from the definition of the height function.

Corollary 4.6
.

For a vertex
v
in a dimer configuration
D
, let its height
h(v)
be the average height of the four incident faces. Then in the south-forest setup of
4.5
, we have that

h(s_{k-1})-h(s_{0})=L-R,

where
L
is the number of left turns made by the path
P
, and
R
is the number of right turns. The same holds for a north-forest path.
