Remark 4.2
.

We remark that
h(v)
as defined above uniquely specifies the dimer incident to
v
. For instance, if
v\in\mathtt{S}
and fix the
a
-face
v+(0,1)
to have height
1
. Then we have that
h(v)
is
\frac{5}{2}
,
\frac{3}{2}
,
\frac{1}{2}
, and
-\frac{1}{2}
for the dimers
(v,v+e_{1})
,
(v,v-e_{2})
,
(v,v-e_{1})
, and
(v,v+e_{2})
respectively. Moreover, it is not hard to see that given the average height function for all vertices of the Aztec diamond, we can recover the tiling.

Proof of
4.6
.

It suffices to prove the result when
k=2
, as the general case follows by concatenation. The
k=2
case follows from
4.5
and averaging out the height changes amongst all of the incident faces around two consecutive vertices on a path.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.4
.

We consider the case
{\mathtt{C}}={\mathtt{S}}
, as the
{\mathtt{C}}={\mathtt{N}}
case is symmetric.
First we show that
F_{{\mathtt{S}}}(D)
contains no (undirected) cycles.
Suppose that
C=(s_{1},\dots,s_{k}=s_{1})
is a cycle in
F_{{\mathtt{S}}}(D)
. First, we necessarily have that
C
is also a directed cycle, since otherwise some vertex in
C
would have two outgoing edges. As a directed cycle,
C
either has four more left turns then right turns, or vice versa. By tracking height changes around
C
using Corollary
4.6
, this gives two different values for the height of
s_{1}
, which is not possible.

Therefore
F_{{\mathtt{S}}}(D)
is a directed acyclic graph. By construction, the only sinks in
F_{{\mathtt{S}}}(D)
are the elements of
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}
, so every component of
F_{{\mathtt{S}}}(D)
must contain at least one element of
F_{{\mathtt{S}}}(D)
. On the other hand, the number of edges in
F_{{\mathtt{S}}}(D)
in
|{\mathtt{S}}|
, whereas the number of vertices is
|{\mathtt{S}}|+|\partial{\mathtt{S}}|
and so
F_{{\mathtt{S}}}(D)
has exactly
n+1=|\partial{\mathtt{S}}|
components. Therefore each component has exactly one element of
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}
, as desired.

Now consider a vertex
v=(i,-n)\in\partial_{S}{\mathtt{S}}
, and let
P=(v=v_{1},\dots,v_{k}=u)
, where
u\in\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}
. First assume
u\in\partial_{W}{\mathtt{S}}
. Define an augmented path
P^{\prime}=(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{k+1})
by letting
v_{0}=v_{1}+2e_{2}=(i-2,-n-2)
and
v_{k+1}=v_{k}-2e_{2}
, adding in the dimers
\{v_{0},v_{0}+e_{2}\},\{v_{k},v_{k}+e_{2}\}
to the dimer configuration
D
and extending the height to include all incident faces to
v_{0},v_{k+1}
. The new path
P^{\prime}
does not wind around its initial vertex, and its initial and final edges have the same direction. Therefore it has an equal number of left and right turns, and so
h(v_{0})=h(v_{k+1})
by Corollary
4.6
. On the other hand, we can compute
h(v_{0})
using the deterministic boundary values for the heights near
v=(i,n)
. This gives
h(v_{0})=i+1/2
. By similar reasoning, letting
u=(-n-1,j)
, we can compute that
h(u)=j-1/2
. Therefore
j=i+1
and so
u=(-n-1,i+1)
. By a symmetric argument, we can show that if
u\in\partial_{E}{\mathtt{S}}
, then
u=(n+1,-i+1)
. This gives Definition
2.1
(iii) when
v\in\partial_{S}{\mathtt{S}}
. A similar argument gives the same condition when
v\in\partial_{N}{\mathtt{S}}
, and so
F_{\mathtt{S}}(D)
is a dimer-compatible forest.

Next, we show that given a DCF
F
on
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
, the set
D_{{\mathtt{S}}}(F)
is a dimer configuration. First, the dual forest
\check{F}
is a DCF on
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{N}})
by
4.3
, and so orienting both
F,\check{F}
towards the sink boundaries is well-defined. Next,
D_{\mathtt{S}}(F)
is a subgraph of
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
where every white (i.e.
{\mathtt{N}}/{\mathtt{S}}
)-vertex has degree one, since in the oriented forest
F,\check{F}
, all vertices have out-degree one. Moreover, every black vertex also has degree one, since a black (i.e.
{\mathtt{E}}/{\mathtt{W}}
)-vertex is incident to an edge in
D_{\mathtt{S}}(F)
if that planar edge contained the black vertex at its midpoint in either
F
or
\check{F}
. Exactly one of these possibilities holds for each black vertex by the dual relationship between
F
and
\check{F}
. Hence
D_{\mathtt{S}}(F)
is a dimer configuration.

It is straightforward to check that the maps
D_{\mathtt{S}},F_{\mathtt{S}}
are inverses. Finally, for a dimer configuration
D
, for every edge
\{v,v+2e\}
in the edge set
E_{\mathtt{S}}
for
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
, it is not possible that both
\{v,v+2e\}
and its dual are contained in both
F_{\mathtt{S}}(D)
and
F_{\mathtt{N}}(D)
since the black vertex
v+e
has degree
1
in
D
. Hence the planar embeddings of
F_{\mathtt{S}}(D)
and
F_{\mathtt{N}}(D)
do not cross, and so they must be duals.
∎

4.2.
Temperley’s bijection and the two-periodic Aztec diamond

As discussed in the introduction, the bijection in
4.4
is important in our setting because the pushforward under
F_{\mathtt{C}}
of the two-periodic Aztec diamond measure
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
is tractable. First, observe that
D_{\mathtt{C}},F_{\mathtt{C}}
interact well with path weights, as described in the next lemma. This is immediate from the definitions.

Lemma 4.7
.

Fix
{\mathtt{C}}\in\{{\mathtt{N}},{\mathtt{S}}\}
, and let
{\mathtt{D}}_{\mathtt{C}}\subset{\mathtt{D}}
be the set of all edges incident to a
{\mathtt{C}}
-vertex in the Aztec diamond graph
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
. Let
\operatorname{Dir}_{\mathtt{C}}
be the set of directed edges in
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{C}})
and let
\phi_{{\mathtt{C}}}:{\mathtt{D}}_{\mathtt{C}}\to\operatorname{Dir}_{\mathtt{C}}
be the map sending the edge
\{v,v+e\}
to the directed edge
(v,v+2e)
, where
v\in{\mathtt{C}}
.

Let
w:{\mathtt{D}}\to[0,\infty)
be any weight function which equals
1
on
{\mathtt{D}}\setminus{\mathtt{D}}_{\mathtt{C}}
, and let
\tilde{w}=w\circ\phi^{-1}
be the corresponding weight function on
\operatorname{Dir}_{\mathtt{C}}
. Extend
w,\tilde{w}
to subgraphs of
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
and directed subgraphs of
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{C}})
, by letting the weight of a subgraph be the product of its edge weights. Then with
F_{\mathtt{C}},D_{\mathtt{C}}
as in
4.4
, for any dimer configuration
D
on
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
or any DCF
F
on
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{C}})
we have

\tilde{w}\circ F_{\mathtt{C}}(D)=w(D)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad w\circ D_{\mathtt{C}}(F)=\tilde{w}(F).

4.7
is relevant because we can produce the dimer measure
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
by starting with a collection of edge weights that are
1
on
{\mathtt{D}}\setminus{\mathtt{D_{C}}}
, for any choice of
{\mathtt{C}}
, as per the next lemma.

Lemma 4.8

(Proposition 8.2,
[
BCJ22
]
)
.

Fix
a\in(0,1)
, and consider the following collections of edge weights on
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
.

•

{\mathtt{N}}
-weights: edges of the form
\{v,v-(\pm 1,1)\}
, where
v\in{\mathtt{N}}
have weight
a^{2}
. All other edges have weight
1
.

•

{\mathtt{S}}
-weights: edges of the form
\{v,v+(\pm 1,1)\}
, where
v\in{\mathtt{S}}
have weight
a^{2}
. All other edges have weight
1
.

Then for either of the two edge weight choices above, the measure on dimer configurations on
{\mathtt{A}}_{n}
given by choosing configurations proportional to their edge weight is the two-periodic Aztec diamond measure
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
.

Figure 13
.
The north and south edge weights on
\mathtt{A}_{n}
as considered in
4.8
, along with the original two-periodic weights and the face weights.

The idea of
4.8
is that a dimer model is determined by its face weights, i.e. the alternating product of edge weights around each face. Two dimer models with the same face weights are called
gauge equivalent
, and produce the same measure, see Section 8 in
[
BCJ22
]
for details. Combining Lemmas
4.8
and
4.7
, we have the following result. For this corollary, we call a directed edge of the form
(v,v+2e)
in
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{C}})
a northeast (NE) edge if
e=(1,1)
, a northwest (NW) edge if
e=(1,-1)
, a southeast (SE) edge if
e=(-1,1)
and a southwest (SW) edge if
e=(-1,-1)
.

Corollary 4.9
.

For
{\mathtt{C}}\in\{{\mathtt{N}},{\mathtt{S}}\}
and
a\in(0,1)
, let
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathtt{C,a,n}}
denote the pushforward measure of
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
under the map
F_{\mathtt{C}}
. Then
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathtt{C,a,n}}
is a measure on dimer-compatible forests in
{\mathtt{G(C)}}
, where for a forest
F
(oriented towards
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{C}}
,
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathtt{C,a,n}}(F)
is proportional to the weight of
F
, where edge weights on
\operatorname{Dir}_{\mathtt{C}}
are given as follows:

•

{\mathtt{C}}={\mathtt{N}}
: NE/NW edges have weight
1
and SE/SW edges weight
a^{2}
.

•

{\mathtt{C}}={\mathtt{S}}
: SE/SW edges have weight
1
and NE/NW edges weight
a^{2}
.

Figure 14
.
The random walk weights from
4.9
.

In other words, the pushforward of
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
under the map
F_{\mathtt{C}}
is a measure on DCFs, where the weights bias towards edges that point in cardinal direction
{\mathtt{C}}
.

Proof.

By
4.8
, the measure
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
assigns weights to dimer configurations based on the
{\mathtt{C}}
-weights in that lemma, which equal
1
off of
{\mathtt{D_{C}}}
. Therefore by
4.7
and the bijectivity of
F_{\mathtt{C}}
(
4.4
), the pushforward
\mathbb{Q}_{{\mathtt{C}},a,n}
assigns measure to DCFs with weights described above.
∎

One of the main advantages of moving from the dimer measure
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
to the DCF measure
\mathbb{Q}_{{\mathtt{C}},a,n}
is the connection to loop-erased random walks via Wilson’s algorithm
[
Wil96
]
.

First, consider a finite weighted directed graph
G=(V,E)
, with a distinguished root vertex
v
, and suppose that for every
w\in V
there is a directed path from
w
to
v
. We can sample a spanning tree
T
on
G
, oriented towards
v
, with probability proportional to the weight of
T
, as follows:

1.

Choose any enumeration
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},\dots v_{k}
of
V\setminus\{v\}
, and set
T_{0}=\{v\}
.

2.

At step
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, run a random walk
X_{i}
with step probabilities proportional to edge weights in
G
, until it hits a vertex in
T_{i-1}
. Set
T_{i}=T_{i-1}\cup\operatorname{LE}(P_{i})
, where
\operatorname{LE}(X_{i})
is the loop-erasure of
X_{i}
(i.e. we remove loops of
X_{i}
in the order in which they appear to get a simple path).

3.

Set
T=T_{k}
.

Wilson’s algorithm has the following obvious extension. Suppose that
H
is a subforest of
G
with vertex set
W\subset V
. Then if we run Wilson’s algorithm on
V\backslash W
with the whole subgraph
H
in place of
T_{0}
, the final graph
F=T_{k}
has law
\mu
given as follows:

•

\mu
assigns positive measure to spanning forests
F
of
G
such that
F|_{W}=H
, and every component of
F
is oriented towards an element of
W
. Here we write
F|_{W}
for the subgraph of
F
induced by the vertex set
W
.

•

Subject to this constraint, the probability of
F
is proportional to its weight.

To see why this holds, observe that running Wilson’s algorithm in this setting is equivalent to running Wilson’s algorithm on a graph
\tilde{G}
, whose vertex set is given by
V
together with an extra sink vertex
w
, where we remove all outgoing edges from
G|_{W}
except those in
H
, and where we connect vertices in
H
to the new sink vertex
w
. We refer to the measure described in the two bullets above as
{\mathtt{Forest}}(G\mid H)
.

Now we examine the upshot of Wilson’s algorithm for the south forest in the two-periodic Aztec diamond. First, for a DCF
F
on
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
, define paths

\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm}=\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm}(F),i=1,\dots,n/2

where (with notation as in
4.2
), the path
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
is the path starting at
v_{i}^{S}
and ending in
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}
in
F
, and the path
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{+}
is the path starting at
v_{i}^{N}
and ending at a sink vertex in
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}
. We let
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(F)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n/2}(\mathcal{S}_{i}^{+}\cup\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-})
. We call
\mathcal{S}
the
backbone
of the DCF
F
, and the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm}
the
backbone paths
.

Lemma 4.10
.

In this lemma,
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
should be understood as a weighted directed graph, with south-biased weights given as in Corollary
4.9
, and
F\sim\mathbb{Q}_{{\mathtt{S}},a,n}
.

1.

Conditional on
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(F)
,
F\sim{\mathtt{Forest}}({\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})\mid\mathcal{S})
.

2.

Let
v\in{\mathtt{S}}
, and let
\Pi^{\mathcal{S}}(v)=(v=v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{T})
denote the path starting at
v
in
F
, stopped at the first vertex
v_{T}\in\mathcal{S}
. Then conditional on
\mathcal{S}
,

(v_{0},\dots,v_{T})\sim\operatorname{LE}(X_{v}),

where
X_{v}
is a random walk on
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
started at
v
, stopped when it hits
\mathcal{S}
.

We can similarly define north backbone paths
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\pm}
, and state a version of
4.10
in this setting.

Proof.

Conditional on
\mathcal{S}
, the only constraint on
F
required for Definition
2.1
is that
F
is a spanning forest of
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathtt{S}})
where every component is oriented towards an element of
\mathcal{S}
. The first part of the lemma then follows from Corollary
4.9
. The second part follows from Wilson’s algorithm for sampling measures of the form
{\mathtt{Forest}}(G\mid H)
.
∎

4.10
gives a precise framework for showing that paths in north/south Temperleyan forests tend to drift north/south, and gives us a probabilistic foothold for understanding the forest measures
\mathbb{Q}_{{\mathtt{C}},a,n}
.

Remark 4.3
.

4.10
says nothing about how we sample the backbone paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm}
prior to filling in the remaining forest. This can again be understood through Wilson’s algorithm. While we will not state a precise correspondence here, one can show that the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm}
are loop-erased random walk paths starting at
\partial^{\mathtt{o}}{\mathtt{S}}
, and conditioned to end at one of the prescribed points on
\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}
, and on non-intersection. The notion of non-intersecting loop-erased random walks, and hence the law of
\mathcal{S}
, can be understood through Fomin’s determinant formula
[
Fom01
]
. Since the backbone paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm}
are coming from
south-drifted
random walks, by conditioning them to hit the east or west boundaries (which forces them to move against their drift), we should expect that they become essentially one-dimensional. Therefore we may loosely expect that the whole ensemble bears a resemblance to non-intersecting one-dimensional walks. This gives some intuition from the forest perspective for why we might expect to see Airy fluctuations. While this is an intriguing picture, it is not as tractable as models of one-dimensional non-intersecting random walks, and we were not to use this description to gain a foothold at the rough-smooth boundary.

4.3.
The smooth phase.

Near the center of the Aztec diamond, and in large regions within corridors, the Aztec diamond measure is close to a full-plane dimer measure called the
smooth phase
. The smooth phase is the local limit away from the boundary of a
2
-periodic dimer model with flat boundary conditions. It is easiest to first describe this measure from the point of view of Temperleyan forests, where we can sample it using
Wilson’s algorithm rooted at
\infty
, see
[
BLPS01
]
. For this algorithm, let
{\mathtt{C}}\in\{{\mathtt{N}},{\mathtt{S}}\}
.

1.

Choose any enumeration
v_{i},i\in\mathbb{N}
of the full-plane vertex set
{\mathtt{\tilde{C}}}
.

2.

Run a random walk
X_{1}
starting at
v_{1}
with
{\mathtt{C}}
-biased edge weights as in Corollary
4.9
. Let
T_{1}=\operatorname{LE}(X_{1})
. Because of the drift,
X_{1}
is transient and so
\operatorname{LE}(X_{1})
is well-defined.

3.

At steps
i=2,3,\dots
, run a random walk
X_{i}
with
{\mathtt{C}}
-biased edge weights until it hits a vertex in
X_{i-1}
. Set
T_{i}=T_{i-1}\cup\operatorname{LE}(X_{i})
. The random walk
X_{i}
hits
T_{i-1}
almost surely.

4.

Set
T=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}T_{i}
.

It is easy to check that the resulting graph
T
is an infinite (spanning) tree on
{\mathtt{\tilde{C}}}
.
We let
\mathbb{Q}_{a,{\mathtt{C}}}
be the law of
T
, and call
T
a
smooth phase
{\mathtt{C}}
-spanning tree
(with parameter
a
).
As with the finite version of Wilson’s algorithm, this law does not depend on the enumeration of
{\mathtt{\tilde{C}}}
. Now, the lattice
{\mathtt{\tilde{S}}}
with edges of the form
\{v,v+2e\}
is dual to the lattice
{\mathtt{\tilde{N}}}
with edges of the same form, and given
T\sim\mathbb{Q}_{a,{\mathtt{S}}}
, we can construct a dual tree
\check{T}
on
{\mathtt{\tilde{N}}}
by letting a dual edge
\check{e}\in\check{T}
if and only if the original edge
e\notin T
. The same duality relation holds between
{\mathtt{\tilde{E}}},{\mathtt{\tilde{W}}}
.

The results of
[
BLPS01
]
(i.e. Theorem 12.2 therein; the versions of these lattices with oriented edge weights as in Corollary
4.9
are dual networks in the sense of that theorem) imply that if
T\sim\mathbb{Q}_{a,{\mathtt{C}}}
, then
\check{T}\sim\mathbb{Q}_{a,{\mathtt{\check{C}}}}
, where
{\mathtt{\check{C}}}
denotes the dual direction. Together we can take the pair
(T,\check{T})
and produce a dimer configuration using an infinite analogue of Temperley’s bijection. Orient both
T,\check{T}
towards
\infty
(i.e. so that there are no sink vertices), and define a perfect matching
D_{{\mathtt{C}}}(T)
of
{\mathtt{\tilde{G}}}=({\mathtt{\tilde{V}}},{\mathtt{\tilde{D}}})
by letting

D_{{\mathtt{C}}}(T)=\{\{v,v+e\}:v\in{\mathtt{C}}\cup{\mathtt{\check{C}}}:(v,v+2e)\text{ is a directed edge in }T\text{ or }\check{T}\}.

Now, given a perfect matching
D
of
{\mathtt{\tilde{G}}}
, we can define an (oriented) spanning forest
F_{\mathtt{C}}(D)
on
{\mathtt{\tilde{C}}}
by letting

F_{\mathtt{C}}(D)=\{(v,v+2e):v\in{\mathtt{C}},\{v,v+e\}\in D\}.

The maps
D_{{\mathtt{C}}}
and
F_{\mathtt{C}}
are inverses. We let

\mathbb{P}_{a}=(D_{\mathtt{S}})_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{a,{\mathtt{S}}}=(D_{\mathtt{N}})_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{a,{\mathtt{N}}}

and call
\mathbb{P}_{a}
the
smooth phase
.

Just as we can define heights in the Aztec diamond, we can also define heights in the smooth phase. As with the Aztec diamond, this requires an anchor point. The natural anchor point is
\infty
, which is the unique choice that guarantees that the height function is ergodic. To define the height function anchored at
\infty
, let
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a}
and
T=F_{\mathtt{S}}(D)
. We first define the height function
h^{f}_{D}
anchored at an
a
-face
f\in{\mathtt{\tilde{F}}}
by using the rules in Section
2.2
, subject to the initial condition
h^{f}_{D}(f)=0
. All these height functions are shifts of each other.

Next, for a simple path
\Pi=(v_{0},v_{1},\dots)
satisfying

(12)

\lim_{n\to\infty}v_{n,2}=-\infty,

we say that the (counterclockwise) winding number of
\Pi
is the number times the path
\Pi
crosses the line
v_{0}+\{0\}\times(0,\infty)
moving right-to-left minus the number of times it crosses moving left-to-right. The southward drift of the random walk and condition (
12
) ensures that each of these counts is finite. We write

\operatorname{Wind}(\Pi)
for the winding number of
\Pi
. For an
a
-face
f
, let
\Pi_{f}
be the path in
T
starting on the
{\mathtt{S}}
-vertex
f-(0,1)
. All paths
\Pi_{f}
satisfy (
12
) almost surely since
T
is built from south-biased random walks, and by this property, there are faces
f
with
\operatorname{Wind}(\Pi_{f})=0
. Choose any such
a
-face
f
and set
h_{D}=h_{D}^{f}
. This is the height function of
D
, anchored at
\infty
.

The next lemma shows that this procedure is well-defined and clarifies the connection between winding numbers and height functions.

Lemma 4.11
.

Suppose that
D
is a dimer configuration on
\tilde{V}
, and that
T=F_{\mathtt{S}}D
is an infinite tree such that the infinite path
(v_{0}=(0,0),v_{1},\dots)
in
T
starting at the origin satisfies (
12
).
Then the function
h_{D}
is well-defined, and for any
a
-face
f
we have

h_{D}(f)=-4\operatorname{Wind}(\Pi_{f}).

Proof.

Both claims in the lemma follow if we can show that

h_{D}^{f}-h_{D}^{f^{\prime}}=4\operatorname{Wind}(\Pi_{f^{\prime}})-4\operatorname{Wind}(\Pi_{f}).

For this, let
v(f)=f-(0,1),v(f^{\prime})=f^{\prime}-(0,1)
. Let
(v,v^{\prime})
be consecutive vertices contained in
\Pi_{f}\cap\Pi_{f^{\prime}}
. By condition (
12
), we can choose
v^{\prime}
so that the paths
\Pi_{f},\Pi_{f^{\prime}}
stay below the line
\mathbb{R}\times\{v_{2}^{\prime}\}
after hitting
v^{\prime}
. By Corollary
4.6
,

h_{D}^{f}(v)=h_{D}^{f}(v(f))+L(f)-R(f),

where
L
is the number of left turns of
\Pi_{f}
prior to hitting
v^{\prime}
and
R
is the number of right turns. A case-by-case analysis starting from the fact that
h_{D}^{f}(f)=0
shows that
h_{D}^{f}(v(f))=(2/\pi)\theta_{f}
, where
\theta_{f}\in(-\pi,\pi)
is the angle formed between the edge
e
leaving
v(f)
and the south-pointing vector
(0,-1)
. Since
\Pi_{f}
stays below the line
\mathbb{R}\times\{v_{2}^{\prime}\}
after hitting
v^{\prime}
,

h_{D}^{f}(v)=(2/\pi)\theta_{f}+L(f)-R(f)=(2/\pi)\theta_{f}-4\operatorname{Wind}(\Pi_{f})-(2/\pi)\gamma_{f},

where
\gamma_{f}
is the angle formed by the edges
e
and
(v,v^{\prime})
. A similar computation holds for
h_{D}^{f^{\prime}}(v)
, and the lemma follows since
\theta_{f}-\gamma_{f}=\theta_{f^{\prime}}-\gamma_{f^{\prime}}
is the angle formed by the edge
(v,v^{\prime})
and the vector
(0,-1)
.
∎

We end this section with a symmetry of the smooth phase that falls out of
4.11
.

Corollary 4.12
.

If
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a}
and
f
is any
a
-face or
b
-face, then

h_{D}(f+(x,y))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}-h_{D}(f+(-x,y)),

where the distributional equality is jointly over all
(x,y)\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
L
be any vertical line with
x
-coordinate
x_{0}\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
. Reflect the spanning tree
T\sim\mathbb{Q}_{a,S}
across
L
to get a new spanning tree
T^{\prime}
. The trees
T,T^{\prime}
are equal in law by the symmetry in Wilson’s algorithm, and moreover, letting
D=D_{\mathtt{S}}T,D^{\prime}=D_{\mathtt{S}}T^{\prime}
, by
4.11
we can see that

h_{D}(x_{0}+x,y)=-h_{D^{\prime}}(x_{0}-x,y)
for all
(x,y)\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
. Since
a
and
b
faces have
x
-coordinates in
2\mathbb{Z}+1
, the lemma follows.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Corollary
4.12
does not hold if
f
is not an
a
- or
b
-face, and this corollary is why we choose to anchor our height function to be
0
on an
a
-face, rather than at the origin, which will have height in
4\mathbb{Z}+1
. This convention differs from our convention for the finite Aztec diamond, where the origin has height in
4\mathbb{Z}
and
a
-faces have heights in
4\mathbb{Z}-1
. We have chosen the latter convention for the Aztec diamond to accommodate the remarkable symmetry
\mathbb{E}h_{D}(0,0)=0
, where
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
, see
5.1
. In the smooth phase, no reasonable convention on the heights yields an integer value for
\mathbb{E}h_{D}(0,0)
, whereas our choice gives
\mathbb{E}h_{D}(f)=0
for any
a
- or
b
-face
f
through Corollary
4.12
.

5.
Basic tools

In this section, we gather together the three sets of basic tools discussed at the beginning of Section
3
. Most of the height function and smooth phase coupling estimates (tools 1 and 2) in the upcoming Sections
5.1
and
5.2
have integrable proofs, and these are postponed until the latter sections of the paper. On the other hand, the straightforward probabilistic estimates on random walks and spanning trees in Section
5.3
are proven therein. A final section (Section
5.4
) combines all three tools to produce a few new height function estimates.

5.1.
Height function estimates

Throughout this section we write
\mathcal{H}_{n}:{\mathtt{\bar{F}}}\to\mathbb{Z}
for the height function of a dimer configuration
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
. In order to state some asymptotics it will also be convenient to extend
\mathcal{H}_{n}
to all of
[-n,n]^{2}
by letting
\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)=\mathcal{H}_{n}([v])
, where
[v]
is the nearest face to the point
v
, breaking ties arbitrarily.

We start with a remarkable symmetry of the two-periodic Aztec diamond.

Lemma 5.1
.

For any face
F=(i,j)\in{\mathtt{F}}
with
i,j\in 2\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(i,j)=-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(-i,j)=-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(i,-j)=\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(-i,-j)

In particular,
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(2i,0)=\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(0,2i)=0
for all
-n/2\leq i\leq n/2
.

The anti-symmetry
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(i,j)=-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(-i,j)
in
5.1
is completely surprising and comes from a delicate formula comparison. We do not have a combinatorial explanation. It is equivalent to a surprising symmetry in height expectation after changing the
a
-face weights from
a
to
1/a
.
5.1
is proven in
Section

12.1
.
We also need the following result from
[
BN25
]
.

Lemma 5.2
.

[
BN25
, special case of Theorem 3.1]

The central height
\mathcal{H}_{n}(0,0)
converges in law to a limiting random variable
X
as
n\to\infty
. Moreover,
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(0,0)^{k}\to\mathbb{E}X^{k}\in\mathbb{R}
as
n\to\infty
, for all fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 5.1
.

The random variable
X
can be written as a sum of independent random variables
H+Y
, where
H
is the limit of the averaged central height
H_{n}
defined prior to
2.1
, and
Y
is the height at the origin
(0,0)
in the smooth phase. Theorem 1.1 in
[
BN25
]
shows that
H
is a discrete Gaussian random variable (as with
5.2
,
[
BN25
]
also prove this in much greater generality). The random variable
Y
, which is the winding number of a biased loop-erased random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, should also be a discrete Gaussian random variable. We do not pursue this here.

More remarkably, we believe that
H\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}-Y
, though we do not have a proof. One upshot of
5.1
is that
\mathbb{E}X=0
, and so
\mathbb{E}H=-\mathbb{E}Y
. We can also compute that
\mathbb{E}H^{2}=\mathbb{E}Y^{2}
and give explicit formulas for this second moment. This computation also gives an explicit evaluation for the right-hand side of
[
BN25
, Equation (61)]
for the special case of the two-periodic Aztec diamond. Since these formulas are not relevant to our argument, we omit this computation as it adds considerable length.

In Section
5.4
, we will use global smooth phase couplings and estimates on the heights in the smooth phase to propagate the height estimates at the origin given by the previous two lemmas throughout the smooth region.

For now, we will move into the rough-smooth boundary and the rough phase. We record two expectation computations, to be proven in
Section

12.2
.

To state the next lemma, recall from the preamble to
3.2
that
\xi_{0}:[-\alpha,\alpha]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the rough-smooth boundary curve in the third quadrant, parametrized so that for all
t\in[-\alpha,\alpha]
, the point
\xi_{0}(t)
is on the line
\{{\bf v}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mathbf{v}\cdot e_{2}=t\}
. Recall the coordinate change
\beta_{n}:[-\alpha n,\alpha n]\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\beta_{n}(t,x)=n\xi_{0}(t/n)+e_{1}x.

In words,
\beta_{n}(t,x)
gives a point at location
t
along the rough-smooth boundary curve, at distance
x
away from the boundary. Note that the transformation
\beta_{n}
does not scale space; the scaling
n\xi_{0}(\cdot/n)
simply puts the limit curve back in unscaled coordinates.

Lemma 5.3
.

There exists
\eta_{a}>0
such that the following holds for all large enough
n
. For all
t\in[-n^{5/6},n^{5/6}]
and
x\in[-n^{8/15},2n^{1/3}\log^{2}n]
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(\beta_{n}(t,x))+\eta_{a}|x|^{3/2}n^{-1/2}|

\displaystyle\leq n^{1/6}.

Lemma 5.4
.

There exists a constant
\mu_{a}>0
such that the following holds for all large enough
n
. For all
x\leq 2n^{1/3}\log^{2}n
we have

\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(\beta_{n}(0,x))\leq-\mu_{a}|x|^{3/2}n^{-1/2}+n^{1/6}

whenever
\beta_{n}(0,x)\in[-n,n]^{2}
.

We can also establish concentration around the expectations in
5.3
and
5.4
. However, we defer this computation to Section
5.4
, since it requires a connection to the smooth region via a smooth phase coupling.

5.2.
Smooth phase couplings

In this section, we collect coupling results between the smooth phase and the Aztec diamond. We start with a local coupling result at the rough-smooth boundary from
[
BCJ22
]
.

Lemma 5.5
.

(Local smooth coupling, Proposition 4.5,
[
BCJ22
]
)

Fix
L>0
, and consider a subset

\Lambda\subset\beta_{n}([-Ln^{2/3},Ln^{2/3}]\times[-Ln^{1/3},Ln^{1/3}]).

Then there exists a constant
C=C(L)>0
such that

d_{\operatorname{TV}}(\mathbb{P}_{a,n}|_{\Lambda},\mathbb{P}_{a}|_{\Lambda})\leq Cn^{-1/3}|\Lambda|^{4}.

Here
d_{\operatorname{TV}}
denotes the total variation distance between the measures.

In
[
BCJ22
]
, this lemma is stated only for boxes. However, the proof goes through verbatim regardless of the geometry of the set
\Lambda
, see Section 7 in
[
BCJ22
]
or the similar proofs of
5.6
and
5.7
in
Section

11
. The set
\beta_{n}([-Ln^{2/3},Ln^{2/3}]\times[-Ln^{1/3},Ln^{1/3}])
appearing in the lemma statement simply corresponds to a region of fixed size at the rough-smooth boundary after we pass to the limiting coordinates. We note that it is not difficult to use the Gibbs property for dimers and replace the factor
|\Lambda|^{4}
by
|\partial\Lambda|^{4}
in the above lemma, where
\partial\Lambda
is the inner boundary of
\Lambda
. However, this improvement does not help us so we have not included this strengthening here.

If we move away from the rough-smooth boundary and into the smooth region, then we can improve the total variation bound in
5.5
and couple on larger sets. The next two lemmas give two couplings on specific larger sets. The choice of parameters in the sets are somewhat arbitrary, and chosen for ease of proof.

Figure 15
.
The coupling sets
\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1},\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
depicted within the smooth region. The green box is the rough-smooth boundary where we will study Airy fluctuations, and where
5.5
holds. The figure is not to scale, e.g. the gap between
\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
and the Airy region is of size
n^{1/3}\log^{2}n
, and the overlap between
\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1},\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
is of order
n^{6/7}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

(Global smooth coupling)

There exist
a
-dependent constants
\gamma_{a},d_{a}>0
such that the following holds. First define

\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}=\beta_{n}([-n^{5/6},n^{5/6}]\times[n^{6/7},n(|\xi_{0}(0)|+\gamma_{a})).

Then

d_{\operatorname{TV}}(\mathbb{P}_{a,n}|_{\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}},\mathbb{P}_{a}|_{\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}})\leq 3e^{-d_{a}n^{6/7}}.

The same bound holds with
\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}
replaced by any of its rotations
R_{\pi/2}\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}
,
R_{\pi}\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}
, or
R_{3\pi/2}\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}
, where
R_{\theta}
denotes counterclockwise rotation by
\theta
.

Lemma 5.7
.

(Mesoscopic smooth coupling)

Define the region

\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}=\beta_{n}([-n^{5/6},n^{5/6}]\times[n^{1/3}\log^{2}n,2n^{6/7}]).

Then there exists an
a
-dependent constant
d_{a}>0
such that

d_{\operatorname{TV}}(\mathbb{P}_{a,n}|_{\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}},\mathbb{P}_{a}|_{\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}})\leq 3e^{-d_{a}\log^{2}n}.

The same bound holds with
\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
replaced by any of its rotations
R_{\pi/2}\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
,
R_{\pi}\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
, or
R_{3\pi/2}\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
.

Remark 5.2
.

Due asymptotics for
K^{-1}_{a,1}(x,y)
, the inverse Kasteleyn matrix entry evaluated at
x
and
y
(see
Section

10
for definitions) given in
[
BN25
, Lemma 5.3]
for two vertices
x
and
y
in the interior of the smooth region, it is not hard to show by mirroring the same proofs as the ones given in
5.6
and
5.7
, that a smooth phase coupling for the entire smooth region as long as we are an appropriate mesoscopic distance away from the boundary. Indeed, such a result follows from the fact that the error between
K^{-1}_{a,1}(x,y)
and its smooth phase counterpart is exponentially small, as established in
[
BN25
, Lemma 5.3]
. An immediate consequence is that the mesoscopic average of the height function considered in our main theorems is equal with high probability to the macroscopic average height function considered in
[
BN25
]
.

The proof of these results can be found in
Section

11
. Note that a smooth phase coupling with the same order of error as in
5.7
should hold on the union
\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}\cup\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
. The reason we prove couplings separately on
\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}
and
\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
instead has to do with technical considerations around a choice of conjugation for the kernel for the determinantal measure
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
.
See
Figure

15
for an illustration of Lemmas
5.5
,
5.6
, and
5.7
.

5.3.
Random walk and spanning tree estimates

Next, we record a few basic estimates coming from standard random walk bounds. Here and throughout the paper, we say that a random walk on
{\mathtt{\tilde{S}}}
is
south-biased
(or north-biased) if it moves according to the edge weights in Corollary
4.9
.

We start with a straightforward estimate on the path of a biased random walk. For this lemma and through the section, for a path
X=(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},\dots)
we write
\operatorname{Range}(X)
for the set of vertices visited by
X
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
X=(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},\dots)
be a south-biased random walk on
{\mathtt{\tilde{S}}}
started at
X_{0}=(1,0)
. For
\alpha\geq 2
, define the (roughly) parabolic region

P^{\alpha}_{\mathtt{S}}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:y\leq\alpha,|x|\leq(\alpha+\log(y^{-}+1))\sqrt{y^{-}+1}\},

where
y^{-}=\max(-y,0)
.
Then for an
a
-dependent constant
d_{a}>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Range}(X)\not\subset P^{n}_{\mathtt{S}})\leq 2\exp(-d_{a}\alpha).

Moving forward, we will also use the notation
P^{\alpha}_{\mathtt{N}}=\rho_{\mathtt{N}}P^{\alpha}_{\mathtt{S}}
.

Proof.

Write
X_{i,1},X_{i,2}
for the
x
- and
y
-coordinates of
X
. Then
X_{\cdot,1}
is a simple symmetric random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
, and
X_{\cdot,2}
is a biased random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
with step probabilities

\mathbb{P}(X_{i,2}-X_{i-1,2}=1)=a^{2}/(1+a^{2}),\qquad\mathbb{P}(X_{i,2}-X_{i-1,2}=-1)=1/(1+a^{2}).

Let
\mu_{a}=\mathbb{E}(X_{i,2}-X_{i-1,2})=(a^{2}-1)/(1+a^{2})
. Then by Hoeffding’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle(X_{\ell}\notin P^{\alpha}_{\mathtt{S}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(|X_{\ell,1}|>(\alpha+\log(\ell|\mu_{a}|/2+1))\sqrt{\ell|\mu_{a}|/2+1}\Big)+\mathbb{P}(X_{\ell,2}>\ell\mu_{a}/2)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp(-|\mu_{a}|^{2}(\alpha+\log(\ell|\mu_{a}|/2+1))^{2}/16)+\exp(-\ell\mu_{a}^{2}/8).

On the other hand, by a union bound

\mathbb{P}(\exists i\in\mathbb{N}\text{ s.t. }X_{i}\notin P^{\alpha}_{\mathtt{S}})\leq\sum_{\ell=\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(X_{\ell}\notin P^{\alpha}_{\mathtt{S}}),

where the sum starts at
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor
, since prior to time
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor
,
X_{\ell}
is deterministically contained in
P^{\alpha}_{\mathtt{S}}
. Combining this with the previous bound gives the result.
∎

The above random walk estimate yields a simple bound for the behaviour of south forest paths in the Aztec diamond off of the backbone.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
F\sim\mathbb{Q}_{{\mathtt{S}},a,n}
, let
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(F)
be the backbone of
F
, and let
\Pi^{\mathcal{S}}(v)
denote the path in
F
from a vertex
v
stopped when it first hits the backbone, as in
4.10
. Define the event

\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{S}}(\alpha)=\{\operatorname{Range}(\Pi^{\mathcal{S}}(v))\subset P^{\alpha}_{\mathtt{S}}+v\text{ for all }v\in{\mathtt{S}}\}.

Then

\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{S}}(\alpha)\mid\mathcal{S})\geq 1-2n^{2}\exp(-d_{a}\alpha).

Here we are conditioning on the entire backbone of
F
, i.e. all south backbone paths.

Proof.

This follows from Part (2) of
4.10
,
5.8
, and a union bound. ∎

We end this section by establishing decorrelation and concentration estimates for heights in the smooth phase.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a}
and let
f\in{\mathtt{\tilde{F}}}
be any face. Then

(13)

\mathbb{P}(|h_{D}(f)|>m)\leq 2\exp(-d_{a}m^{2})

for all
m>0
.
Moreover, for any two faces
f,f^{\prime}
we have the covariance estimate

\operatorname{Cov}(h_{D}(f),h_{D}(f^{\prime}))\leq c_{a}\exp(-d_{a}\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{\infty}).

Proof.

We first prove the lemma in the setting where all faces are
a
-faces.

Let
f
be any
a
-face. We have
h_{D}(f)=-4\operatorname{Wind}(\Pi_{f})
, with notation as in
4.11
. From Wilson’s algorithm,
\Pi_{f}=\operatorname{LE}(X)
, where
X
is a south-biased random walks started at
v_{f}=f-(0,1)
.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, on the event
\operatorname{Wind}(\Pi_{f})\geq m
, the random walk
X
must satisfy
X_{\ell,2}\geq(v_{f})_{2}
for some
\ell\geq m^{2}/4
. Using Hoeffding’s inequality and a union bound as in proof of
5.8

\mathbb{P}(\exists\ell\geq m^{2}/4\text{ s.t. }X_{\ell,2}\geq(v_{f})_{2})\leq\sum_{\ell=m^{2}/4}^{\infty}\exp(-\ell\mu_{a}^{2}/4)\leq 2\exp(-d_{a}m^{2}),

giving (
13
).

We move on to the covariance estimate, starting with the case when
f,f^{\prime}
are
a
-faces. We may assume
f_{2}\leq f^{\prime}_{2}
, and set
\alpha=\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{\infty}
and
v:=f-(0,1),v^{\prime}:=f^{\prime}-(0,1)
. We have that

(14)

[P^{\alpha/6}_{\mathtt{N}}+v^{\prime}]\cap[P^{\alpha/6}_{\mathtt{S}}+w]=\emptyset,\qquad\text{ for all }w\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},w\in P^{\alpha/6}_{\mathtt{S}}+v.

Here recall that
P^{\alpha/6}_{\mathtt{N}}
is a north-facing parabola given by rotating
P^{\alpha/6}_{\mathtt{S}}
by
\pi
.
Let
X,X^{\prime}
be independent south-biased walks starting at
v,v^{\prime}
. Then by Wilson’s algorithm, we have

(\Pi_{f},\Pi_{f^{\prime}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(\operatorname{LE}(X),Y)

where
Y
is given by
\operatorname{LE}(X^{\prime}|_{[0,T]})
, where
T
is the first time
X^{\prime}
hits
\operatorname{LE}(X)
, concatenated with the remainder of the path to infinity in
\operatorname{LE}(X)
at the hitting location of
X^{\prime}
. To prove the estimate in the lemma, we will show that

(15)

\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Wind}(Y)\neq\operatorname{Wind}(\operatorname{LE}(X^{\prime})))\leq 4\exp(-d_{a}\alpha).

To see how this proves the covariance bound, observe that gives a coupling between the independent pair
(\frac{1}{4}(\operatorname{Wind}(\operatorname{LE}(X)),\frac{1}{4}(\operatorname{Wind}(\operatorname{LE}(X^{\prime})))
and the height vector
(h_{D}(f),h_{D}(f^{\prime}))

such that the two disagree with probability at most
c_{a}\exp(-d_{a}\alpha)
. The covariance estimate follows by using this coupling together with the tail bound in (
13
).

To prove (
15
), observe that by
5.8
,

(16)

\operatorname{LE}(X)\subset P^{\alpha/6}_{\mathtt{S}}+v

with probability at least
1-2\exp(-d_{a}\alpha)
. Now, let
w
be the final vertex of
Y
that is contained in
P_{{\mathtt{N}}}^{\alpha/6}+v^{\prime}
, and let
\bar{Y}
be the segment of
Y
ending at
w
. Similarly, let
w^{\prime}
be the final vertex on
\operatorname{LE}(X^{\prime})
that is in
P_{{\mathtt{N}}}^{\alpha/6}+v^{\prime}
, and let
\bar{X}^{\prime}
be the segment of
\operatorname{LE}(X^{\prime})
ending at
w^{\prime}
. If
\bar{X}^{\prime}=\bar{Y}
, then
Y
and
\operatorname{LE}(X^{\prime})
have the same winding number, so it suffices to estimate the probability that
\bar{X}^{\prime}\neq\bar{Y}
.

In order for
\bar{X}^{\prime}
and
\bar{Y}
to disagree on the event (
16
), the path
X^{\prime}
would have to re-enter
P_{{\mathtt{N}}}^{\alpha/6}+v^{\prime}
after entering the set
P^{\alpha/6}_{\mathtt{S}}+v
. By the relation (
14
), if
T
denotes the time when
X^{\prime}
hits
\operatorname{LE}(X)
, then this would require that

X^{\prime}|_{[T,\infty)}-X^{\prime}(T)\not\subset P_{{\mathtt{S}}}^{\alpha/6},

which has probability bounded above by
2\exp(-d_{a}\alpha)
by another application of
5.8
(and the fact that
T
is a stopping time, so
X^{\prime}|_{[T,\infty)}-X^{\prime}(T)
is another south-biased random walk). This completes the proof of the coupling.

The general case of the lemma when some faces are not
a
-faces follows from a similar argument, this time decomposing the height of a face as a winding number plus a term coming from the initial direction of the loop-erased random walk.
∎

5.4.
Compound estimates

We end Section
5
with two height estimates that combine results from the three sections above. The first estimate gives expectation and concentration estimates on the height function throughout the portion of the smooth region where we have smooth phase couplings. With notation as in
5.6
and
5.7
, define the cross-shaped set

{\mathtt{Cross}}={\mathtt{Cross}}_{n}:=\bigcup_{\theta\in\{0,\pi/2,\pi,3\pi/2\}}R_{\theta}(\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}\cup\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}).

For this lemma, a face
f=(i,j)\in{\mathtt{F}}
is a
c
-face if
i,j\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and
i+j\in 4\mathbb{Z}
.

Corollary 5.11
.

For all large enough
n
, for all
c
-faces
f\in{\mathtt{Cross}}
we have that

|\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)|\leq 2\exp(-d_{a}n^{6/7}),\qquad\mathbb{E}|\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)|^{2}\leq c_{a}

for constants
c_{a},d_{a}>0
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma when
f\in\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}\cup\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
, as the claim on the rest of
{\mathtt{Cross}}
follows by a symmetric argument. First assume
f\in\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}
. By
5.6
we can couple the height function
\mathcal{H}_{n}
to the smooth phase height function
\mathcal{H}
so that with probability at least
1-3e^{-d_{a}n^{6/7}}
we have

(17)

\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)-\mathcal{H}_{n}(0,0)=\mathcal{H}(f)-\mathcal{H}(0,0).

Importantly, the coupling in
5.6
only couples the increments of the height function, since the anchor points for the two height functions are different. Therefore letting
A
be the event where (
17
) holds, and letting
X=\mathcal{H}_{n}(0,0)+\mathcal{H}(v)-\mathcal{H}(0,0)
, we have

\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)=X+\mathbf{1}(A^{c})(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)-X)

Now,
\mathbb{E}X=0
since
\mathcal{H}(f)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{H}(0,0)
by the translation invariance of
\mathbb{P}_{a}
, and
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(0,0)=0
by
5.1
. Moroever, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

|\mathbb{E}\mathbf{1}(A^{c})(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)-X)|\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(A^{c})}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)-X)^{2}}\leq c_{a}n\exp(-d_{a}n^{-6/7}),

where the final inequality uses that
\mathbb{P}(A^{c})\leq 3e^{-d_{a}n^{6/7}}
, the tail bound on heights in the smooth phase (
5.10
), and the fact that
|\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)|\leq 4n
deterministically since
\mathcal{H}_{n}
has increments bounded by
4
on adjacent faces, and fixed boundary conditions. Combining these results gives the expectation bound, after changing
d_{a}
. The second moment bound follows similarly, this time using that
\mathbb{E}X^{2}\leq c_{a}
by
5.2
and
5.10
.

Now, for a
c
-face
f\in\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
, consider any
c
-face
f^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}\cap\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}
. Then by
5.7
we can couple
\mathcal{H}_{n}
to the smooth phase height function
\mathcal{H}
such that

\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)-\mathcal{H}_{n}(f^{\prime})=\mathcal{H}(f)-\mathcal{H}(f^{\prime})

with probability at least
1-2\exp(-d_{a}\log^{2}n)
. We can then proceed similarly to bound
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)
and
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)^{2}
, this time using as input the estimates on the moments of
\mathcal{H}_{n}(f^{\prime})
from this lemma, rather than those for
\mathcal{H}_{n}(0,0)
.
∎

Next, we aim to upgrade the expectation estimates in Lemmas
5.3
and
5.4
to concentration estimates. We will use the following standard concentration inequality.

Lemma 5.12
.

For any faces
f,f^{\prime}\in{\mathtt{F}}
and any
r>0
we have

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)-\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)\mid H_{n}(f^{\prime}))|\geq r\sqrt{\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{1}})\leq 2\exp(-r^{2}/18).

Moreover, the function

x\mapsto\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)\mid H_{n}(f^{\prime})=x),\qquad x\in 4\mathbb{Z}

is a non-decreasing
1
-Lipschitz function of
x
.

Proof.

The concentration bound follows from a martingale argument and Azuma’s inequality, see
[
Gor21
, Theorem 5.8]
for details. The fact that the function
\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)\mid H_{n}(f^{\prime})=x)
is
1
-Lipschitz and non-decreasing follows from height monotonicity for random tilings. The details of the argument are again contained in the proof of
[
Gor21
, Theorem 5.8]
.
∎

Corollary 5.13
.

Define the region

{\mathtt{Cap}}=\beta_{n}([-n^{5/6},n^{5/6}]\times[-n^{1/2}\log^{2}n,2n^{1/3}\log^{2}n]).

Let
A_{n}
be the event where for all
v=\beta_{n}(t,x)\in{\mathtt{Cap}}
we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{H}_{n}(R_{\theta}v)+\eta_{a}|x|^{3/2}n^{-1/2}|

\displaystyle\leq n^{1/4}\log^{2}n,\quad\theta=0,\pi,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{H}_{n}(R_{\theta}v)-\eta_{a}|x|^{3/2}n^{-1/2}|

\displaystyle\leq n^{1/4}\log^{2}n,\quad\theta=\pm\pi/2.

Here
\eta_{a}
is the constant from
5.3
.
Then
\mathbb{P}(A_{n})=1-o(n^{-1/6})
.

Similarly, let
B_{n}
be the event where for all
v=\beta_{n}(0,x)\in[-n,n]^{2}
with
x\leq 2n^{1/3}\log^{2}n
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{n}(R_{\theta}v)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\mu_{a}}{2}|x|^{3/2}n^{-1/2}+n^{1/4}\log^{2}n,\quad\theta=0,\pi.

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{n}(R_{\theta}v)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mu_{a}}{2}|x|^{3/2}n^{-1/2}+n^{1/4}\log^{2}n,\quad\theta=\pm\pi/2.

Here
\mu_{a}
is as in
5.4
. Then
\mathbb{P}(B_{n})=1-o(n^{-1/6})
.

Proof.

Let
A_{n}^{0}\supset A_{n},B_{n}^{0}\supset B_{n}
be the versions of these events where we only ask the inequalities to holds when
\theta=0
. We first prove that
\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{0}),\mathbb{P}(B^{n}_{0})=1-o(n^{-1/6})
. Throughout the proof we focus on bounding heights on the face set
{\mathtt{F}}
. The extension to all general heights in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
follows by approximation through the nearest face.

Bounding
\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{0})
.
For
i\in[-n^{1/3},n^{1/3}]\cap\mathbb{Z}
, let
f_{i}
be the
c
-face closest to the point
\beta_{n}(n^{1/2}i,n^{1/2})
. Then for every face
f\in{\mathtt{Cap}}
, we can find an index
I(f)\in[-n^{1/3},n^{1/3}]\cap\mathbb{Z}
with
\|F-F_{I(f)}\|_{1}\leq 2n^{1/2}\log^{2}n
. Therefore by applying
5.12
with
r=\log n/2
, taking a union bound over the
o(n^{2})
-many faces contained in
{\mathtt{Cap}}
, with probability at least
1-o(n^{2})\cdot\exp(-\log^{2}n/72)
we have

(18)

|\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)-\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}(f_{I(f)}))|\leq n^{1/4}\log^{2}n/\sqrt{2}.

Next, we claim that there exists a constant
c_{a}>0
such that for all faces
f,f_{i}
and all
j_{0}\in 4\mathbb{Z}
we have

(19)

|\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}(f_{i})=j_{0})-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)|\leq c_{a}+j_{0}.

Indeed, we can write

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)-\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}(f_{i})=j_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in 4\mathbb{Z}}\Big|\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}(f_{i})=j)-\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}(f_{i})=j_{0})\Big|\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f_{i})=j)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in 4\mathbb{Z}}|j-j_{0}|\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f_{i})=j)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}|\mathcal{H}_{n}(f_{i})-j_{0}|\leq c_{a}+j_{0}.

Here the second inequality uses that
\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}(f_{i})=j)
is a
1
-Lipschitz function of
j
(
5.12
), and the final inequality uses Corollary
5.11
. Finally, Corollary
5.11
, Chebyshev’s inequality, and a union bound give that

(20)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}(\text{ There exists }i\in[-n^{1/3},n^{1/3}]\cap\mathbb{Z}\text{ s.t. }|\mathcal{H}_{n}(F_{i})|\geq\tfrac{1}{4}n^{1/4}\log^{2}n)\\
&\leq c_{a}n^{-1/6}\log^{-4}n.\end{split}

Together with (
18
), (
19
), and the estimate from
5.3
, this implies
\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{0})=1-o(n^{-1/6})
.

Bounding
\mathbb{P}(B_{n}^{0})
.
This estimate is similar. Indeed, similarly to (
18
), with probability at least
1-2n^{2}\exp(-\log^{2}n/72)
, for all faces of the form
f=\beta_{n}(0,x)
for some
x\leq 2n^{1/3}\log^{2}n
we have

(21)

|\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)-\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}(f_{0}))|\leq\frac{1}{2}(n^{1/2}-x)^{1/2}\log n,

where
f_{0}=\beta_{n}(0,n^{1/2})
as before. Moreover, from Corollary
5.11
and Chebyshev’s inequality we have

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{H}_{n}(f_{0})|\geq n^{1/10})\leq c_{a}n^{-1/5}.

Combining this bound, (
19
), (
21
), and
5.4
gives that with probability
1-o(n^{-1/6})
, for all faces
f=\beta_{n}(0,x)
with
x\leq 2n^{1/3}\log^{2}n
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)\leq

\displaystyle-\mu_{a}|x|^{3/2}n^{-1/2}+\frac{1}{2}(n^{1/2}-x)^{1/2}\log n+c_{a}+n^{1/10}+n^{1/6}.

yields the desired estimate on
\mathbb{P}(B_{n}^{0})
after simplification.

Bounding
\mathbb{P}(A_{n}),\mathbb{P}(B_{n})
.
To extend the height estimates on
\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{0}),\mathbb{P}(B_{n}^{0})
to all of
A_{n},B_{n}
, observe that all steps go through verbatim, except we cannot apply Lemmas
5.3
and
5.4
directly since these lemmas do not pertain to points of the form
R_{\theta}v
where
v\in{\mathtt{Cap}}
or
v=\beta_{n}(0,x)\in[-n,n]^{2}
and
\theta\neq 0
. Rather, we need to apply these lemmas in conjunction with the symmetries in expectation from
5.1
(in the case when
\theta=\pi
, we could also use the stronger symmetries from
4.1
, but this is not useful for
\theta=\pm\pi/2
).
∎

6.
An Airy surface in a smooth background

In this section, we prove
2.1
, following the proof sketch outlined in Section
3
. We will also prove a few variants of this result which offer different perspectives on the idea that at the rough-smooth boundary we see an Airy surface sitting in a smooth background. As discussed there, our starting point is the main result of
[
BCJ18
]
, which shows that Airy statistics arise after taking small averages of the height function around the rough-smooth boundary.

6.1.
The Airy line ensemble, the Airy surface, and the main result of
[
BCJ18
]

We start by giving a formal definition of the Airy line ensemble.

Definition 6.1
.

The (stationary)
Airy line ensemble
is a random sequence of continuous functions
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{i}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},i\in\mathbb{N})
whose law is uniquely characterized by the following two properties:

•

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{A}_{i}(x)>\mathcal{A}_{i+1}(x)
.

•

For any finite set of times
T=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}
, the point process

\bigcup\{(t_{j},\mathcal{A}_{i}(t_{j})):i\in\mathbb{N},j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\}

is a determinantal point process on
T\times\mathbb{R}
with kernel

(22)

{\mathcal{A}}(\tau_{1},\zeta_{1};\tau_{2},\zeta_{2})=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\tau_{1},\zeta_{1};\tau_{2},\zeta_{2})-\phi_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}),

where

(23)

\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\tau_{1},\zeta_{1};\tau_{2},\zeta_{2})&=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})}\mathrm{Ai}(\zeta_{1}+\lambda)\mathrm{Ai}(\zeta_{2}+\lambda)d\lambda\\
\end{split}

and

(24)

\phi_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=\mathbbm{I}_{\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})}}e^{-\frac{(\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2})^{2}}{4(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})}-\frac{(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})(\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{2})}{2}+\frac{(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})^{3}}{12}}.

The function
\mathcal{A}
is known as the
extended Airy kernel
.

In the above definition and in the sequel we use the integer interval notation
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket:=\{a,\dots,b\}
.
Next, as in the introduction we define the
Airy surface

\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\{0,1,2,\dots\}
by letting

\mathcal{A}(x,t)=\#\{i\in\mathbb{N}:\mathcal{A}_{i}(t)\geq x\}.

This function is well-defined since
\mathcal{A}_{k}\to-\infty
as
k\to\infty
, uniformly on compact sets. This follows, for example, from
[
DV21
, Theorem 1.6]
. The Airy surface is the analogue of the height function for random tilings, and so it is a natural object to introduce when studying height function convergence in the absence of a tractable family of non-intersecting paths.

Next, we recast the main theorem of
[
BCJ18
]
in terms of height function convergence to the Airy surface. To set up some notation, for a finite set
\phi
and a point
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, define the mollified height function

(25)

\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi}(v)=\frac{1}{|\phi|}\sum_{u\in\phi}\mathcal{H}_{n}(v+u),

with ties broken using the lexicographic order. Also, define the scaling transformation

\tilde{\beta}_{n}(t,x)=[\beta_{n}(2^{1/3}\lambda_{2}n^{2/3}t,2^{2/3}\lambda_{1}n^{1/3}x)]_{a},

where the constants
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
are as in (
5
). Here the outer bracket
[\cdot]_{a}
maps a point in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to the nearest
a
-face, where ties are broken with the lexicographic order. The transformation
\tilde{\beta}_{n}
maps limiting coordinates at the rough-smooth boundary to unscaled coordinates in the two-periodic Aztec diamond. This is the scaling transformation used in
[
BCJ18
]
, and differs from the scaling transformations
\gamma_{n},\hat{\gamma}_{n}
we defined in the introduction by a shear that becomes negligible in the
n\to\infty
limit. For most of this section, we will work with the transformation
\tilde{\beta}_{n}
, in order to more easily work with the results of
[
BCJ18
]
. We translate to the scaling
\hat{\gamma}_{n}
in a straightforward final step. Next, define

(26)

\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}:=\mathcal{H}_{n}\circ\tilde{\beta}_{n},\qquad\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi}:=\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi}\circ\tilde{\beta}_{n}

for the height function and mollified height function defined in the limiting coordinates.

Theorem 6.1

(Theorem 1.1,
[
BCJ18
]
)
.

Suppose we have the following data:

•

A collection of times
t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq\cdots\leq t_{k}
and spatial locations
x_{1}\leq y_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq y_{2}\cdots\leq x_{k}\leq y_{k}
.

•

Mollifiers
\phi_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
of the form

\phi_{n}=\{2\lfloor\ell\log^{2}n\rfloor e_{2}:\ell\in\{0,\dots,a_{n}\}\},

where the sequence
a_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and satisfies
a_{n}=o(n^{1/3}\log^{-2}n)
as
n\to\infty
.

•

Sequences
t_{i,n}\to t_{i},x_{i,n}\to x_{i},y_{i,n}\to y_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,k
such that for every
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
we have that

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}n^{2/3}t_{i,n}+2a_{n}\log^{2}n+4

\displaystyle<\lambda_{2}n^{2/3}t_{i+1,n}+2a_{n}\log^{2}n,

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}n^{1/3}x_{i,n}+8<\lambda_{1}n^{1/3}y_{i,n}+4

\displaystyle<\lambda_{1}n^{1/3}x_{i+1,n}.

Then we have the following Fourier transform convergence. There exists a constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, such that for any
\lambda=(\lambda_{i,j})\in\mathbb{C}^{k\times\ell},\|\lambda\|_{\infty}<\varepsilon_{0}
we have

(27)

\begin{split}\lim_{n\to\infty}&\mathbb{E}\exp\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\lambda_{i,j}[\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{j,n},y_{i,n})-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{j,n},x_{i,n})]\right)\\
=\;\;&\mathbb{E}\exp\left(-4\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\lambda_{i,j}[\mathcal{A}(t_{j},y_{i})-\mathcal{A}(t_{j},x_{i})]\right).\end{split}

This theorem is a stronger version of the statement (
9
) described in the introduction. Note that in the original statement in
[
BCJ18
]
, the sequences
t_{i,n},x_{i,n},y_{i,n}
are fixed, equal to
t_{i},x_{i},y_{i}
, and the prelimiting inequalities in the third bullet point above are replaced with the stronger assumptions that
t_{i}<t_{i+1},y_{i}<x_{i+1}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
. The proof goes through verbatim in the more general setting described above, since the inequalities
t_{i}<t_{i+1},y_{i}<x_{i+1}
are only used to ensure that all of the line segments from

\tilde{\beta}_{n}(t_{i},x_{j})+2\lfloor\ell\log^{2}n\rfloor e_{2}\qquad\text{to}\qquad\tilde{\beta}_{n}(t_{i},y_{j})+2\lfloor\ell\log^{2}n\rfloor e_{2}

are disjoint for any choice of
i,j\in\{1,\dots,k\},\ell\in\{0,\dots,a_{n}\}
. The
+4
and
+8
terms in the inequalities in the third bullet ensure that we do not run into issues from rounding to the nearest
a
-face. We translate
6.1
to a statement that begins to look more like
2.1
.

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
t_{i},x_{i},\phi_{n}
be as in
6.1
. Then there exist random variables
H_{i,j,n},i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,n\in\mathbb{N}
such that we have the following joint convergence in law:

(28)

\begin{split}&(H_{i,j,n}-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n}),H_{i,j,n}-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},y_{j,n}):i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket)\\
\implies&(4\mathcal{A}(t_{i},x_{j}),4\mathcal{A}(t_{i},y_{j}):i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket).\end{split}

Proof.

The Laplace transform convergence in (
27
) implies the following convergence in distribution.

(29)

\begin{split}(\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},y_{j,n})&-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n}):i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,j\in\mathbb{N})\\
&\implies(\mathcal{A}(t_{i},x_{j})-\mathcal{A}(t_{i},y_{j}):i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,j\in\mathbb{N}).\end{split}

We can translate this statement to involve height shifts
H_{i,j,n}
. Indeed, by Skorokhod’s representation theorem we can realize the above distributional convergence as almost sure convergence on some probability space, so that

\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},y_{j,n})-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n})\to\mathcal{A}(t_{i},x_{j})-\mathcal{A}(t_{i},y_{j})

almost surely for all
i=1,\dots,k,j\in\mathbb{N}
. In this coupling, define
H_{i,j,n}=\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i},y_{j})+\mathcal{A}(t_{i},y_{j})
. This realizes (
28
) as almost sure convergence, yielding the result.
∎

6.2.
The proof of
2.1

We can now clearly see the two obstacles to proving
2.1
:

•

Corollary
6.2
requires a specific mollification of increasing size with
n
, which has the effect of eliminating fluctuations from the smooth background.

•

Corollary
6.2
requires a different (and abstractly defined) random height shift for every statistic, whereas
2.1
gives a comparison with the central height.

We deal with both of these by appealing to the smooth phase couplings in Section
5.2
. We first show that the height shift can be taken independently of the coordinate
j
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Consider the setting of Corollary
6.2
, where the averaging parameter
a_{n}=o(n^{1/24}\log^{-2}n)
. In this setting we can replace
H_{i,j,n}
with the height shift
H_{i,n}:=H_{i,1,n}
and the statement still holds.

Proof.

First, by potentially filling in the sequence
x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{k},y_{k}
with new intermediate pairs and associated new approximation sequences, we may assume that

(30)

\displaystyle x_{i}=y_{i-1},\quad\text{ and }\quad\lambda_{1}n^{1/3}x_{i,n}\geq\lambda_{1}n^{1/3}y_{i-1,n}+5.

We claim that with this assumption, any choice of random variables
H_{i,j,n}
satisfying (
28
) will satisfy

H_{i,j,n}-H_{i,j+1,n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}0

as
n\to\infty
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,k-1\rrbracket
. This is equivalent to the statement that

(31)

\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},y_{j,n})-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},x_{j+1,n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}0

for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,k-1\rrbracket
. To prove (
31
), consider the set

\Lambda=\beta_{n}(t_{j,n},y_{i,n})+[-100a_{n}\log^{2}n,100a_{n}\log^{2}n]^{2}.

Since
a_{n}\log^{2}n=o(n^{1/24})
, by
5.5
we can couple the Aztec diamond measure
D_{n}\sim\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
with the smooth phase
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a}
such that
D_{n}|_{\Lambda}=D|_{\Lambda}
with probability
1-o(1)
. Let
\mathcal{H}
denote the height function in
D
, and define the functions
\tilde{\mathcal{H}},\tilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\phi_{n}}
as they were defined for
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Then on the event where
D_{n}|_{\Lambda}=D|_{\Lambda}
, we have that

\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},y_{j,n})-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},x_{j+1,n})=\tilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},y_{j,n})-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},x_{j+1,n}),

so it suffices to show that the right-hand side above converges to
0
in probability with
n
. This follows from the covariance bound
5.10
, the fact that
\phi_{n}
only averages over
a
-faces, and the symmetry in Corollary
4.12
, which implies
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}(f)=0
on any
a
–face
f
.
∎

We can push the smooth phase coupling idea from
6.3
a bit more in order to eliminate the need for the mollifying sequence
\phi_{n}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

In Corollary
6.2
/
6.3
, we can allow
\phi_{n}\subset(2\mathbb{Z})^{2}
to be any sequence of sets of satisfying
|\phi_{n}|\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, and
\max_{x\in\phi_{n}}\|x\|_{\infty}=o(n^{1/24})
. The random variables
H_{i,n}
do not depend on the choice of
\phi_{n}
.

Moreover, under the assumption that

(32)

\inf_{(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}\|\tilde{\beta}_{n}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n})-\tilde{\beta}_{n}(t_{i^{\prime},n},x_{j^{\prime},n})\|_{\infty}\to\infty,

we have the following joint convergence:

(33)

\begin{split}(H_{i,n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}(\tilde{\beta}_{n}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n})+v)&:i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,v\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{2})\\
\implies(4\mathcal{A}(t_{i},x_{j})-\mathcal{H}_{i,j}((1,1)+v)&:i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,v\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}),\end{split}

where
\mathcal{A}
is the Airy surface, and
(\mathcal{H}_{i,j},i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket)
is a collection of height functions drawn from the smooth phase
\mathbb{P}_{a}
, independent of
\mathcal{A}
and of each other.

Remark 6.2
.

The upper bound on the diameter of the mollifying sequence is not optimal, and comes from a technical constraint on the smooth coupling. The machinery we develop in later sections could improve this bound to
\max_{x\in\phi_{n}}\|x\|_{\infty}=o(n^{1/3})
, but we do not pursue this improvement here. Note that the condition that
\phi_{n}\subset(2\mathbb{Z})^{2}
is purely so that we average over only
a
- and
b
-faces. This could easily be loosened, but we would need to account for the fact that the expected height in the smooth phase at other faces is non-zero.

Note also that the assumption (
32
) simply ensures that the heights
\mathcal{H}_{i,j}
are independent in the limit. If there were a pair of points
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
(or multiple pairs) where
\tilde{\beta}_{n}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n})-\tilde{\beta}_{n}(t_{i^{\prime},n},x_{j^{\prime},n})
was converging to a point in
\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, rather than
\infty
, then a version of (
33
) would hold where
\mathcal{H}_{i,j}
and
\mathcal{H}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
were spatial shifts of each other.

To prove
6.4
, we need a simple lemma related to tail-triviality of the smooth phase. For this lemma, for a dimer configuration
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a}
we let
T_{x}D
be the translation of
D
by
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, i.e.

T_{x}(D)=\{e+x:e\in D\}.

The smooth phase
\mathbb{P}_{a}
is invariant under a translation
T_{x}
if and only if
x
is a
c
-face (i.e. if
x=(i,j)
where
i,j\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and
i+j\in 4\mathbb{Z}
).

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
A_{1},\dots,A_{k}
be events in the space of all dimer configurations on
{\mathtt{\tilde{V}}}
(i.e. the space of all perfect matchings of
{\mathtt{\tilde{V}}}
). Let
x_{n,1},\dots,x_{n,k}\in{\mathtt{\tilde{F}}},n\in\mathbb{N}
be
c
-faces, and let
((U_{n,1},\dots,U_{n,k}):n\in\mathbb{N})
be a sequence of disjoint subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
x_{n,i}\in U_{n,i}
for all
i
and

\inf\{\|x_{n,i}-y\|_{\infty}:y\notin U_{n,i}\}\to\infty

with
n
for all
i=1,\dots,k
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{n}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a}
restricted to
V_{n}^{c}
where
V_{n}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}U_{n,i}
. Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{a}(D\in T_{x_{n,i}}A_{i},i=1,\dots,k\;\mid\;\mathcal{F}_{n})=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}(D\in A_{i}),

where the convergence holds in
L^{1}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction, with the
n=0
base case being tautological. Now suppose that the limiting claim holds for some
n=0,1,\dots
. By translation invariance of
\mathbb{P}_{a}
we have

\mathbb{P}_{a}(D\in T_{x_{n,i}}A_{i},i=1,\dots,n\mid\mathcal{F}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbb{P}_{a}(D\in T_{x_{n,i}-x_{n,1}}A_{i},i=1,\dots,n\mid\mathcal{F}_{n}^{*}),

where
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{*}
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
D
restricted to
T_{-x_{n,1}}V_{n}
. Therefore it suffices to prove the claim when
x_{n,1}=(0,0)
. Now, let
\mathcal{G}_{n}\supset\mathcal{F}_{n}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
D|_{U_{n,1}^{c}}
together with the events
T_{x_{n,i}}A_{i},i=2,\dots,n
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{a}(D\in T_{x_{n,i}}A_{i},i=1,\dots,n\mid\mathcal{F}_{n})

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{P}_{a}(D\in A_{1}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n})\mathbf{1}(D\in T_{x_{n,i}}A_{i},i=2,\dots,n)\mid\mathcal{F}_{n})

where the equality uses the tower property of conditional expectation. Now,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{a}(D\in A_{1}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n})=\mathbb{P}_{a}(D\in A_{1})

in probability by Kolmogorov’s
0
-
1
law. (To apply Kolmogorov’s
0
-
1
law we can use Wilson’s algorithm to construct the smooth phase
D
as a function of an IID sequence of random variables. In this construction, any limit points of the left-hand side above are measurable with respect to the tail
\sigma
-algebra). Moreover, by the inductive hypothesis,

\mathbb{P}(T_{x_{n,i}}D\in A_{i},i=2,\dots,n)\mid\mathcal{F}_{n})=\prod_{i=2}^{n}\mathbb{P}(D\in A_{i})

in probability. Combining the previous three displays yields the result.
∎

Proof of
6.4
.

Define

\psi_{n}=\{2\lfloor\ell\log^{2}n\rfloor e_{2}:\ell\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor n^{1/100}\rfloor\}\},

and let
\phi_{n}
be an arbitrary mollifying sequence satisfying the conditions of the corollary.
Let
b_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
be any sequence with
\phi_{n}\cup\psi_{n}\subset[-b_{n},b_{n}]^{2}
and
b_{n}=o(n^{1/24})
. Let

U_{n}={\mathtt{\tilde{V}}}\cap\bigcup_{i,j=1}^{k}([-b_{n},b_{n}]^{2}+u_{i,j}),

where
u_{i,j}:=\tilde{\beta}_{n}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n})
.
Then
|U_{n}|\leq 4k^{2}b_{n}^{2}
, and there exists
L>0
such that for all
n
, we have
U_{n}\subset\beta_{n}([-Ln^{2/3},Ln^{2/3}\times[-Ln^{1/3},Ln^{1/3}])
. Therefore by
5.5
, we can couple the two-periodic Aztec diamond
D_{n}\sim\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
and the smooth phase
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a}
so that

(34)

\mathbb{P}(D|_{U_{n}}=D_{n}|_{U_{n}})\geq 1-Cn^{-1/3}k^{8}b_{n}^{8}\geq 1-o(1).

Let
\mathcal{H}
denote the height function in
D
. On the event where
D|_{U_{n}}=D_{n}|_{U_{n}}
,

(35)

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}^{\psi_{n}}(u_{i,j})-\mathcal{H}^{\phi_{n}}(u_{i,j})&=\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\psi_{n}}(u_{i,j})-\mathcal{H}^{\phi_{n}}_{n}(u_{i,j}).\end{split}

Now, arguing as in the proof of
6.3
, as
n\to\infty
we have that

(36)

\mathcal{H}^{\psi_{n}}(u_{i,j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}0,\qquad\mathcal{H}^{\phi_{n}}(u_{i,j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}0.

Putting this together with (
34
), (
35
), and
6.3
for the mollifiers
\psi_{n}
then gives

(H_{i,n}-\mathcal{H}^{\phi_{n}}(u_{i,j}):i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket)\implies(\mathcal{A}(t_{i},x_{j}):i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket).

where the random variables
H_{i,n}
are the same as in (
28
). This is the first claim in the lemma.

We move on to the display (
33
). We will work in a version of the coupling above, but with

b_{n}=\min\Big(n^{1/100},\inf_{(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}\|\tilde{\beta}_{n}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n})-\tilde{\beta}_{n}(t_{i^{\prime},n},x_{j^{\prime},n})\|_{\infty}/3\Big).

This ensures that the sets
u_{i,j}+[-b_{n},b_{n}]^{2},i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
are disjoint. We also set

\phi_{n}:=(2\mathbb{Z})^{2}\cap([-b_{n},b_{n}]^{2}\setminus[-b_{n}/2,b_{n}/2]^{2}).

The bound (
34
) still holds here, as does the second convergence in (
36
) and on the event where
D|_{U_{n}}=D_{n}|_{U_{n}}
, for any
i,j
and any point
v\in u_{i,j}+[-b_{n},b_{n}]^{2}
we have

\mathcal{H}(v)-\mathcal{H}^{\phi_{n}}(u_{i,j})=\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)-\mathcal{H}^{\phi_{n}}_{n}(u_{i,j}).

By this equality and the first part of the lemma, to prove (
33
) it suffices to show that

(37)

(H_{i,n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(u_{i,j});\mathcal{H}(u_{i,j}+v))\implies(4\mathcal{A}(t_{i},x_{j});\mathcal{H}_{i,j}((1,1)+v)),

where the convergence in law is joint over all
i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and all
v\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.
To prove (
37
), define the set

V_{n}={\mathtt{V}}\cap\bigcup_{i,j=1}^{k}([-b_{n}/2,b_{n}/2]^{2}+u_{i,j}).

On the event in (
34
), given
D|_{V_{n}^{c}}
and
D_{n}|_{V_{n}^{c}}
, resample the dimer configuration
D|_{V_{n}}=D_{n}|_{V_{n}}
according to the
2
-periodic weights to give a new configuration
D^{\prime}|_{V_{n}}=D_{n}^{\prime}|_{V_{n}}
. Extend
D^{\prime},D_{n}^{\prime}
to equal
D,D_{n}
on
V_{n}^{c}
, and let
\mathcal{J}
,
\mathcal{J}_{n}
denote the height functions in these configurations on the full plane and the Aztec diamond, respectively. By the Gibbs property for the dimer laws
\mathbb{P}_{a},\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
, we have that
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{J}_{n})
so it suffices to show that (
37
) holds for the pair
(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{J}_{n})
.
Observe that by the definition of
V_{n},\phi_{n}
, we have
\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n})=\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n})
for all
i,j
. Therefore to prove (
37
) it suffices to show that conditional on
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}
(and the
H_{i,n}
, which can be taken as measurable functions of
H_{n}^{\phi_{n}}
) we have

(38)

(\mathcal{J}(u_{i,j}+v))_{i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,v\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\implies(\mathcal{H}_{i,j}((1,1)+v))_{i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,v\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}.

For this, observe that if we let
\mathcal{F}_{n}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
D|_{V_{n}^{c}}
and let
\mathcal{G}_{n}
be
\sigma
-algebra generated by both
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}
we have

\mathbb{P}(D^{\prime}\in\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{n})=\mathbb{P}(D^{\prime}\in\cdot\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}).

Now, the sets
V_{n}
fit the assumptions of
6.5
since
b_{n}\to\infty
with
n
.
Therefore by that lemma, for any sets
A_{i,j},i,j=1,\dots,k
we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{J}(u_{i,j}+\cdot)\in A_{i,j}:i,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\mid\mathcal{F}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}\prod_{i,j=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{J}((1,1)+\cdot)\in A_{i,j}).

Combining the previous two displays gives (
38
).
∎

Next, we recall the definition of the central height. Using the mollifier notation introduced above and letting
\zeta_{n}
be the set of
all
faces with
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq n^{3/4}
, the central height
H_{n}
is the unique integer in the interval

(\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\zeta_{n}}(0,0)-1/2,\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\zeta_{n}}(0,0)+1/2].

To complete the proof of
2.1
, we need to show that all of the height shifts
H_{i,n}
can be replaced by
H_{n}
. Up to a coordinate change, this will immediately imply the theorem.

Lemma 6.6
.

In the setting of
6.4
, we can take
H_{i,n}=H_{n}
for all
i
, and the lemma still holds.

To prove
6.6
, we require a strengthening of
6.1
in the
k=\ell=1
case that allows the endpoint
y_{1}
to tend to
\infty
with
n
. The proof in this setting is a similar to the proof of
6.1
in
[
BCJ18
]
with a few minor tweaks, and is included in
Section

13
.

Theorem 6.7
.

Suppose we have:

•

Sequences
t_{n}\to t
and
x_{n}\leq y_{n}=\log^{3}n
with
x_{n}\to x\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
.

•

Mollifiers
\phi_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
of the form

\phi_{n}=\{2\lfloor k\log^{2}n\rfloor e_{2}:k\in\{0,\dots,a_{n}\}\},

where the sequence
a_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and satisfies
a_{n}=o(n^{1/3}\log^{-2}n)
as
n\to\infty
.

Then there exists a constant
\varepsilon>0
such that for any
\lambda\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
we have

(39)

\begin{split}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\exp\left(\lambda[\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{n},y_{n})-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{n},x_{n})]\right)=\mathbb{E}\exp\left(4\lambda\mathcal{A}(x,t)\right),\end{split}

where if
x=\infty
, we set
\mathcal{A}(x,t)=0
.

Proof of
6.6
.

First, in the setting of
6.4
, we can extend the collection of points
x_{i},i=1,\dots,k
and sequences
x_{i,n}
to infinite collections
x_{i},x_{i,n},i,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
x_{i}\to\infty
with
n
. The convergences in law in the lemma continue to hold by Kolmogorov’s extension theorem. We claim that in this setting, any sequence of heights
H_{i,n}
realizing the convergence

(40)

(H_{i,n}-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\psi_{n}}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n}):j\in\mathbb{N})\implies(4\mathcal{A}(t_{i},x_{j}):j\in\mathbb{N})

with the explicit choice of

\psi_{n}=\{2\lfloor k\log^{2}n\rfloor e_{2}:k\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor n^{1/100}\rfloor\}\},

must satisfy

(41)

|H_{i,n}-H_{n}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}0

as
n\to\infty
, which yields the desired result. To prove (
41
), first note that for any fixed
i
, we have
\mathcal{A}(t_{i},x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}0
as
x\to\infty
, since the Airy point process has a largest point almost surely. Therefore

\lim_{j\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}H_{i,n}-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\psi_{n}}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n})=0,

where the convergence is in probability.
Similarly, by
6.7
, letting
y_{n}=\log^{3}n
as in that theorem, we have that in probability,

\lim_{j\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\psi_{n}}(t_{j,n},y_{n})-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\psi_{n}}(t_{i,n},x_{j,n})=0.

Subtracting together these two equations gives that in probability,

\lim_{j\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\psi_{n}}(t_{j,n},y_{n})-H_{i,n}=0.

Given this, to prove the lemma we just need to show that

(42)

\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\psi_{n}}(t_{j,n},y_{n})-H_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}0.

Recall the sets
\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1},\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
defined in the global smooth phase couplings,
5.6
and
5.7
. Then by applying these two lemmas, with probability

1-o(1)
we can couple our dimer configuration
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
with two other dimer configurations
D_{1},D_{2}\sim\mathbb{P}_{a}
such that

(43)

D|_{\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},i}}=D_{i}|_{\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},}i},\qquad\text{ for }i=1,2.

Now, let
f_{1}
be an
a
-face chosen so that
f_{1}+\psi_{n}\subset\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}\cap\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},2}
, and let
f_{2}=\tilde{\beta}_{n}(t_{j,n},y_{n})
. Since
y_{n}\to\infty
much faster than
\log^{2}n
, we have that
f_{2}+\psi_{n}\subset\Lambda_{\operatorname{sm},1}
. Then letting
h_{i}
be the height function for
D_{i}
, in the above coupling we have

(44)

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}^{\psi_{n}}_{n}(f_{2})-\mathcal{H}^{\psi_{n}}_{n}(f_{1})&=h_{2}^{\psi_{n}}(f_{2})-h_{2}^{\psi_{n}}(f_{1}),\\
\mathcal{H}^{\psi_{n}}_{n}(f_{1})-\mathcal{H}^{\zeta_{n}}_{n}(0,0)&=h_{1}^{\psi_{n}}(f_{2})-h_{1}^{\zeta_{n}}(0,0).\end{split}

Here
\zeta_{n}
is the set of all faces with
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq n^{3/4}
.
Then arguing as in (
36
), all four terms on right-hand side above tend to
0
with
n
in probability. To handle the averaging in
h_{1}^{\zeta_{n}}(0,0)
, which also includes faces that are not
a
- or
b
-faces, we use the reflection symmetry
\mathbb{E}h_{1}(x,y)=-\mathbb{E}h_{1}(1-x,1+y)
from Corollary
4.12
to get that
\mathbb{E}h_{1}^{\zeta_{n}}(0,0)=O(n^{-3/4})
. Therefore putting together the above discussion and the displays (
43
), (
44
), we have that

\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\psi_{n}}(f_{2})-\mathcal{H}^{\zeta_{n}}_{n}(0,0)=\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\psi_{n}}(t_{j,n},y_{n})-\mathcal{H}^{\zeta_{n}}_{n}(0,0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}0.

To complete the proof, observe that the height difference
\mathcal{H}^{\zeta_{n}}_{n}(0,0)-h^{\zeta_{n}}_{n}(0,0)
is always an integer. Since
h^{\zeta_{n}}_{n}(0,0)
converges to
0
in probability, this implies that
\mathcal{H}^{\zeta_{n}}_{n}(0,0)-H_{n}
also converges to
0
in probability, since
H_{n}
is the nearest integer to
\mathcal{H}^{\zeta_{n}}_{n}(0,0)
. Putting this together with the previous display gives (
42
).
∎

2.1
is almost immediate from the previous few lemmas, and a minor change of coordinates. We restate a stronger version of that theorem here, together with the variants discussed in Remark
2.3
. Note that the upcoming
6.8
(along with all statements in the body of the paper) is stated in terms of coordinates centered around the rough-smooth limit curve whereas
2.1
is set up using the second order Taylor approximation to the limit curve. The two versions are equivalent since the limit curve is smooth in a neighbourhood
\xi_{0}(0)
and the lower order Taylor terms are negligible in our scaling.

First, define the scaling transformation
\gamma_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
given by

(45)

\gamma_{n}(t,x)=n\xi_{0}(t/n)+(x,0),

and let

\hat{\gamma}_{n}(t,x)=[\gamma_{n}(2^{1/3}\lambda_{2}n^{2/3}t,2^{5/3}\lambda_{1}n^{1/3}x)]_{a}-(1,1).

Theorem 6.8
.

Consider
k
(potentially equal) points
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and sequences
u_{i,n}\to u_{i},n\to\infty
. Then for any mollifying sequence
\phi_{n}\subset(2\mathbb{Z})^{2}
satisfying
|\phi_{n}|\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and
\max_{x\in\phi_{n}}\|x\|_{\infty}=o(n^{1/24})
, we have that

(46)

(\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(\hat{\gamma}(u_{i,n}))-H_{n}:i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket)\implies(-4\mathcal{A}(u_{i}):i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket).

Moreover, under the additional assumption that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\inf_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\|\hat{\gamma}_{n}(u_{i,n})-\hat{\gamma}_{n}(u_{j,n})\|_{\infty}=\infty,

we have the convergence

(47)

\begin{split}&(\mathcal{H}_{n}(\hat{\gamma}_{n}(u_{n,i})+v)-H_{n}:i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,v\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{2})\\
\implies&(\mathcal{H}^{i}(v)-4\mathcal{A}(u_{i}):i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}),\end{split}

where
\mathcal{A}
is the Airy surface, and the random fields
\mathcal{H}^{i},i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
are i.i.d. copies of the height function for
\mathbb{P}_{a}
, independent of
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

If we replace
\hat{\gamma}_{n}
with
\tilde{\beta}_{n}
, then
6.8
is
6.4
together with
6.6
. There are minor notational differences between the statements. To move from the coordinates
\tilde{\beta}_{n}
to
\hat{\gamma}_{n}
, we can simply observe that

\hat{\gamma}_{n}(t,x)=\tilde{\beta}_{n}(\zeta_{n}(t,x)),

where the change-of-coordinate function
\zeta_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfies

\max_{u\in K}\|\zeta_{n}(u)-u\|_{\infty}=O(n^{-1/3})

for every compact set
K
. Since the theorem statement for
\tilde{\beta}_{n}
allows for arbitrary sequences converging to the points
u_{i}
, pre-composing
\tilde{\beta}_{n}
with
\zeta_{n}
does not affect convergence statements.
∎

7.
Global path control

In this section, we establish global control of backbone path locations, culminating in a stronger version of
3.2
. In particular, we will show that there is a
last path
passing through the rough-smooth boundary, which will be our candidate path for convergence to the Airy process. We start by recalling some definitions from earlier sections and stating a slightly more detailed version of
3.2
.

Let
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
be a sample from the two-periodic Aztec diamond with parameter
a
, let
\mathcal{H}_{n}
be its height function, let
F_{\mathtt{S}}(D),F_{\mathtt{N}}(D)
be the south and north Temperleyan forests associated to
D
and let
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\pm},\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm}.i=1,\dots,n/2
be the north and south backbone paths in these forests. Here recall that the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-},\mathcal{S}_{i}^{+}
start at the source vertices
v^{S}_{i},v^{N}_{i}
respectively, and the north backbone paths
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{i}^{+}
start at the boundary vertices
w^{S}_{i},w^{N}_{i}
, respectively. We think of these paths as plane curves, formed by connecting adjacent vertices with line segments, and parametrizing the resulting paths by arc-length, starting at the source vertex. For example, we will write
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}:[0,t_{i}^{-}]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
where
t_{i}^{-}
is the length of
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
,
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}(0)=v^{S}_{i}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}(t_{i}^{-})\in\partial_{E}{\mathtt{S}}\cup\partial_{W}{\mathtt{S}}
.

Since the south and north paths cannot cross each other, these backbone paths interlace, with the order of the interlacing determined by the start point order, as in
4.3
. For example, moving left-to-right, we see the following backbone paths starting on the south boundary:

\mathcal{S}_{1}^{-}\prec\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}\prec\mathcal{S}_{2}^{-}\prec\cdots\prec\mathcal{S}_{n/2}^{-}\prec\mathcal{N}_{n/2}^{-}.

We let
I=I_{n}\in\{0,\dots,n/2\}
be the split point described in
4.2
, so that for
j\leq I
, the path
\mathcal{S}_{j}^{-}
ends on the west boundary
\partial_{W}{\mathtt{S}}
, and for
j>I
, the path
\mathcal{S}_{j}^{-}
ends on the east boundary
