\partial_{E}{\mathtt{S}}
. By the interlacing above (alternately, by
4.2
), the paths
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{-},i<I
end on the west boundary and the paths
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{-},i>I
end on the east boundary. The path
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
may end on the east or west boundary.

Next, recall that the transformation
\beta_{n}
maps the point
n\xi_{0}(0)
where the rough-smooth boundary intersects the line
\{x=y\}
to
(0,0)
. This transformation also flatten outs and rotates the rough-smooth boundary (without scaling) so that it becomes a horizontal line. Define the regions

\displaystyle{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}

\displaystyle:=\beta_{n}([-2n^{3/4},2n^{3/4}]\times[-n^{1/2}\log^{3/2}n,2n^{1/3}\log^{2}n]),

\displaystyle{\mathtt{PRS}}_{n}^{*}

\displaystyle:=\beta_{n}([-2n^{3/4},2n^{3/4}]\times[-n^{1/2}\log^{2}n,n^{3/4}]),

which surround the rough-smooth boundary, together with the set
and the set
{\mathtt{X}}=\{{\bf v}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:v_{1}=\pm v_{2}\}
.

Finally, recall the event
\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{S}}(\alpha)
from
5.9
, which constrains south forest paths to parabolas away from the south backbone. We define
\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{N}}(\alpha)
as the analogous parabolic constraint event for north forest paths (with north-opening parabolas). More precisely, we let
\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{N}}(\alpha)
be the set of DCFs
F
on
{\mathtt{G(N)}}
such that
\rho_{N}F\in\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{S}}(\alpha)
, where
\rho_{N}(x,y)=(-x,-y)
, as in
4.1
.

Theorem 7.1
.

With probability
1-o(1)
as
n\to\infty
, the following events hold for
D_{n}\sim\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
and the associated Temperleyan forests:

1.

|H_{n}|\leq\log n
.

2.

The events
\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{S}}(\log^{2}n),\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{N}}(\log^{2}n)
hold.

3.

The paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
with
I-n^{1/4}\leq i\leq I
do not intersect the region
({\mathtt{X}}\cup{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n})\setminus{\mathtt{RS}}^{*}_{n}
.

4.

The paths
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{-},i\geq I
and
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{+},i\in\llbracket 1,n/2\rrbracket
do not enter the region
{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}
.

5.

The paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-},i\leq I-n^{1/4}\log^{4}n
do not intersect the region
{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}
, and the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
does not enter the region
{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}
prior to coalescing with another path.

6.

H_{n}=4I-n-1
.

Note that the proof of
7.1
will actually yield bounds on slightly larger regions, which we will define as we move through this section. We start with a simple lemma that converts height estimates from Section
5.4
to bounds on path locations. For this lemma, define three overlapping regions:

\displaystyle{\mathtt{A}}_{SW}

\displaystyle:=\beta_{n}([-n^{5/6},n^{5/6}]\times[n^{1/3}\log^{2}n,n(|\xi_{0}(0)|+\gamma_{a}))

\displaystyle{\mathtt{B}}_{SW}

\displaystyle:=\beta_{n}([-n^{5/6},n^{5/6}]\times[-n^{1/2}\log^{2}n,2n^{1/3}\log^{2}n])

\displaystyle{\mathtt{C}}_{SW}

\displaystyle:={\mathtt{C1}}_{SW}\cup{\mathtt{C2}}_{SW},\quad\text{ where }

\displaystyle{\mathtt{C1}}_{SW}

\displaystyle:=\beta_{n}([-n^{5/6},n^{5/6}]\times[-n^{1/2}\log^{2}n/2,-n^{1/2}\log^{3/2}n])

\displaystyle{\mathtt{C2}}_{SW}

\displaystyle:=\beta_{n}(\{0\}\times(-\infty,-n^{1/2}\log^{2}n]).

Here
\gamma_{a}
is as in
5.6
.
For
S\in\{{\mathtt{A}},{\mathtt{B}},{\mathtt{C}},{\mathtt{C1}},{\mathtt{C2}}\}
, we also define

\displaystyle S_{NW}

\displaystyle=R_{-\pi/2}S_{SW},\qquad S_{SE}=R_{\pi/2}S_{SW},\qquad S_{NE}=R_{\pi}S_{SW},

\displaystyle\qquad S_{\times}

\displaystyle=S_{NW}\cup S_{SW}\cup S_{SE}\cup S_{NE}.

where here recall that
R_{\theta}
denotes a counterclockwise rotation by
\theta
. See
Figure

16
for an illustration of these regions.

Figure 16
.
Some of the important forbidden sets in
7.2
.

Lemma 7.2
.

The following events hold with probability
1-o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.

1.

The paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm},\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\pm},i\geq n/4+n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
do not enter the region

{\mathtt{D^{-}}}:={\mathtt{C}}_{NE}\cup{\mathtt{C}}_{SW}\cup{\mathtt{B}}_{NE}\cup{\mathtt{B}}_{SW}\cup{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}.

Similarly, the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm},\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\pm},i\leq n/4-n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
do not enter
{\mathtt{D}}^{+}:={\mathtt{C}}_{NW}\cup{\mathtt{C}}_{SE}\cup{\mathtt{B}}_{NW}\cup{\mathtt{B}}_{SE}\cup{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}
.

2.

The paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm},\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\pm},|i-n/4|\leq 2n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
do not enter the set
{\mathtt{C}}_{\times}
.

3.

The paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm},\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\pm},|i-n/4|\geq n^{1/4}\log^{4}n/2
do not enter the set
{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}\cup{\mathtt{B}}_{\times}
.

4.

The split point
I_{n}
satisfies
I_{n}-n/4\in[-n^{1/4}\log^{2}n-1,n^{1/4}\log^{2}n]
.

Proof.

We first translate the height bounds from Corollaries
5.11
and
5.13
. These corollaries imply that with probability
1-o(1)
, we have that

•

|\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)|\leq\log n
for all
v\in{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}
by Corollary
5.11
.

•

\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)\leq n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
for all
v\in{\mathtt{B}}_{SW}\cup{\mathtt{B}}_{NE}
and
\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)\geq-n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
for all
v\in{\mathtt{B}}_{SE}\cup{\mathtt{B}}_{NW}
by the first estimate in Corollary
5.13
.

•

|\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)|\leq n^{1/4}\log^{4}n
for all
v\in{\mathtt{B}}_{\times}
by the first estimate in Corollary
5.13
.

•

\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)\leq-100n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
for all
v\in{\mathtt{C}}_{SW}\cup{\mathtt{C}}_{NE}
and
\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)\geq 100n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
for all
v\in{\mathtt{C}}_{SE}\cup{\mathtt{C}}_{NW}
. This uses the first estimate in Corollary
5.13
on the
{\mathtt{C1}}
-pieces and the second estimate on the
{\mathtt{C2}}
-pieces.

On the event where these four bounds hold, we will show that the four points in the lemma hold deterministically.

For part
1
, by Remark
4.1
, the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm},\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\pm},i\geq n/4+n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
all start at vertices of height at least
4n^{1/4}\log^{2}n+O(1)
. They can only change their height by winding (
4.5
). On the other hand, the set
{\mathtt{D}}^{-}
is connected to the boundary of the Aztec diamond, so the first time any of these paths enters this set, its height would have to be within
4
of its initial height. Therefore by the second and third bullets above, none of these paths can enter
{\mathtt{D}}^{-}
. The second piece of part
1
follows similarly, as does part
2
.

For part
3
, observe that the sets
{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}\cup{\mathtt{B}}_{\times}\cup{\mathtt{D}}^{-}
and
{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}\cup{\mathtt{B}}_{\times}\cup{\mathtt{D}}^{+}
are connected to the boundary of the Aztec diamond, and by the above bullet points, with probability
1-o(1)
the heights on these sets are bounded above by
n^{1/4}\log^{4}n/100
(for the first set) and below by
-n^{1/4}\log^{4}n/100
(for the second set). Therefore by a similar argument as for parts
1
and
2
, any path whose initial height has absolute value at least
n^{1/4}\log^{4}n/99
cannot enter at least one of these sets, and so it cannot enter
{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}\cup{\mathtt{B}}_{\times}
. Part
3
then follows by translating path indices to heights via Remark
4.1
.

For part
4
, observe that
{\mathtt{D}}^{-}
cuts off the south and west boundaries of the Aztec diamond. Therefore by second part
1
, we have
I_{n}-n/4\leq n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
. Similarly, the set
{\mathtt{D}}^{+}
cuts off the south and east boundaries of the Aztec diamond, and so
I_{n}-n/4\geq-n^{1/4}\log^{2}n-1
.
∎

To move beyond the height restrictions in
7.2
to get the full suite of restrictions in
7.1
, we will need to control the possibility that paths backtrack or create spirals, which will change their height via
4.5
. This is a particular challenge when paths do this in a nested fashion, creating a kind of
onion
. We can control the presence of onions by using the parabolic path estimates from the
\mathsf{Para}
events defined in
5.9
. The next lemma gives a precise statement.

For this lemma, for
r_{1},r_{2}>0
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
define

\displaystyle B_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}+[-r_{1},r_{1}]\times[-r_{2},r_{2}],\qquad

\displaystyle\partial^{\pm}B_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}+\{\pm r_{1}\}\times[-r_{2},r_{2}].

Lemma 7.3
.

For
r_{1},r_{2}\geq 1
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
let

\mathsf{Onion}(r_{1},r_{2},\ell)

be the event where there exists a path
\mathcal{C}_{i_{0}}^{*}:[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}
for some
i_{0}\in\{1,\dots,n/2\}
,
*\in\{+,-\},\mathcal{C}\in\{\mathcal{N},\mathcal{S}\}
and path segments
\pi_{1}=\mathcal{C}_{i_{0}}^{*}|_{[s_{1},t_{1}]},\pi_{2}=\mathcal{C}_{i_{0}}^{*}|_{[s_{2},t_{2}]}
for some
t_{1}\leq s_{2}
satisfying the following two conditions:

•

There exists
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\subset B_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathbf{v})
.

•

The points
\pi_{1}(s_{1}),\pi_{2}(t_{2})
sit on the same boundary
\partial^{\pm}B_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathbf{v})
and the points
\pi_{1}(s_{2}),\pi_{2}(t_{1})
sit on the opposite boundary
\partial^{\mp}B_{r,R}(\mathbf{v})
.

•

Let

M\subset B_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathbf{v})
be the union of
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
and the region in
B_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathbf{v})
bounded between these two paths. Then there are at most
\ell
paths of the form
\mathcal{S}_{j}^{\pm}
or
\mathcal{N}_{j}^{\pm}
that enter
M
.

Informally, the event
\mathsf{Onion}(r_{1},r_{2},\ell)
says that there exists an
onion
of width
r_{1}
, height
r_{2}
, and
\ell
layers.
Then assuming that
r_{2}\geq 1
and
r_{1}\geq 10\ell\sqrt{r_{2}}\log^{2}n
, we have

(48)

\mathsf{Onion}(r_{1},r_{2},\ell)\cap\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{S}}(\log^{2}n)\cap\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{N}}(\log^{2}n)=\emptyset.

See
Figure

17
for an illustration of
7.3
, and a sketch of the proof.

Figure 17
.
An example of the event
\mathsf{Onion}(r_{1},r_{2},3)
. If
\ell,r_{2}
are too small relative to the width
r_{1}
, then in any onion configuration, we can find a north or south vertex
w
from which loop-erased random walk paths can only join the backbone by exiting the parabolic set
w+P^{\log^{2}n}_{\cdot}
. Two such vertices (one north vertex, one south vertex), are shown in the above figure.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we assume we are on the event defined by the left-hand side of (
48
). Working from this starting point, we will derive the inequality between
r_{1},r_{2}
, and
\ell
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that the path
\mathcal{C}_{i_{0}}^{*}
realizing the event
\mathsf{Onion}(r_{1},r_{2},\ell)
is of the form
\mathcal{N}_{i_{0}}^{-}:[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}
, and that
\pi_{1}(s_{1}),\pi_{2}(t_{2})\in\partial^{+}B_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathbf{v})
. The other cases have symmetric arguments. We may also assume
r_{1},r_{2}\leq n
since the Aztec diamond has height and width
2n
. This is the setting of
Figure

17
.

We proceed by induction on
\ell
, starting with the base case when
\ell=1
. In this case, the only
\mathcal{S}
- or
\mathcal{N}
-path that enters the sets
M
is the path
\mathcal{N}_{i_{0}}^{-}
. In particular, no
\mathcal{N}
-path enters the set
M
. Now consider a vertex
\mathbf{w}\in{\mathtt{S}}
that lies in the set
M
, and satisfies
|w_{1}-v_{1}|\leq 2
. The south forest path starting from
\mathbf{w}
must exit
M
at a vertex
\mathbf{u}
on the boundary set
\mathbf{v}+\{-r_{1},r_{1}\}\times[-r_{2},r_{2}]
. On the other hand, since we are on the event
\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{S}}(\log^{2}n)
, the exit point must satisfy

\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{w}+P^{\log^{2}n}_{\mathtt{S}},

which implies that

r_{1}\leq|u_{1}-w_{1}|+2\leq(\log^{2}n+\log(|u_{2}-w_{2}|+1))\sqrt{|u_{2}-w_{2}|+1}+2.

Using the bounds
|u_{2}-w_{2}|\leq 2r_{2}
,
1\leq r_{2}\leq n
, and simplifying gives

(49)

r_{1}<10\log^{2}n\sqrt{r_{2}},

which implies the lemma for
\ell=1
.

Now, let
r_{2}\geq\ell\geq 2
and assume that the lemma holds for
\ell-1
. Consider the path
\mathcal{N}_{i_{0}}^{-}:[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}
. Let
[a_{1},b_{1}],[a_{2},b_{2}],\dots,[a_{k},b_{k}]\subset[0,t]
be an enumeration of all the intervals for which

\mathcal{N}_{i_{0}}^{-}|_{[a_{i},b_{i}]}\subset B_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathbf{v}),

and the points
\mathcal{N}_{i_{0}}^{-}(a_{i}),\mathcal{N}_{i_{0}}^{-}(b_{i})
lie on opposite boundaries
\partial^{\pm}B_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathbf{v})
and
\partial^{\mp}B_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathbf{v})
. In other words, we have enumerated all left-to-right and right-to-left crossings of
[-r_{1},r_{1}]\times[-r_{2},r_{2}]
. Since we know
\mathcal{S}_{i_{0}}^{-}
has at least one left-to-right crossing and one right-to-left crossing of this box, we can find an index
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
such that the intervals
[a_{i},b_{i}]
and
[a_{i+1},b_{i+1}]
represent opposite types of crossings. We may take these two intervals as the intervals
[s_{1},t_{1}]
and
[s_{2},t_{2}]
realizing the event
{\mathtt{Onion}}(r_{1},r_{2},\ell)
.

Now, since the crossings are consecutive and we have assumed that
\pi_{1}(s_{1}),\pi_{2}(t_{2})\in\partial^{+}B_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathbf{v})
, each of the
\ell-1
other
(\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{N})
-paths that enter the region
M
must enter through the boundary
\partial^{+}B_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathbf{v})
, as in
Figure

17
. At this point, there are two cases:

•

At least one of these paths entering
M
also enters the smaller box

B:=\mathbf{v}+(r_{1}-20\log^{2}n\sqrt{r_{2}},r_{1}]\times[-r_{2},r_{2}].

In this case, this path crosses from left-to-right and then back from right-to-left across the set
M\setminus B
. Let
M^{\prime}\subset M\setminus B
be the region between these crossings. By the ordering/disjointness of the north and south backbone paths,
\mathcal{S}_{i_{0}}^{-}
does not enter
M^{\prime}
. Therefore the set
M^{\prime}
realizes the event
{\mathtt{Onion}}(r_{1}-10\log^{2}n\sqrt{r_{2}},r_{2},\ell-1)
, which implies the inequality in the lemma by the inductive hypothesis.

•

No paths enter the set
M\cap B
. In this case, the set
M\cap B
and the path
\mathcal{S}_{i_{0}}^{-}
realizes the event
{\mathtt{Onion}}(10\log^{2}n\sqrt{r_{2}}-\varepsilon,r_{2},1)
for every
\varepsilon>0
, contradicting (
49
). ∎

We can combine the previous two lemmas to upgrade the path control in Part (1) of
7.2
to include all paths above (or below) the split point
I=I_{n}
. This will imply Part (3) of
7.1
. The caveat is that we need to slightly shrink the sets
{\mathtt{D}}^{\pm}
. For this next lemma, define

\displaystyle{\mathtt{D}}^{+}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{v\in{\mathtt{D}}^{+}:|v_{1}+v_{2}|\wedge|v_{1}-v_{2}|\leq n^{5/6}/2\}\setminus({\mathtt{B}}_{SW}\cup{\mathtt{B}}_{NE}),

\displaystyle{\mathtt{D}}^{-}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{v\in{\mathtt{D}}^{-}:|v_{1}+v_{2}|\wedge|v_{1}-v_{2}|\leq n^{5/6}/2\}\setminus({\mathtt{B}}_{SE}\cup{\mathtt{B}}_{NW}).

Lemma 7.4
.

With probability
1-o(1)
, the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-},i\leq I
do not enter
{\mathtt{D}}^{+}_{0}
. Similarly, the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-},i\geq I+1,\mathcal{N}_{i}^{+},i\geq I+1
do not enter
{\mathtt{D}}^{-}_{0}
, and the paths
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{+},i\leq I
do not enter
{\mathtt{D}}^{+}_{0}
.

Proof.

For the proof, we first define an event
E
of probability
1-o(1)
, and then show that the lemma holds deterministically on
E
. We will only verify the lemma for the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-},i\leq I
, as the claim for the other three sets of paths follows by a symmetric argument.

First, let
E_{1}
be the probability
(1-o(1))
-event where the four points in
7.2
hold. Next, let
E_{2}
be the event where for any path segment
\pi
in the south forest
F_{\mathtt{S}}(D)
started at a vertex
v
and with
\pi\subset{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}
, we have

(50)

\pi\subset P_{\mathtt{S}}^{\log^{2}n},

and similarly for any path segment
\gamma
in the north forest
F_{\mathtt{N}}(D)
started at a vertex
v
and with
\gamma\subset{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}
, we have
\gamma\subset P_{\mathtt{N}}^{\log^{2}n}
. The event
E_{2}
has probability
1-o(1)
in the smooth phase by the standard estimate in
5.8
and a union bound. Therefore it also has probability
1-o(1)
in the Aztec diamond by the smooth phase couplings,
5.6
,
5.7
.
We now define

E=E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{S}}(\log^{2}n)\cap\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{N}}(\log^{2}n),

which has probability
1-o(1)
by
5.9
,
7.2
, and the above discussion regarding
E_{2}
. In the remainder of the proof, we work on
E
. We will only prove the claim for
{\mathtt{D}}^{+}_{0}
, as the proof for
{\mathtt{D}}^{-}_{0}
is symmetric. For the sake of contradiction, suppose that one of the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-},i\leq I
enters the set
{\mathtt{D}}^{+}_{0}
. By path ordering, the rightmost path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
must then also enter
{\mathtt{D}}^{+}_{0}
.

Figure 18
.
An illustration of the first part of the proof of
7.4
. For the the path
{\mathtt{S}}_{I}^{-}
to cross over the line
L_{2}
, it must either cross through the sets
{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}
, or else use one of the corridors between
{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}
and
{\mathtt{C}}_{SE}
or
{\mathtt{C}}_{NW}
. The former is essentially rules out since south paths
{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}
have a strong southward drift. The latter event is illustrated above, and results in an unfavourable
{\mathtt{Onion}}
event.

We first split
{\mathtt{D}}^{+}_{0}
into two pieces,

{\mathtt{D}}_{1}=\{v\in{\mathtt{D}}^{+}_{0}:v_{1}+v_{2}\geq-n^{5/6}/2\},\qquad{\mathtt{D}}_{2}={\mathtt{D}}^{+}_{0}\setminus{\mathtt{D}}_{1}.

We first rule out that the paths
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
enter
{\mathtt{D}}_{1}
. The argument is sketched in
Figure

18
. Setting up some notation, define the lines

L_{i}=\{(x,y):x+y=-n^{5/6}/i\},\quad i=1,2.

Let
R
denote the region bounded between the lines
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, and the sets
{\mathtt{C}}_{SE},{\mathtt{C}}_{NW}
.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose that one of the paths the paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-},i\leq I
enters the set
{\mathtt{E}}_{1}
. By path ordering, the rightmost path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
must then also enter
{\mathtt{E}}_{1}
. By Part (4) of
7.2
, we have that
|I-n/4|\leq n^{1/4}\log^{2}n+1
. Therefore by Part (2) of
7.2
, the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
cannot enter the sets
{\mathtt{C}}_{SE}\cup{\mathtt{C}}_{NW}
, so the only way
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
can enter
{\mathtt{D}}_{1}
is if it crosses through the region
R
, first crossing the line
L_{1}
and then later crossing the line
L_{2}
. Let
\pi_{1}=\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}|_{[s,t]}\subset R
denote the segment of
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
given by taking the
first
crossing of
R
from
L_{1}
to
L_{2}
. Let
v=\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(s),w=\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(t)
denote the first and last points on
\pi_{1}
. We claim that

(51)

w_{1}-v_{1}\geq n^{5/6}/100.

Suppose that (
51
) fails. Then
w_{2}-v_{2}\geq 0.4n^{5/6}
, since
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are line segments parallel to the line
y=-x
, vertically shifted by
n^{5/6}/2
. On the other hand, the set
{\mathtt{B}}_{NW}\cap R
is a thin strip contained in a set of the form

\{(x,y)+v_{NW}:|x-y|\leq n^{3/4},|x+y|\leq n^{5/6}/2\}

for some
v_{NW}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The same holds true of
{\mathtt{B}}_{SE}\cap R
(with the vertex
v_{NW}
replaced by a vertex
v_{SE}
). In particular, for
n
large enough we have the estimate

(52)

\max\{|a_{2}-a_{2}^{\prime}|:a,a^{\prime}\in{\mathtt{B}}_{*}\cap R\}<0.39n^{5/6}\},\quad*\in\{\text{NE},\text{SW}\}.

The negation of (
51
) together with (
52
) implies that there exists a path
\pi^{\prime}\subset\pi_{1}\cap{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}
starting and ending at vertices
a,a^{\prime}
and with
a^{\prime}_{2}-a_{2}\geq n^{5/6}/100
. This contradicts the parabolic path estimate (
50
), and so (
51
) must hold.

Figure 19
.
The setup of the second part of the proof of
7.4
. Given that the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
does not enter
{\mathtt{D}}_{1}
, for it to enter
{\mathtt{D}}_{2}
it must cross through the boundary strip
M
in a way that contradicts a parabolic drift estimate. One example of this is given above.

Now, since the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
started on the south side of the Aztec diamond and finishes on the west side, there must exist a path
\pi_{2}\subset\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
contained in the subset
S^{\prime}
of the strip
[v_{1},w_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}
which is bounded below by
\pi_{1}
:

S^{\prime}=\{(x,y)\in[v_{1},w_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}:\text{ There exists }x\in\pi_{1},v_{2}\leq y\},

and which crosses
S^{\prime}
from right to left. Let
S^{\prime\prime}
be the region in
S^{\prime}
bounded between
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
.

By the ordering on paths, the only paths of the form
\mathcal{S}^{\pm}_{i}
that can enter the region
S^{\prime\prime}
are the paths
\mathcal{S}^{-}_{i},i\leq I
. Additionally, only those paths with index
i\geq n/4-n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
can enter
S^{\prime\prime}
. Indeed, the region
S^{\prime\prime}
is contained in the union of the set
{\mathtt{D}}^{+}
together with the component of the Aztec diamond that is connected to the north boundary. Therefore any path
\mathcal{S}^{-}_{i},i\leq I
that enters
S^{\prime}
must enter the region
{\mathtt{D}}^{+}
, and therefore must have index
i\geq n/4-n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
by Part (1) of
7.2
.

In summary, we have constructed a region in a strip
[v_{1},w_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}
of width at least
n^{5/6}/100
, bounded between two
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
paths crossing from left to right and right to left, which contains at most
2n^{1/4}\log^{2}n\leq 2n^{1/4}\log^{n}

\mathcal{S}_{i}
-paths. By
\mathcal{S}
- and
\mathcal{N}
-path interlacing, this region also contains at most
3n^{1/4}\log^{2}n

\mathcal{N}
-paths. That is, we are on the onion event

\mathsf{Onion}(n,n^{5/6}/100,5n^{1/4}\log^{2}n).

By
7.3
, this event cannot hold simultaneously with
E
. Having arrived at a contradiction, we can conclude that
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
does not enter the set
{\mathtt{D}}_{1}
.

To finish the proof, we show that
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
does not enter
{\mathtt{D}}_{2}={\mathtt{D}}^{+}_{0}\setminus{\mathtt{D}}_{1}
. The argument is illustrated in
Figure

19
. Let
M
be the closure of the set

\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{\mathtt{D}}^{+}:d(v,{\mathtt{D}}_{2})\leq n^{1/4}\}.

Note that
M\subset{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}
, since when defining
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{+}
we removed a boundary strip around the perimeter of
{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}
in the region containing
{\mathtt{D}}_{2}
. Let
\partial^{-}M
be the portion of the boundary of
M
contained in
{\mathtt{D}}_{2}
, and let
\partial^{+}M
be the portion of the boundary not contained in
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{-}
.

Since
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
does not hit any vertex in
{\mathtt{D}}_{1}
, for
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
to enter
{\mathtt{D}}_{2}
, there must be two disjoint path segments
\pi_{1}=\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}|_{[s_{1},t_{1}]},\pi_{2}=\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}|_{[s_{2},t_{2}]}
with
t_{1}\leq s_{2}
contained in
M
, such that:

•

\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(s_{1}),\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(t_{2})\in\partial^{+}M
and
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(s_{2}),\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(t_{1})\in\partial^{-}M
.

•

The vertex
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(t_{2})
has a smaller polar coordinate in
(0,2\pi)
than
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(s_{1})
. We can guarantee this path ordering by choosing
[s_{1},t_{1}]
minimally,
[s_{2},t_{2}]
maximally, and using that
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
moves from the south boundary to the west boundary of the Aztec diamond.

We will use these two points to derive a contradiction, repeatedly using that
M\subset{\mathtt{A}}_{\times}
, so the estimate (
50
) is in effect.

First, by (
50
) we must have
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(t_{1})\in P_{\mathtt{S}}^{\log^{2}n+4}+\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(s_{1})
(the
+4
here is to accommodate the fact that the points
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(s_{i}),\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(t_{i})
may not be vertices in
{\mathtt{S}}
). This forces the vertex
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(s_{1})
to lie on the portion of
\partial^{+}M
where
y=x+n^{5/6}/2+n^{1/4}
; call this part of the boundary
\partial^{++}M
. By the ordering in the second bullet, the point
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(t_{2})
must also lie on
\partial^{++}M
. However, again appealing to condition (
50
), we have that
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(t_{2})\in P_{\mathtt{S}}^{\log^{2}n+4}+\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(s_{2})
. Since
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(s_{2})\in\partial^{-}M
, this is a contradiction.
∎

By
7.4
, every path
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm},\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\pm}
is forbidden from entering either
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{-}
or
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{+}
, with the exception of the two paths
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+},\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
. These are the free paths which appear in
Figure

7
. While they will typically cross through the smooth region, we can still control the possibility that they approach the rough-smooth boundary. This is the goal of the next lemma, which will imply Part (4) of
7.1
. For this next lemma, we define one last region,
\Theta
, given as follows. First, let
L
denote the vertical line containing the point
\beta_{n}(n^{5/6}/5,-n^{1/2}\log^{2}n)
, and let
L_{W}\subset L
be the largest segment of
L
whose endpoints are contained in
{\mathtt{C}}_{SW},{\mathtt{C}}_{NW}
. Let
L_{E}=R_{\pi}L_{W}
, and let
\Theta
be the connected component of

[-n-1,n+1]^{2}\setminus({\mathtt{C}}_{\times}\cup L_{W}\cup L_{E})

containing the origin, see
Figure

20
.

Figure 20
.
A sketch of
7.5
. The region containing the origin is
\Theta
. It is well-separated by the red curves from the black region, where we will eventually study Airy fluctuation.

Lemma 7.5
.

The following holds with probability
1-o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.
Either
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
coalesces with
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
and
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
coalesces with
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{+}
, or else
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
coalesces with
\mathcal{S}_{I+1}^{-}
and
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
coalesces with
\mathcal{N}_{I+1}^{+}
. Prior to the coalescence points, the paths
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+},\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
are contained in the set
\Theta
.

Proof.

The coalescence claims are deterministic given the definition of the split point
I
by
4.3
, and from this point forward in the proof we write
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+},\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
for just the portion of these paths prior to the coalescence point with another
\mathcal{S}
- or
\mathcal{N}
-path. We must check that the paths
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+},\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
are contained in the set
\Theta
. We may assume that
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
eventually coalesces with
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
and
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
coalesces with
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{+}
, as the other case follows a symmetric argument. Throughout, we work on the high probability event
E
defined in the proof of
7.4
. The lemma in this case is deterministic on this event.

For
i=3,4,5
, let
L_{i}
be the vertical line that crosses the boundary
\partial^{-}{\mathtt{Cap}}_{SW}
at the point
\beta_{n}(n^{5/6}/i,-n^{1/2}\log^{2}n)
. Let
L_{i,W}\subset L_{i}
be the largest line segment whose endpoints lie on
{\mathtt{C}}_{SW},{\mathtt{C}}_{NW}
, and let
L_{i,E}=R_{\pi}L_{i,W}
.

We first rule out the possibility that
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
crosses
L_{4,E}
. First, by Part (4) of
7.2
, we have that
|I-n/4|\leq n^{1/4}\log^{2}n+1
, and so by Part (2) of
7.2
, the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
does not enter
{\mathtt{C}}_{\times}
. Therefore the only way for
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
to cross
L_{4,E}
is if it first crosses
L_{3,E}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
later coalesces with
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
never crosses
{\mathtt{C}}_{\times}\cup L_{3,E}
, since this would require it to enter
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{+}
, contradicting
7.4
. Therefore
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
must cross
L_{3,E}
again after crossing
L_{4,E}
prior to joining
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
.

Now, we can find subpaths
\pi_{1}=\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}|_{[s_{1},t_{1}]},\pi_{2}=\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}|_{[s_{2},t_{2}]},t_{1}\leq s_{2}
that stay in the region
R
bounded by the four sets

L_{3,E},\quad L_{4,E},\quad{\mathtt{C}}_{SE},\quad{\mathtt{C}}_{NE}

such that
\pi_{1}
starts on
L_{3,E}
and ends on
L_{4,E}
and
\pi_{2}
starts on
L_{4,E}
and ends on
L_{3,E}
. Let
R^{\prime}\subset R
denote the subregion bounded between the paths
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
. Again, the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
cannot enter
R^{\prime}
by
7.4
, since it cannot cross
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{+}
. Similarly, the path
\mathcal{N}_{I+1}^{+}
cannot enter
R^{\prime}
by
7.4
since it cannot cross
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{-}
. Therefore by the ordering on the
\mathcal{S}
- and
\mathcal{N}
-paths, no path can enter the set
R^{\prime}
other than
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
itself, and so since the width of
R^{\prime}
is
n^{5/6}/12
we can conclude that we are on the event
\mathsf{Onion}(n,n^{5/6}/12,1)
. This event has an empty intersection with
E
by
7.3
.

Now, we will use the fact that
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
does not cross
L_{4,E}
to rule out the possibility that
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
crosses
L_{E}=L_{5,E}
. Consider the region
M
of the Aztec diamond bounded between the three
\mathcal{N}
-paths
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{I-1}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{I+1}^{+}
(including the piece of
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
after it coalesces with
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{+}
). There are two
\mathcal{S}
-paths that lie within this region:
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
and
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
, and for every point
v\in\partial M
, there is an
{\mathtt{S}}
-vertex in
M
that is within distance
4
of
v
. Since we are on the event
{\mathtt{Para_{S}}}(\log^{2}n)
, there must be a path of south vertices ending on
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
which is contained in the connected component of
C
of

(v+P_{\mathtt{S}}^{\log^{2}n+4})\cap M

containing
v
. In other words,
C\cap(\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-})\neq\emptyset
. Now, if
v\in L_{5,E}
, then the only two ways that this can happen are if:

•

Either
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
or
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
hits
L_{4,E}
, or

•

One of the three paths
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{I-1}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{I+1}^{+}
hits
{\mathtt{C}}_{\times}
.

The first of these is ruled out by the previous argument, and the second by
7.2
. Therefore
\partial M\cap L_{5,E}=\emptyset
, and so
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}\cap L_{5,E}=\emptyset
.

By a symmetric argument we can ensure that
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{+}
does not cross
L_{4,W}
and that
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
does not cross
L_{5,W}=L_{W}
. Putting these estimates together with the fact that
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+},\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
do not enter
{\mathtt{C}}_{\times}
(
7.2
) gives the lemma.
∎

Given the path control above, we can confirm that the split point and the central height are related deterministically. This is the final piece of
7.1
.

Corollary 7.6
.

With probability
1-o(1)
, we have
H_{n}=4I_{n}-n-1
.

Proof.

Consider any
a
-face
f
, and let

\pi=(v_{0},\dots,v_{k}),\qquad v_{k}\in\partial^{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{S}}

be the path in
F_{\mathtt{S}}(D)
starting from the
{\mathtt{S}}
-vertex
v_{0}:=f-(0,1)
. By Corollary
4.6
, the height
\mathcal{H}_{n}(v_{0})
is given by
R-L+\mathcal{H}_{n}(v_{k-1})
, where
R
and
L
denote the number of right and left turns along the path
\pi
. We first translate this to a statement about face heights on the event where
v_{k}
joins the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
.
First, using that the boundary heights in the Aztec diamond are deterministic, we can compute that

\mathcal{H}_{n}(v_{k-1})=4I-n-5/2+\mathbf{1}(v_{k}-v_{k-1}=(2,-2)).

Also,

\mathcal{H}_{n}(v_{0})=\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)+1+2\theta/\pi,

where
\theta\in(-3\pi/2,\pi/2)
is the polar coordinate of the vector
v_{1}-v_{0}
. Finally,

R-L=4\operatorname{Wind}(\pi)-\mathbf{1}(v_{k}-v_{k-1}=(2,-2))+2\theta/\pi+5/2.

where
\operatorname{Wind}(\pi)
is the number of times that the path
\pi
crosses the vertical ray
f+\{0\}\times[0,\infty)
moving right-to-left minus the number of times it crosses moving left-to-right. Putting all this together gives that

(53)

\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)=4\operatorname{Wind}(\pi)+4I-n-1.

Now, let
f
be the
a
-face closest to the point
\beta_{n}(0,n^{3/4})
. To complete the proof, given (
53
) it is enough to show that with probability
1-o(1)
, we have that

•

The south forest path
\pi
started at
v_{0}:=m\beta_{n}(0,n^{3/4})-(0,1)
joins the backbone path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
, and

•

\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)=4\operatorname{Wind}(\pi)+H_{n}
.

We show that the first bullet holds deterministically on the event
E
from
7.4
.

Indeed, the path
\pi
starts in the set
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{+}
, so by
7.4
, it starts in a region which is above the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
, and so by backbone path ordering, the path
\pi
cannot join any path
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-},i<I
. Next, suppose that the path
\pi
enters the region
{\mathtt{C}}_{SW}
before coalescing with any other path, and let
\pi_{0}
be the segment of
\pi
up until the point when
\pi
hits
{\mathtt{C}}_{SW}
. In this case, Part (2) of
7.2
and
7.4
imply that the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
does not enter the region
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{+}\cup\pi_{0}\cup{\mathtt{C}}_{SW}
. On the other hand, this region cuts off the vertex
v^{S}_{I_{n}}
from the sink boundary of the Aztec diamond, which is a contradiction. Therefore
\pi
coalesces with another path before entering the region
{\mathtt{C}}_{SW}
.

Next, prior to coalescing with another path, the path
\pi
stays in the region
P=v_{0}+P^{\log^{2}n}_{\mathtt{S}}
. Now, the connected component
Q
of
R\setminus{\mathtt{C}}_{SW}
containing
v_{0}
is itself contained in
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{-}
, and also in the union of
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{+}
and the component of
[-n,n]^{2}\setminus{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{+}
which is cut off from the north boundary.
Therefore by
7.4
and path ordering again, the path
\pi
cannot join any path of the form
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-},i\geq I+1
or
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{+},i\neq I
. Finally,
C
is disjoint from the set
\Theta
in
7.5
, and so
\pi
cannot join
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
. Therefore
\pi
must join
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
, as desired.

For the second bullet point, we use the smooth phase coupling in
5.7
. We also continue to work on the high-probability event
E
. By
5.7
, letting
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a}
be a dimer configuration with height functions
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we can couple this configuration with our Aztec diamond configuration
D_{n}
so that with probability
1-o(1)
we have
D|_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},2}}=D_{n}|_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},2}}
. Also, let
\pi^{\prime}
be the south forest path started at
v_{0}
in
D
, and let
\pi_{0}
be the portion of
\pi
stopped when the path first leaves the set
\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},2}
. Note that
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
agree on the segment
\pi_{0}
. Now, we claim that with probability
1-o(1)
, we have

(54)

\mathcal{H}(f)=4\operatorname{Wind}(\pi^{\prime})=4\operatorname{Wind}(\pi_{0})=4\operatorname{Wind}(\pi).

The first equality is by
4.11
and the second equality uses the parabolic path estimate in
5.8
, together with the fact that the distance between
v_{0}
to the boundary of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},2}
is at least
n^{3/4}/100
for large
n
. The third equality is deterministic on
E
. Indeed,
\pi
will not wind around
v_{0}
after exiting
\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},2}
and before joining
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
, since we are on the event
\mathsf{Para}_{\mathtt{S}}(\log^{2}n)
, and the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
will not wind around
v_{0}
since this would entail it entering
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{+}
. Finally, with high probability we have that

\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)=\mathcal{H}(f)+H_{n}.

This follows by the same reasoning in the proof of
6.6
, specifically the argument for the convergence (
42
). Putting this display together with (
54
) yields the second bullet point.
∎

Proof of
7.1
.

Part
1
follows from
5.2
. Part
2
follows from
5.9
. For Part
3
, Part (4) of
7.2
guarantees that with probability
1-o(1)
,

[I_{n}-n^{1/4},I_{n}]\subset[n/4-2n^{1/4}\log^{2}n,n/4+n^{1/4}\log^{2}n].

Therefore by Part (2) of
7.2
and
7.4
, no paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-},i\in\llbracket I_{n}-n^{1/4},I_{n}\rrbracket
enter the set
{\mathtt{C}}_{\times}\cup{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{+}
with probability
1-o(1)
. Finally,

({\mathtt{X}}\cup{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n})\setminus{\mathtt{RS}}^{*}_{n}\subset{\mathtt{C}}_{\times}\cup{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{+},

yielding the result. For Part
4
,
7.4
guarantees that with probability
1-o(1)
, none of the paths
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{+}
enter the region
{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}
, since in order to do so, these paths would need to first enter both
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{-}
and
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{+}
. The path
\mathcal{N}_{I}^{-}
does not enter the region
{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}
by
7.5
, since
\Theta\cap{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}=\emptyset
and none of the paths
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{+}
enter
{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}
. By path ordering, none of the paths
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{-},i\geq I
enter
{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}
either.

For part
5
, the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{+}
does not enter the region
{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}
prior to coalescing with another path by
7.5
, again since
\Theta\cap{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}=\emptyset
. The paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-},i\leq I_{n}-n^{1/4}\log^{4}n
do not enter
{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}
by Part (3) of
7.2
and the lower bound on
I_{n}
in Part (4) of
7.2
, since
{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}\subset{\mathtt{B}}_{SW}\cup{\mathtt{A}}_{SW}
. Part
6
follows from Corollary
7.6
.
∎

8.
Local path regularity at the rough-smooth boundary

In this section, we prove a more refined path regularity estimate in the rough-smooth boundary region
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
, using the global path control in
7.1
as the main input.

Throughout this section, we will work on the high probability event where the six points of
7.1
hold. Call this event
{\mathtt{Reg}}_{n}
. The main content of this section is the following proposition. We also write
I=I_{n}
for the split point, and throughout we think of the backbone paths
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\pm},\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\pm}
as piecewise linear plane curves, parametrized by arc length, and directed from source to sink. Finally, in this section and the next section, for a backbone path
\gamma\in\{\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{i}^{-}:i=1,\dots,n/2\}
ending on the west boundary we write
U(\gamma),L(\gamma)
for the two components of
[-n,n]^{2}\setminus\gamma
, where
U(\gamma)
contains
(n,n)
and
L(\gamma)
contains
(-n,-n)
.

Proposition 8.1
.

For all large enough
n
, the following estimates hold on the event
{\mathtt{Reg}}_{n}
.

1.

Let
k\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor\}
, and suppose that
v=(v_{1},v_{2})=\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}(s),w=(w_{1},w_{2})=\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}(t)
are two points in
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
for some
s<t
. Then

(55)

w_{1}-v_{1}<(2k+1)n^{1/4}\log^{2}n.

2.

Similarly, suppose
k\in\{1,\dots,\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor\}
, and that
v=\mathcal{N}_{I-k}^{-}(s),w=\mathcal{N}_{I-k}^{-}(t)
are two points in
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
for some
s<t
. Then

(56)

w_{1}-v_{1}<2kn^{1/4}\log^{2}n.

Figure 21
.
The base case of
8.1
. If the path
\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}}^{-}
has an overhang which is much larger than
\sqrt{n^{1/2}\log^{3/2}n}
, then this will trap a north path by virtue of the parabolic path estimates, since the region above
\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}}^{-}
in
{\mathtt{LG}}_{n}^{*}
does not contain any backbone paths. The inductive step is similar, except instead of having a free region above a path
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{-}
or
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{-}
, we have a path whose overhang depth is controlled by the inductive hypothesis.

Proof.

We prove both parts of the proposition simultaneously by induction on the paths, using the ordering

\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{I-1}^{-},\mathcal{S}_{I-1}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{I-2}^{-},\dots.

The base case is the estimate for the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
. See
Figure

21
for an illustration of this case. Consider points
v=\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(s),w=\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(t)\in{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*},s<t
. We assume that
v_{1}<w_{1}
, since otherwise (
55
) is immediate. Consider the strip
R=([v_{1},w_{1}]\times\mathbb{R})\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
. We can recursively define times
s_{1}<t_{1}\leq s_{2}<t_{2}\leq\cdots\leq s_{k}<t_{k}
as follows. For this recursion, set
s_{0}=t_{0}=0
.

1.

For odd
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
t_{i}=\inf\{r\geq t_{i-1}:\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(r)\in{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}\cap(\{v_{1}\}\times\mathbb{R})
and then let
s_{i}=\sup\{r\leq t_{i}:\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(r)\in{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}\cap(\{w_{1}\}\times\mathbb{R})
.

2.

For even
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
t_{i}=\inf\{r\geq t_{i-1}:\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(r)\in{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}\cap(\{w_{1}\}\times\mathbb{R})
and then let
s_{i}=\sup\{r\leq t_{i}:\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(r)\in{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}\cap(\{v_{1}\}\times\mathbb{R})
.

We can only perform this recursion finitely many times, stopping at some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, since the path
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
starts to the left of
[v_{1},w_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}
, ends to the right of this strip and never intersects the set
({\mathtt{X}}\cup{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n})\setminus{\mathtt{RS}}^{*}_{n}
(Part (3) of
7.1
), we have that
k
is odd. Since
v=\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(s),w=\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}(t)\in{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
for some
s<t
, we have that
k\geq 3
.

Now, the set

R\setminus(\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}|_{[s_{1},t_{1}]}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}|_{[s_{k},t_{k}]})

has
k+1
connected components,
C_{1},\dots,C_{k+1}
. We can index these components so that
C_{1}
sits below
C_{2}
, which sits below
C_{3}
, etc. With this indexing,
C_{2}\cap U(S_{I}^{-})
contains two path segments
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}|_{[s_{i},t_{i}]},\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}|_{[s_{j},t_{j}]}
on its boundary.
In particular, for every
r\in[v_{1},w_{1}]
we can find a north vertex
u\in C_{2}\cap U(S_{I}^{-})
with
|u_{1}-r|\leq 1
. We will consider such a vertex when
r=(v_{1}+w_{1})/2
, and let
\gamma
be the north forest path starting at
u
. By Part (4) of
7.1
and path ordering, no
\mathcal{N}
-backbone paths enter the region
U(\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-})\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
, and so the path
\gamma
does not join a backbone path before exiting
C_{2}
. Therefore by Part (2) of
7.1
, the path
\gamma
must be contained in the region
u+P^{\log^{2}n}_{\mathtt{N}}
prior to exiting
C_{2}
. Since
C_{2}
is bounded above and below by
\mathcal{S}
-paths, the path
\gamma
must exit through the set
\{v_{1},w_{1}\}\times\mathbb{R}
, and so

(57)

(u+P^{\log^{2}n}_{\mathtt{N}})\cap(\{v_{1},w_{1}\}\times\mathbb{R})\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}\neq\emptyset.

Now,
|u-(v_{1}+w_{1})/2|\leq 1
, so for this to hold,
(u+P^{\log^{2}n}_{\mathtt{N}})
must intersect
R
in a location where the half-width of the parabola is at least
(w_{1}-v_{1})/2-1
. We have the inequality

\operatorname{ht}(R):=\max\{|x_{2}-y_{2}|:x,y\in R\}\leq(2n^{1/3}\log^{2}n+n^{1/2}\log^{3/2}n)+2(w_{1}-v_{1}),

on the height of
R
(which is almost a parallelogram). Therefore by the definition of
P^{\log^{2}n}_{\mathtt{N}}
, we have

\displaystyle(w_{1}-v_{1})/2-1

\displaystyle\leq\log^{2}n+\log(\operatorname{ht}(R)+1)\sqrt{\operatorname{ht}(R)+1}

(58)

\displaystyle\leq 2n^{1/4}\log^{7/4}n+(w_{1}-v_{1})/4,

where the second inequality holds for large enough
n
. This yields (
55
) in the base case.

We move on to the inductive step. The key difference in the base case and the inductive step is that in the base case, we used that
U(\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-})\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
contains no north backbone paths. For any
k\neq 0
, the corridors
U(\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}),U(\mathcal{N}_{I-k}^{-})
will contain dual backbone paths within the set
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
. However, if the paths
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
(or
\mathcal{N}_{I-k}^{-}
) backtrack too much, then we can use the inductive hypothesis to rule out the presence of such paths deep into a backtrack.

Fix
k\geq 1
. We will only present the case when we prove the bound (
56
) on
\mathcal{N}_{I-k}^{-}
given the bound (
55
) on
\mathcal{S}_{I-k-1}^{-}
. The proof of the bound (
55
) on
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
given the bound (
56
) on
\mathcal{N}_{I-k}^{-}
follows a symmetric argument (and is more similar to the base case).

Consider points
v=\mathcal{N}_{I-k}^{-}(s),w=\mathcal{N}_{I-k}^{-}(t)\in{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*},s<t
. We assume that

(59)

w_{1}-v_{1}>(2k-1)n^{1/4}\log^{2}n,

We define points
s_{1}<t_{1}\leq s_{2}<\cdots\leq s_{k}<t_{k}
and regions
C_{1},\dots,C_{k+1}
exactly as before, with
\mathcal{N}_{I-k}^{-}
in place of
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-}
. Again,
k
must be odd and at least equal to
3
, and we work with the region
C_{2}
.

Now, suppose
\mathcal{S}_{I-k+1}^{-}
enters
C_{2}
, and let
v^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}_{I-k+1}^{-}(s^{\prime})
,
w^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}_{I-k+1}^{-}(t^{\prime})
,
s^{\prime}<t^{\prime}
, denote the first and last times when
\mathcal{S}_{I-k+1}^{-}
is contained in
C_{2}
. The region
C_{2}
is bounded above and below by portions of the north paths
\mathcal{N}_{I-k}^{-}
, and so either both
v^{\prime},w^{\prime}
are contained in
\{v_{1}\}\times\mathbb{R}
, or else both are contained in
\{w_{1}\}\times\mathbb{R}
. Now, for every point
u^{\prime}\in C_{2}
on the curve
\mathcal{S}_{I-k+1}^{-}|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]}
we have

(60)

|u_{1}^{\prime}-v_{1}^{\prime}|\leq\max(u^{\prime}_{1}-v_{1}^{\prime},w_{1}^{\prime}-u_{1}^{\prime})<(2k-1)n^{1/4}\log^{2}n.

Here the first inequality uses that
v_{1}^{\prime}=w_{1}^{\prime}
, and the second inequality uses the inductive hypothesis. Here we can apply the inductive hypothesis since
u^{\prime},v^{\prime},w^{\prime}\in C_{2}\subset{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
. At this point, we assume that
v_{1}^{\prime}=v_{1}
, as the case when
v_{1}^{\prime}=w_{1}
is symmetric. Define the set

C_{3}^{\prime}=C_{3}\cap([v_{1},w_{1}^{*}]\times\mathbb{R}),\quad w_{1}^{*}:=w_{1}-(2k-1)n^{1/4}\log^{2}n.

which contains no points on any south backbone paths by the above discussion, and is non-empty by (
59
). Now, similarly to the base case we can find a south vertex
u\in C_{2}^{\prime}
with
|u_{1}-(v_{1}+w_{1}^{*})/2|\leq 1
. Letting
\gamma
be the south forest path starting from
u
, as in (
57
) we have that

(u+P^{\log^{2}n}_{\mathtt{S}})\cap(\{v_{1},w_{1}^{*}\}\times\mathbb{R})\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}\neq\emptyset.

At this point, we can proceed as in the base case following (
59
) to prove that

(w_{1}^{*}-v_{1})/4\leq 2n^{1/4}\log^{7/4}n+1,

which gives (
56
) after substituting in
w_{1}
for
w_{1}^{*}
and simplifying.
∎

We can use
8.1
to show that north and south paths are close in the region
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
, see
Figure

22
. For this corollary, we introduce the smaller set

{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}:=\beta_{n}([-n^{3/4},n^{3/4}]\times[-n^{1/2}\log^{3/2}n,2n^{1/3}\log^{2}n]).

Corollary 8.2
.

On the event
{\mathtt{Reg}}_{n}
we have the following estimates for
k\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor-1\}
.

1.

For any vertex
w\in\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}
we can find a vertex
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
with
\|w-w^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq(2k+2)n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
.

2.

For any vertex
v\in\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}
we can find a vertex
w\in\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
with
\|v-v^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq(2k+1)n^{1/4}\log^{2}n
.

Figure 22
.
The idea behind Corollary
8.2
is that in the absence of large path overhangs (
8.1
), if a pair of paths
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}
deviate too far from each other, then some vertex
\mathbf{v}
cannot be connected to a backbone path without exiting its bounding parabola
\mathbf{v}+P^{\log^{2}n}_{\cdot}
.

Proof.

For the proof, it is useful to define an intermediate set
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{\prime}
with
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}\subset{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{\prime}\subset{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
, whose definition is the same as that of
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}
but with the interval
[-3n^{3/4}/2,3n^{3/4}/2]
in place of
[-n^{3/4},n^{3/4}]
.

Fix
k
, and consider a south vertex
v\in{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{\prime}
contained in the corridor
L(\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-})\cap U(\mathcal{S}_{I-k-1}^{-})
between
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
and
\mathcal{S}_{I-k-1}^{-}
(here notation is as in the proof of
8.1
). Let
r=d_{\infty}(v,\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*})
, and let
\gamma
be the south forest path starting at
v
. We first show that

(61)

\text{$\gamma$ \text{ joins } $\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}$}\qquad\implies\qquad r\leq(2k+3/2)n^{1/4}\log^{2}n.

Let
\gamma_{0}
be the segment of
\gamma
stopped at the first vertex along a south backbone path. By Part (2) of
7.1
, we have that

\gamma_{0}\subset v+P_{\mathtt{S}}^{\log^{2}n}
, and so

\gamma_{0}\subset A:=(v+P_{\mathtt{S}}^{\log^{2}n})\cap L(\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-})\cap U(\mathcal{S}_{I-k-1}^{-}).

The set
A
is necessarily contained in
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
by Part (3) of
7.1
. Here we have used that any vertex
v
in
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{\prime}
is far away from the left and right boundaries of the larger set
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
, so the set
v+P_{\mathtt{S}}^{\log^{2}n}
only crosses
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
on the part of the boundary where it would immediately enter
{\mathtt{PRS}}_{n}^{*}
.

Now, suppose that
\gamma
joins
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
. Then
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
has non-empty intersection with the set
(v+P_{\mathtt{S}}^{\log^{2}n})\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
; let
w
denote the intersection point. Now, since
v\in L(\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-})
, the path
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
also intersects the vertical ray
v+\{0\}\times[0,\infty)
within the set
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
. Call
w^{\prime}
the intersection point. Finally, let
w^{\prime\prime}
be a point on the horizontal line
v+\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}
, on the segment of
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
connecting the vertices
w,w^{\prime}
. We have that

\displaystyle w^{\prime}_{1}=v_{1}^{\prime},\qquad|w_{1}-v_{1}|\leq 2n^{1/4}\log^{7/4}n,\qquad|w^{\prime\prime}_{1}-v_{1}|\geq r.

Here the equality is by definition, the first inequality follows as in (
58
), and the third inequality is from the definition of
r
.
On the other hand, the backtrack estimate in
8.1
implies that

\min(|w_{1}^{\prime\prime}-w_{1}|,|w_{1}^{\prime\prime}-w_{1}^{\prime}|)\leq(2k+1)n^{1/4}\log^{2}n.

Putting these results together and simplifying gives the implication (
61
).

Now, consider any vertex
w\in\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}
. We can find a south vertex
v\in L(\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-})\cap U(\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-})
with
\|v-w\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. This vertex is necessarily in
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{\prime}
(again, this uses that the paths
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}
do not intersect the upper and lower boundaries of
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
). Since
v\in U(\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-})
, the south forest path from
v
must join
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
, so we can apply (
61
) to bound
d_{\infty}(v,\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*})
, and then in turn bound
d_{\infty}(w,\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*})
by adding
1
. This gives part
1
of the corollary. Part
2
follows a symmetric proof.
∎

9.
Convergence of the backbone to the Airy line ensemble

In the section, we prove the main theorem of the paper,
2.2
, which shows that the backbone paths
\mathcal{S}_{I}^{-},\mathcal{S}_{I-1}^{-},\mathcal{S}_{I-2}^{-}
in the south Temperleyan forest converge to the Airy line ensemble near the rough-smooth boundary. Corollary
8.2
will show that the same is also true of the north backbone paths. We begin by stating a more detailed version of the main theorem, which also encompasses Remark
2.5
. First, recall the scaling transformation
\gamma_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined prior to
6.8
:

\gamma_{n}(t,x)=n\xi_{0}(t/n)+(x,0).

Define functions
\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},i\in\{1,\dots,I_{n}\}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,+}(t)

\displaystyle=\sup\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\gamma_{n}(2^{1/3}\lambda_{2}n^{2/3}t,2^{5/3}\lambda_{1}n^{1/3}x)\in\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}+1-i}^{-}\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,-}(t)

\displaystyle=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\gamma_{n}(2^{1/3}\lambda_{2}n^{2/3}t,2^{5/3}\lambda_{1}n^{1/3}x)\in\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}+1-i}^{-}\cap{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}\},

where we let the above supremum/infimum be
-\infty/+\infty
if the corresponding set is empty. We similarly define
\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,{\mathtt{N}}+},\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,{\mathtt{N}}-}
with the north path
\mathcal{N}_{I_{n}-i}^{-}
in place of
\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}+1-i}^{-}
.

Theorem 9.1
.

Fix
\ast\in\{+,-,{\mathtt{N}}+,{\mathtt{N}}-\}
. Then the line ensemble
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,*}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
converges to the parabolic Airy line ensemble
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
in the sense of finite dimensional distributions. That is, for any finite set
T\subset\mathbb{R}
we have

(62)

(\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,*}|_{T}:i\in\mathbb{N})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{A}_{i}|_{T}:i\in\mathbb{N}).

Moreover, the limiting Airy line ensemble is the same as the line ensemble in the height function limit from

6.8
. More precisely, with
\phi_{n},\mathcal{H}_{n},\hat{\gamma}_{n}
as in
6.8
we have the joint FDD convergence

(63)

\begin{split}(\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(\hat{\gamma}_{n}(u))-H_{n},\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,*}(t))\implies(-4\mathcal{A}(u),\mathcal{A}_{i}(t)).\end{split}

Here the joint convergence is over
u\in S,t\in T
, and
i\in\mathbb{N}
, where
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{2},T\subset\mathbb{R}
are arbitrary finite or countable sets.
Finally, the convergence in (
62
) and (
63
) is uniform on certain mesoscopic sets. That is, in both (
62
) and (
63
) we may replace
\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,*}(\cdot)
with

(64)

\sup_{|u|\leq n^{-1/3-\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,*}(\cdot+u)\qquad\text{ or }\qquad\inf_{|u|\leq n^{-1/3-\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,*}(\cdot+u)

for any
\varepsilon>0
, without affecting the result.

Remark 9.1
.

In the final mesoscopic strengthening of the theorem, the trivial estimate allows us to take suprema and infima over ranges of size
\Delta n^{-2/3}
, which is the scale on which we see the discrete lattice. Of course, the optimal result in this direction would be uniform convergence to the Airy line ensemble, as discussed in the introduction.

Moreover, as with
6.8
, we can strengthen the above theorem to allow the finite sets
S,T
to vary with
n
. We could also give a version of the above theorem with
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}
replaced by
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, where in the limit we also see i.i.d. copies of the full-plane smooth phase (as in (
47
)). For brevity, we have omitted these versions.

The main step in proving
9.1
is the following proposition. For this proposition and for the remainder of the section, fix a mollifying sequence
\phi_{n}
satisfying the conditions of
6.8
. For a fixed mesh size
\delta>0
, a line index
i\in\mathbb{N}
and a time
t\in\mathbb{R}
, define random variables

A_{n,\delta}(i,t)=\max\{x\in[-\delta^{-1},\delta^{-1}]\cap\delta\mathbb{Z}:[H_{n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(\hat{\gamma}_{n}(x,t))]/4\geq i-\delta\},

where we set this
\max
to
-\infty
if
[H_{n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(\hat{\gamma}_{n}(x,t))]/4<i
for all
x
in the given range.

The random variables
A_{n,\delta}
are an approximation of the location of the
i
-th Airy line at a mesh size
\delta
. Indeed, by
6.8
,

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{n,\delta}(i,t)\quad\implies\quad\mathcal{A}_{i}(t),

jointly over finitely many lines
i
and times
t
.

Proposition 9.2
.

Fix
\varepsilon,\delta>0
, a time
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(|A_{n,\delta}(i,t)-\sup_{|u|\leq n^{-1/3-\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,+}(\cdot+u)|>\sqrt{\delta})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(|A_{n,\delta}(i,t)-\inf_{|u|\leq n^{-1/3-\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,-}(\cdot+u)|>\sqrt{\delta})

\displaystyle=0.

Before proving
9.2
, we use it to establish
9.1
.

Proof of
9.1
.

We first note that by Corollary
8.2
, for any fixed
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,+}(t)-\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,{\mathtt{N}}+}(t)|\geq n^{-1/12}\log^{3}n)\to 0

as
n\to\infty
, and similarly with
+\mapsto-
,
{\mathtt{N}}+\mapsto{\mathtt{N}}-
. Therefore it suffices to prove
9.1
only when
*\in\{+,-\}
. Next, write

\mathcal{B}_{i}^{n,-}(t)=\inf_{|u|\leq n^{-1/3-\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,-}(\cdot+u),\qquad\mathcal{B}_{i}^{n,+}=\sup_{|u|\leq n^{-1/3-\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,+}(\cdot+u).

It furthermore suffices to prove (
63
) with
\mathcal{B}_{i}^{n,\pm}
in place of
\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,\pm}
.

Now, fix a finite set
T\subset\mathbb{R}
and a countable dense set
Q\subset\mathbb{R}
. Using
6.8
and the Skorokhod representation theorem, we can consider a coupling of all of the Aztec diamond measures at each
n\in 4\mathbb{N}
with the Airy line ensemble so that for any
t,q\in Q
we have

(65)

\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(\hat{\gamma}_{n}(q,t))-H_{n}\to-4\mathcal{A}(q,t)

almost surely. It suffices to show that in this coupling, for any fixed
i\in\mathbb{N},t\in Q
, we have

\mathcal{B}_{i}^{n,\pm}(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{i}(t)
. The statement (
63
) then follows by taking
Q
so that
S\cup T^{2}\subset Q^{2}
.

Observe that by (
65
), we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{1}(|\mathcal{A}_{i}(t)|\leq\delta^{-1}-1)|A_{n,\delta}(i,t)-\mathcal{A}_{i}(t)|\leq\delta,

and so by
9.2
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{B}_{i}^{n,\pm}(t)-\mathcal{A}_{i}(t)|>\delta+\sqrt{\delta})\leq\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{A}_{i}(t)|>\delta^{-1}).

Since
\delta>0
was arbitrary and the random variables
\mathcal{A}_{i}(t)
are all finite, this gives the desired result.
∎

In the remainder of the section we prove
9.2
.

The main part of the proof involves showing that around any fixed point
\hat{\gamma}_{n}(x,t)
we can define a box
O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
size
n^{1/3-\varepsilon}\times n^{1/3-\varepsilon}
such that with high probability:

•

No south backbone paths enter the box
O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)

•

We have

(66)

O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\subset U(\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}-J})\cap L(\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}-J+1}),

where

J\approx(H_{n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(\hat{\gamma}_{n}(x,t)))/4.

The dimensions of the box
O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
are important here. The key point will be that the width of the box is larger than the size of the maximum backtrack of any south backbone path entering the region. The maximum backtrack size is
O(n^{1/4}\log^{2}n)
by
8.1
. The idea for constructing the box
O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
is to use the local smooth phase coupling,
5.5
, to rule out the potential presence of backbone paths. The difficulty with that lemma is that it only provides a coupling on a rectangle of width
o(n^{1/12})
, which is too small to make
8.1
effective. The workaround is to first build a ring of overlapping boxes of small size, each of which is individually coupled to the full-plane smooth phase. We build the rings in the next lemma, and then use that lemma together with the path regularity from Section
8
to verify that a box inside this ring satisfies the two bullet points above.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
, and fix
\varepsilon>0,(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For ease of notation, set
c_{n}=\log^{3}n
. Define the set

S_{\varepsilon}=\{v\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:\|v\|_{1}=\lfloor n^{1/3-\varepsilon}\rfloor\},

and for
v\in S_{\varepsilon}
let

B_{v}=\hat{\gamma}_{n}(x,t)+c_{n}(v+[-3/5,3/5]^{2}).

Then with probability
1-o(1)
, the following claims hold:

(i)

We can couple
D
with
|S_{\varepsilon}|
-many copies of the smooth phase
D_{v}\sim\mathbb{P}_{a},v\in S_{\varepsilon}
such

D_{v}|_{B_{v}}=D|_{B_{v}},\qquad\text{ for all }v\in S_{\varepsilon}.

(ii)

In each of the smooth phases
D_{v},v\in S_{\varepsilon}
, any path
\pi
in the associated south forest, started at a point in
D_{v}
, satisfies

\pi\subset P^{\log^{2}n}_{\mathtt{S}}.

Similarly, any path
\pi
in the associated north forest, started at a point in
D_{v}
, satisfies

\pi\subset P^{\log^{2}n}_{\mathtt{N}}.

(iii)

Let
T=\bigcup_{v\in S_{\varepsilon}}B_{v}
, let
\partial^{-}T
be the part of the boundary of
T
connected to the bounded component
U^{-}
of
T^{c}
, and let
\partial^{+}T
be the part of the boundary of
T
connected to the unbounded component
U^{+}
of
T^{c}
.

Consider any north or south backbone path
\pi
, and let
\pi_{0}:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
be a segment of
\pi
with
\pi_{0}|_{(s,t)}
contained in the interior of
T
, and
\pi_{0}(s),\pi_{0}(t)
on the boundary of
T
. Then:

•

If
\pi
is a south backbone path,
\pi_{0}(s)\in\partial T^{+}
and
\pi_{0}(t)\in\partial T^{-}
, then
\pi_{0}(s)
is contained in a box
B_{v}
with
v_{2}\geq 1
.

•

If
\pi
is a south backbone path,
\pi_{0}(s)\in\partial T^{-}
and
\pi_{0}(t)\in\partial T^{+}
, then
\pi_{0}(s)
is contained in a box
B_{v}
with
v_{2}\leq-1
.

•

If
\pi
is a north backbone path,
\pi_{0}(s)\in\partial T^{+}
and
\pi_{0}(t)\in\partial T^{-}
, then
\pi_{0}(s)
is contained in a box
B_{v}
with
v_{2}\leq-1
.

•

If
\pi
is a north backbone path,
\pi_{0}(s)\in\partial T^{-}
and
\pi_{0}(t)\in\partial T^{+}
, then
\pi_{0}(s)
is contained in a box
B_{v}
with
v_{2}\geq 1
.

Figure 23
.
The setup of
9.3
, together with the proof of (iii) given (ii). Essentially, the smooth phase couplings in part (ii) force strong drifts through the ring of boxes
\{B_{v},v\in S_{\varepsilon}\}
. This means
\mathcal{S}
-paths can only enter through a box in the top half of the ring, and exit through a box in the bottom, whereas the opposite is true for
\mathcal{N}
-paths.

Proof.

By
5.5
and a union bound bound over
|S_{\varepsilon}|
-many boxes, we can achieve all of the couplings in part (i) simultaneously with probability at least

1-c|S_{\varepsilon}|n^{-1/3}\log^{24}n\geq 1-cn^{-\varepsilon/2},

where the constant
c
depends on
x,t
, and the inequality holds for large enough
n
.
The parabolic path control in part (ii) follows from
5.8
and a union bound, using that paths in the smooth phase forests are loop-erased random walks.

Part (iii) is deterministic given part (ii). See
Figure

23
for an illustration. We only prove the claim about south backbone paths, as the claim about north backbone paths follows symmetrically. Consider a given path segment
\pi_{0}
, let
B_{v}
be a box that
\pi_{0}
intersects, and let
\tau:[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\to\mathbb{R}
be a segment of
\pi_{0}
contained in
B_{v}
. If we define
\tilde{\tau}:[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\to[-1,1]^{2}
by letting

\displaystyle\tau(r)=\hat{\gamma}_{n}(x,t)+c_{n}(v+\tilde{\tau}(r)),

then by part (ii), for large enough
n
we have that for any
r<r^{\prime}\in[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
:

(67)

\displaystyle|\tilde{\tau}(r^{\prime})_{1}-\tilde{\tau}(r)_{1}|\leq 1/100,\qquad\tilde{\tau}(r^{\prime})_{2}-\tilde{\tau}(r)_{2}\leq 1/100.

Now, for any south backbone path segment
\pi_{0}
contained in
T
, the inequalities above imply that
\pi_{0}
can enter at most three distinct boxes, and that

|(\pi_{0}(s))_{1}-(\pi_{0}(t))_{1}|\leq 3c_{n}/100.

Using this estimate, and the second bound in (
67
), we get that if
\pi_{0}
first enters
T
through a box
B_{v}
with
v_{2}\leq 0
on the boundary
\partial^{+}T
, then it cannot exit
T
on the boundary
\partial^{-}T
. This gives the first bullet. Similarly,
\pi_{0}
exits into
\partial^{+}T
through a box
B_{v}
with
v_{2}\geq 0
, then it cannot have started in the set
\partial^{-}T
. This gives the second bullet.
∎

Lemma 9.4
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and define a set

O_{\varepsilon}=O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)=\hat{\gamma}_{n}(x,t)+[-n^{1/3-\varepsilon},n^{1/3-\varepsilon}]^{2}.

Then with probability
1-o(1)
, no backbone paths of the form
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-},k\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor\log^{50}n\rfloor\}
enter the set
O_{\varepsilon}
.

Figure 24
.
The proof setup for
9.4
.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1/12)
, and consider the probability
(1-o(1))
-event where points (i)-(iii) of
9.3
hold with
\varepsilon
and points
1,2
or Corollary
8.2
hold. On this event, we will show that deterministically, no backbone paths of the form
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-},k\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor\log^{50}n\rfloor\}
enter the set
O_{2\varepsilon}
as long as
n
is sufficiently large.

Fix
k\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor\log^{50}n\rfloor-1\}
and suppose that
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
enters the set
O_{2\varepsilon}
. Let
T,\partial^{\pm}T,U^{\pm}
be as in
9.3
. The set
O_{2\varepsilon}
is contained in
U^{-}
, and moreover, every point in
T
is
L^{\infty}
-distance at least
n^{1/3-\varepsilon}>n^{1/4}\log^{100}n
away from
O_{2\varepsilon}
. In particular, by Corollary
8.2
, the path
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}
must also enter the set
U^{-}
. This path also stays in the set
L(\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-})
.

Now, let
R
be a connected component of
L(\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-})\cap(T\cup U^{-})
such that
R\cap U^{-}\neq\emptyset
. We aim to show that
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}
cannot enter
R\cap U^{-}
, which will complete the proof since
R
is arbitrary. The proof setup is illustrated in
Figure

24
.

The boundary of
R
can be decomposed into two pieces:

•

\partial_{1}R
, which is given by a path segment
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}|_{[s,t]}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}|_{(s,t)}
is contained in the interior of
T\cup U^{-}
and
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}(s),\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}(t)\in\partial^{+}T
.

•

\partial_{2}R
, which is a closed connected subset of
\partial^{+}T
between the points
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}(s)
and
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}(t)
.

We view
\partial_{2}R
as a parametrized simple curve
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
and choose the parametrization so that

\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}(s)=\pi(s),\qquad\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}(t)=\pi(t),

and so that
\pi
is one-to-one.
Now at this point, suppose
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}
intersects the set
R\cap U^{-}
, and let
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]}
be a segment of
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}
such that:

1.

\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]}
is contained in
R
, and intersects the set
U^{-}
.

2.

\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}(s^{\prime}),\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}(t^{\prime})
are on the boundary
\partial^{+}T
.

3.

t^{\prime}
is chosen maximally among all choices of
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}
satisfying
1,2
.

Now, by the first two bullet points in
9.3
(iii), there exists
v,w\in S_{\varepsilon}
such that

\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}(s)\in B_{v},\qquad\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}(t)\in B_{w},\;\;\;\text{ and }v_{1}\geq 1,\;\;w_{1}\leq-1,

and by the third and fourth bullet points in
9.3
(iii), there exists
v^{\prime},w^{\prime}\in S_{\varepsilon}
such that

\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}(s^{\prime})\in B_{v^{\prime}},\qquad\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}(t^{\prime})\in B_{w^{\prime}},\;\;\;\text{ and }v_{1}^{\prime}\leq-1,\;\;w_{1}^{\prime}\geq 1.

Now let
r_{1},r_{2}\in(s,t)
be such that
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}(s^{\prime})=\pi(r_{1})
and
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}(s^{\prime})=\pi(r_{2})
. From the previous two displays, we have that
s<r_{2}<r_{1}<t
(see also
Figure

24
).

On the other hand, consider the curve

\tau=\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}|_{[0,t]}\cup\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}|_{[0,s^{\prime}]}\cup\pi_{[r_{1},t]}.

The set
\tau
cuts off the curve
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}|_{(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})}
from the west boundary of the Aztec diamond, in the sense that
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}|_{(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})}
and
\{-n\}\times[-n,n]
are in different components of the set
[-n,n]^{2}\setminus\tau
. Therefore since the path
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}
finishes on the west boundary, there must be a point
r^{\prime}\geq t^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}(r^{\prime})\in\tau
. Since
r_{2}<r_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}
cannot intersect itself or
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
, the only way this can happen is if the path
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}
cuts back through the set
R\cap U^{-}
, (again,
Figure

24
may help here). This contradicts the maximality of
t^{\prime}
.

Therefore
\mathcal{S}_{I-k}^{-}
cannot enter the set
O_{2\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon<1/12
. A symmetric proof with the roles of the north and south paths reversed implies that
\mathcal{N}_{I-k-1}^{-}
cannot enter
O_{2\varepsilon}
. Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, the lemma follows.
∎

Our next goal is to prove (
66
). To do so, we first need an estimate on the minimum index of a path that enters a region near
O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
. For this lemma, we will need to work with the scaling map
\tilde{\beta}_{n}
used in
6.7
, introduced at the beginning of Section
6
.

Lemma 9.5
.

Fix
L>0
, and consider the region

{\mathtt{A}}_{L}=\tilde{\beta}_{n}([-L,L]\times[-L,\log^{3}n]).

Define

K(L)=\max\{i\in\mathbb{N}:\mathcal{S}_{I-i}^{-}\text{ or }\mathcal{N}_{I-i}^{-}\text{ enters the region }{\mathtt{A}}_{L}\}.

Then

\mathbb{P}(K(L)\leq\log^{4}n)=1-o(1).

Proof.

First, with notation as in
7.1
, let
K
denote the connected component of

{\mathtt{A}}_{L}\cup[({\mathtt{X}}\cup{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n})\setminus{\mathtt{RS}}^{*}_{n}]

containing
{\mathtt{A}}_{L}
. The set
K
is connected to the boundary of the Aztec diamond at the point
(n+1,-n-1)
. Therefore by path ordering, if a north or south backbone path with index
I-i,i>\log^{4}n
enters the region
{\mathtt{A}}_{L}
, then the same must be true of the path
\mathcal{N}_{I-\lfloor\log^{4}n\rfloor}^{-}
. By Part (3) of
7.1
, this path can only enter the region
K
through
{\mathtt{A}}_{L}
. Moreover, since
K
is connected to the Aztec diamond boundary, if it enters the region
{\mathtt{A}}_{L}
, it first enters without winding around that region, and so its entry height is within
4
of its initial height (
4.5
).

Now, by parts
1
and
6
of
7.1
, we have that
|I_{n}-n/4|\leq\log n
with probability
1-o(1)
, and so
I_{n}-\lfloor\log^{4}n\rfloor\leq n/4-\log^{4}n/2
. By the relationship between path index and starting height, the starting height of the path
\mathcal{N}_{I-\lfloor\log^{4}n\rfloor}^{-}
is bounded above by
-\log^{4}n
. Therefore to complete the proof, it suffices to show that

X=\min\{\mathcal{H}_{n}(x):x\in{\mathtt{A}}_{L}\}

is bounded below by
-\log^{4}n/2
with probability
1-o(1)
. We have that
X\geq X_{0}-X_{1}-X_{2}
, where

\displaystyle X_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}_{n}(0,0)

\displaystyle X_{1}

\displaystyle=\max\{|\mathcal{H}_{n}(0,0)-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(t,\log^{3}n)|:t\in[-L,L]\},

\displaystyle X_{2}

\displaystyle=\max\{|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(t,\log^{3}n)-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(t,x)|:t\in[-L,L],x\in[-L,\log^{3}n]\},

and
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}=\mathcal{H}_{n}\circ\tilde{\beta}_{n}
.
First,
X_{0}
converges in law as
n\to\infty
by
5.2
and so
X_{0}\geq-\log^{4}n/6
with probability
1-o(1)
. Next, as in the proof of
6.6
we can couple a dimer configuration
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
with two copies of the smooth phase measure
D_{1},D_{2}\sim\mathbb{P}_{a}
using Lemmas
5.6
,
5.7
so that with probability
1-o(1)
we have
D_{i}|_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},i}}=D|_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},i}}
for
i=1,2
. On the event where this coupling succeeds, using bounds on heights in the smooth phase (
5.10
), we have that

\max\{|\mathcal{H}_{n}(v)-\mathcal{H}_{n}(0,0)|:v\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},1}\cup\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},2}\}\leq\log n

with probability
1-o(1)
, and so
X_{1}\leq\log^{4}n/6
with probability
1-o(1)
. Finally, we show that
X_{2}\leq\log^{4}n/6
with probability
1-o(1)
. To prove this, by Markov’s inequality and a union bound over the
O(n\log^{3}n)
-many lattice points in
{\mathtt{A}}_{L}
, it is enough to show that we can find
\lambda>0
such that

(68)

\sup_{(t,x)\in[-L,L]\times[-L,\log^{3}n]}\mathbb{E}\exp(\lambda|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(t,\log^{3}n)-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(t,x)|)\leq\exp(\log^{3}n)

for all large enough
n
.
Suppose that (
68
) fails. Then for all
\lambda>0
, we can find sequences
t_{n,\lambda}\in[-L,L],x_{n,\lambda}\in[-L,\log^{3}n]
such that

\mathbb{E}\exp(\lambda|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(t_{n,\lambda},\log^{3}n)-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(t_{n,\lambda},x_{n,\lambda})|)\geq\exp(\log^{3}n)

for all
n
in some infinite set
Y_{\lambda}
.
We can apply diagonalization argument here to find
t_{n},x_{n},n\in Y
that works simultaneously for all
\lambda>0
, and then by passing to a further subsequence
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
we may assume that
t_{n}\to t\in[-L,L]
and that
x_{n}\to x\in[-L,\infty]
. Now, letting

\phi_{n}=\{2\lfloor\ell\log^{2}n\rfloor e_{2}:\ell\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor\log^{1/2}n\rfloor\}\},

then

\lim_{n\in Y^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\exp(\lambda|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{n},\log^{3}n)-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(t_{n},x_{n})|)=\infty.

for all
\lambda>0
. This contradicts
6.7
.
∎

We now precisely state and prove (
66
).

Lemma 9.6
.

Fix
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\varepsilon>0
, and let
O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
be as in
9.4
. Let
\phi_{n}
be any mollifying sequence satisfying the conditions of
6.8
. Then there exists a sequence
\sigma_{n}
which tends to
0
with
n
(and depends on
\phi_{n},x,t
) such that with probability
1-o(1)
, we have that

(69)

O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\subset U(\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}-J})\cap L(\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}-J+1}),

where
|J-(H_{n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(\hat{\gamma}_{n}(x,t)))/4|<\sigma_{n}.

In the special case when this forces
J=0
, we remove the intersection with
L(\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}-J+1})
.

Proof.

All statements in the proof will hold with probability
1-o(1)
, and we do not mention this further. We may also assume
\varepsilon<1/12
, and for ease of notation we set
u=\hat{\gamma}_{n}(x,t)
.

First, with notation as in
9.5
, the set
{\mathtt{A}}_{|t|+1}
has at most
\log^{4}n
south backbone paths that enter it:
\mathcal{S}_{I-i}^{-}
,
i=0,\dots,\lfloor\log^{4}n\rfloor
. Here the upper bound on
i
is from
9.5
, and the fact that no
\mathcal{S}^{+}
-paths and no paths of the form
\mathcal{S}_{I+i},i>0
enter this region uses
7.1
, parts 4 and 5, along with north and south path interlacing.

Next, by
9.4
, none of the paths
\mathcal{S}|_{I-i}^{-}
,
i=0,\dots,\lfloor\log^{4}n\rfloor
enter the sets
O_{\varepsilon}=O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
. Therefore there exists some index
J
such that (
69
) holds (where again, we omit the intersection with
L(\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}-J+1})
if
J=0
). It remains to show that
J=(H_{n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(u)/4
. For this, consider the south forest path
\pi:[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
started at the south vertex adjacent to the
a
-face
u
, and let
\pi(s)\in{\mathtt{S}}
denote the location when
\pi
either first coalesces with a backbone path or exits the set
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
. Note that
\pi(0)\in U(\mathcal{S}_{I-\lfloor\log^{4}n\rfloor-1}^{-})
since
\mathcal{S}_{I-\lfloor\log^{4}n\rfloor-1}^{-}
does not enter the set
{\mathtt{A}}_{|t|+1}
or the set
({\mathtt{X}}\cup{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n})\setminus{\mathtt{RS}}^{*}_{n}
(the latter uses Part (3) of
7.1
).

We first show that
\pi(s)
is a coalescence point. Indeed, by the parabolic path estimate in Part (2) of
7.1
, if
\pi(s)
were in
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
, then it would be located on the lower boundary of
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
, at a point in the interior of
{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n}
. In particular, in this case the set

T=\pi|_{[0,s]}\cup[({\mathtt{X}}\cup{\mathtt{PRS}}^{*}_{n})\setminus{\mathtt{RS}}^{*}_{n}]\cup{\mathtt{A}}_{|t|+1}

is connected, and
[-n-1,n+1]^{2}\setminus T
puts the south and west boundaries of the Aztec diamond in different components. As discussed above, the path
\mathcal{S}_{I-\lfloor\log^{4}n\rfloor-1}^{-}
does not enter
T
, which contradicts that this path moves from the south to the west boundary.

Therefore
\pi(s)
is a coalescence point. Moreover, since south forest paths enter
O_{\varepsilon}
,
\pi(s)
is not located in
O_{\varepsilon}
, and again using the parabolic path estimate in Part (2) of
7.1
together with the fact that the height of
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
is less than
2n^{1/2}\log^{3/2}n
, we have that

\pi(s)\in{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}\cap(u+[-n^{1/3-\varepsilon}/100,n^{1/3-\varepsilon}/100]\times(-\infty,-n^{1/3-\varepsilon}]).

Here we have used that
\varepsilon<1/12
to absorb the width of the parabola
P^{\log^{2}n}_{\mathtt{S}}
as it intersects
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}^{*}
(this width is
O(n^{1/4}\log^{3/4}n)
). Now, let
\mathcal{S}_{I-K},K\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor\log^{4}n\rfloor+1\}
be the path containing
\pi(s)
. Arguing deterministically as in the proof of (
53
) in Corollary
7.6
, we have that

\mathcal{H}_{n}(u)=4\operatorname{Wind}(\pi)+4(I-K)-n-1,

and so by Part (6) of
7.1
we have that

H_{n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}(u)=-4\operatorname{Wind}(\pi)+4K.

Now, by
5.5
we can couple our dimer configuration
D_{n}\sim\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
with a dimer configuration
D\sim\mathbb{P}_{a}
such that the two agree on the set
u+[-b_{n},b_{n}]^{2}
, where
n^{1/100}\leq b_{n}=o(n^{1/24})
and
\phi_{n}\subset[-b_{n},b_{n}]^{2}
. Let
\mathcal{H}
be the height function for
D
, and let
\pi_{0}
be the south forest path in
D
started at
\pi(0)
. On the event where this coupling holds, we have that

\mathcal{H}_{n}(u)-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(u)=\mathcal{H}(u)-\mathcal{H}^{\phi_{n}}(u)=4\operatorname{Wind}(\pi_{0})+o(1).

Here the final equality uses
4.11
, and the
o(1)
term goes to
0
in probability as
n\to\infty
(by Corollary
4.12
,
5.10
). Therefore to complete the proof, we must show that
\operatorname{Wind}(\pi_{0})=\operatorname{Wind}(\pi)
with high probability. First,
\pi_{0}
will not cross vertical line

V=u+\{0\}\times[0,\infty)

after it first exits the box
u+[-b_{n},b_{n}]^{2}
with high probability by the parabolic path estimate in
5.8
. Up until this point,
\pi_{0}
and
\pi
agree. Similarly,
\pi
will not cross
V
after it exits this box and before it joins the backbone path
\mathcal{S}_{I-K}^{-}
.

Next, if
\mathcal{S}_{I-K}^{-}
were to cross
V
within the set
{\mathtt{RS}}_{n}
, then it would have a backtrack of size at least
n^{1/3-\varepsilon}/2
, since it cannot enter the set
O_{\varepsilon}
. Since
\varepsilon<1/12
, this would contradict
8.1
. Therefore the barrier estimate in Part (3) of
7.1
guarantees that the number of left-to-right crossings of the line
V
by
\mathcal{S}_{I-K}^{-}
equals the number of right-to-left crossings, which finally implies that
\operatorname{Wind}(\pi_{0})=\operatorname{Wind}(\pi)
, as desired.
∎

At last, we are ready to prove
9.2
.

Proof of
9.2
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1/12),\delta\in(0,1/2)
, and let

Z_{\delta}=[-\delta^{-1},\delta^{-1}]\cap\delta\mathbb{Z}.

Define
M:Z_{\delta}\to\mathbb{N}
so that
M(x)
is the nearest integer to the point
[H_{n}-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(\hat{\gamma}_{n}(x,t))]/4
, and define
J^{\pm}:Z_{\delta}\to\mathbb{N}
so that

\displaystyle J^{-}(x)=\min\{i=0,1,\dots:O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\subset U(\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}-i})\neq\emptyset\},

\displaystyle J^{+}(x)=\max\{i=1,\dots:O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\subset L(\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}-i+1})\neq\emptyset\},

where in the definition of
J^{+}
, we set the maximum to
0
if the set above is empty.
By
9.6
,
J^{-}=J^{+}
and
|J^{-}-M|<\sigma_{n}
with probability
1-o(1)
for a sequence
\sigma_{n}\to 0
with
n
.

Now, by the backtrack estimate in
8.1
and the fact that the width of
O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
is of order
n^{1/3-\varepsilon},\varepsilon<1/12
, the following implications hold simultaneously with probability
1-o(1)
for all
i\in\{0,\dots,\log n\},x\in Z_{\delta}
:

\displaystyle O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)

\displaystyle\subset U(\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}+1-i})\quad\implies\quad\sup_{|u|\leq n^{-1/3-2\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,+}(t+u)<x.

\displaystyle O_{\varepsilon}(x,t)

\displaystyle\subset L(\mathcal{S}_{I_{n}+1-i})\quad\implies\qquad\inf_{|u|\leq n^{-1/3-2\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,-}(t+u)>x.

At this point, we fix
i
and work deterministically on the event where the above implications hold,
J^{-}=J^{+}
, and
|J^{-}-M|<\sigma_{n}
for
\sigma_{n}<\delta
. On this event, the two implications above give that

(70)

\displaystyle\sup_{|u|\leq n^{-1/3-2\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,+}(t+u)

\displaystyle<\min\{x\in Z_{\delta}:J^{-}\leq i-1\},

(71)

\displaystyle\inf_{|u|\leq n^{-1/3-2\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n,-}(t+u)

\displaystyle>\max\{x\in Z_{\delta}:J^{+}\geq i\},

where the right-hand side of (
70
) equals
-\infty
if the set is empty, and the right-hand side of (
71
) equals
-\infty
if that set is empty. Using that
J^{-}=J^{+}
and
|J^{-}-M|<\sigma_{n}<\delta
then implies

(72)

A_{n,\delta}(i,t)=\max\{x\in Z_{\delta}:J^{+}\geq i\}=\min\{x\in Z_{\delta}:J^{-}\leq i-1\}-\delta

on the event
E=\{|A_{n,\delta}(i,t)|\leq\delta^{-1}-1\}
. The final event
E
holds with probability
1-o(1)
as we first take
n\to\infty
and then take
\delta\to 0
, since in this double limit,
A_{n,\delta}(i,t)
converges in law to
\mathcal{A}_{i}(t)
, which is a finite almost surely. Combining (
70
), (
71
), and (
72
) gives the proposition.
∎

10.
Correlation Kernel and Estimates

In this section, we introduce the Kasteleyn matrix as well as the relevance of its inverse. We give a formula for the entries of the inverse of the Kasteleyn matrix of the two-periodic Aztec diamond, as well as formulas which serve as a starting point for taking asymptotics of these entries. These results are refinements of the ones in
[
CJ16
]
, and are proved in
Section

14
.

10.1.
Inverse Kasteleyn matrix for the two-periodic Aztec diamond

Suppose that
G
is a planar weighted bipartite graph. Recall that the Kasteleyn matrix,
K
, of the graph
G
