is a type of signed weighted adjacency matrix whose rows are indexed by the black vertices and whose columns are indexed by the white vertices of the graph, see
[
Joh18
, Section 3]
for more background. The importance of the Kasteleyn matrix is due to the following theorem. Here the underlying dimer measure assigns a dimer configurations a probability proportional to the product of edge weights.

Theorem 10.1

(
[
Ken97
,
Joh18
]
)
.

Suppose that
E=\{\mathtt{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
is a collection of distinct edges on
G
with
\mathtt{e}_{i}=(\mathtt{b}_{i},\mathtt{w}_{i})
, where
\mathtt{b}_{i}
and
\mathtt{w}_{i}
denote black and white vertices. The dimer configuration forms a determinantal point process on the edges of
G
with correlation kernel
L
meaning that the probability of observing edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{r}
is given by

\det L(\mathtt{e}_{i},\mathtt{e}_{j})_{1\leq i,j\leq r}

where

L(\mathtt{e}_{i},\mathtt{e}_{j})=K({\mathtt{b}_{i},\mathtt{w}_{i}})K^{-1}({\mathtt{w}_{j},\mathtt{b}_{i}}).

More general versions of this theorem hold, but we do not need these here. The Kasteleyn matrix
K_{a,b}
for the two-periodic Aztec diamond where
a
-faces and
b
-faces have weight
a,b>0
takes entries

(73)

\begin{split}&K_{a,b}(x,y)\\
&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(b(1-j)+aj)(1-k)\mathrm{i}+(bj+a(1-j))k&\mbox{if }y=x+(-1)^{j}e_{k+1},x\in\mathtt{E},\\
(b(1-j)+aj)k+(bj+a(1-j))(1-k)\mathrm{i}&\mbox{if }y=x+(-1)^{j}e_{k+1},x\in\mathtt{W},\\
0&\mbox{if $(x,y)$ is not an edge.}\end{array}\right.\end{split}

where
\mathrm{i}^{2}=-1
and
j,k\in\{0,1\}
. Here we only need to consider
K_{a,1}
. For historical reasons, e.g. see
[
CJ16
]
, we set
n=4m
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, where
n
is the size of the Aztec diamond, and will use this convention throughout and interchange between
n
and
m
for convenience.

We now proceed in stating formulas needed to give a suitable form for
K^{-1}_{a,1}
as well as its asymptotics.
Let

\tilde{c}(u_{1},u_{2})=2(1+a^{2})+a(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})(u_{2}+u_{2}^{-1}),

which is related to the so-called
characteristic polynomial
for the dimer model
[
KOS06
]
; see
[
CJ16
, (4.11)]
for an explanation. Write

(74)

h(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=\varepsilon_{1}(1-\varepsilon_{2})+\varepsilon_{2}(1-\varepsilon_{1}),

and let
\Gamma_{R}
denote a positively oriented circle of radius
R
around the origin, where we adopt this notation for the rest of the paper. In what follows below, we set
\mathtt{W}_{0}=\mathtt{S}
,
\mathtt{W}_{1}=\mathtt{N}
,
\mathtt{B}_{0}=\mathtt{W}
, and
\mathtt{B}_{1}=\mathtt{E}
, along with the full-plane analogs using
\tilde{\mathtt{W}}_{0}
, etc.
The full-plane smooth phase inverse Kasteleyn matrix is given by

\mathbb{K}^{-1}_{1,1}(x,y)=-\frac{\mathrm{i}^{1+h(\varepsilon_{x},\varepsilon_{y})}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{1}}\frac{du_{1}}{u_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{1}}\frac{du_{2}}{u_{2}}\frac{a^{\varepsilon_{y}}u_{2}^{1-h(\varepsilon_{x},\varepsilon_{y})}+a^{1-\varepsilon_{y}}u_{1}u_{2}^{h(\varepsilon_{x},\varepsilon_{y})}}{\tilde{c}(u_{1},u_{2})u_{1}^{\frac{x_{1}-y_{1}+1}{2}}u_{2}^{\frac{x_{2}-y_{2}+1}{2}}},

where

x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\tilde{\mathtt{W}}_{\varepsilon_{x}}
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2})\in\tilde{\mathtt{B}}_{\varepsilon_{y}}
with
\varepsilon_{x},\varepsilon_{y}\in\{0,1\}
; see
[
CJ16
, Section 4]
for details and connections with
[
KOS06
]
. Define

\sqrt{\omega^{2}+2c}=e^{\frac{1}{2}\log(\omega+\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2c})+\frac{1}{2}\log(\omega-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2c})}

for
\omega\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathrm{i}[-\sqrt{2c},\sqrt{2c}]
, where the logarithm in the exponent has arguments in
(-\pi/2,3\pi/2)
. This is the same choice of branch cut given in
[
CJ16
]
. For
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, define

(75)

F_{s}(w)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{1}}\frac{u^{s}}{1+a^{2}+aw(u+u^{-1})}\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{u}.

Introduce

(76)

G(\omega)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2c}}(\omega-\sqrt{\omega^{2}+2c})

where
c=a/(1+a^{2})

and let

(77)

\mu(u)=1-\sqrt{1+c^{2}(u-u^{-1})^{2}}\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{ and }s(u)=1-\mu(u)

with the sign chosen so that
G(1/\omega)=-\mu(-u\mathrm{i})/(c(u+u^{-1}))\mathrm{i}
if we set
u\mathrm{i}=G(\omega)
. With the choice of branch cut, we have
\sqrt{(-w)^{2}+2c}=-\sqrt{w^{2}+2c}
and so
G(-\omega)=-G(\omega)
and for
t\in(\sqrt{2c},1/\sqrt{2c})
,

\begin{split}\sqrt{(\mathrm{i}t)^{2}+2c}&=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{t^{2}-2c}\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and}\\
\hskip 14.22636pt\sqrt{\frac{1}{(\mathrm{i}t)^{2}}+2c}&=\sqrt{(-\mathrm{i}\frac{1}{t})^{2}+2c}=-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{t^{-2}-2c}.\end{split}

With this in mind, we use the notation

(78)

\theta(t)=\frac{t}{\sqrt{t^{2}-2c}}\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{for}\hskip 14.22636ptt\in(\sqrt{2c},1/\sqrt{2c}).

We now bring forward the following theorem from
[
CJ16
]
, which is the formula for
K^{-1}_{a,1}
for the two-periodic Aztec diamond.

Theorem 10.2

(Theorem 2.3 in
[
CJ16
]
)
.

For
n=4m
,
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathtt{W}_{\varepsilon_{1}}
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2})\in\mathtt{B}_{\varepsilon_{2}}
with
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
,
the entries of
K^{-1}_{a,1}
are given by

\begin{split}&K_{a,1}^{-1}(x,y)=\mathbb{K}^{-1}_{1,1}(x,y)-\Big(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n+x_{2},n+y_{1},n+y_{2})\\
&-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{a}(-1)^{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}}\big(\mathcal{B}_{1-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(1/a,n-x_{1},n+x_{2},n-y_{1},n+y_{2})\\
&+\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},1-\varepsilon_{2}}(1/a,n+x_{1},n-x_{2},n+y_{1},n-y_{2})\big)\\
&+\mathcal{B}_{1-\varepsilon_{1},1-\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n-x_{1},n-x_{2},n-y_{1},n-y_{2})\Big),\end{split}

where for
a<r<1/a
, we have

(79)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}^{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}+1}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{r}}\frac{\mathrm{d}u_{1}}{u_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{r}}\frac{\mathrm{d}u_{2}}{u_{2}}\frac{F_{\frac{x_{2}}{2}}(\nu(u_{1}))F_{\frac{y_{1}}{2}}(\nu(u_{2}))}{u_{1}^{\frac{x_{1}-1}{2}}u_{2}^{\frac{y_{2}-1}{2}}}\\
&\times\left(\frac{1}{4c^{2}}u_{1}^{2}u_{2}^{2}(2-\mu(-\mathrm{i}u_{1}))^{2}(2-\mu(-\mathrm{i}u_{2}))^{2}\right)^{m}\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}=0}^{1}Y^{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(u_{1},u_{2}).\end{split}

The function
Y_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}
is defined in
Appendix

B
.

Note that we will sometimes write
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})=\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,(x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2}))
for convenience.

10.2.
Formulas for
K_{a,1}^{-1}
for asymptotic analysis

In this subsection, we give suitable formulas for
K^{-1}_{a,1}
that are amenable to asymptotic analysis. Much of the notation is the same as the one used in
[
CJ16
]
. Define

(80)

{H_{x_{1},x_{2}}(\omega)}=\frac{\omega^{2m}G\left(\omega\right)^{-\frac{x_{1}}{2}}}{G\left(\omega^{-1}\right)^{-\frac{x_{2}}{2}}},

where
-n<x_{k},x_{2}<n
.
Let

(81)

\begin{split}&V_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}=0}^{1}(-1)^{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}+\gamma_{1}(1+\varepsilon_{2})+\gamma_{2}(1+\varepsilon_{1})}G\left(\omega_{1}\right)^{\varepsilon_{1}}G\left(\omega_{2}^{-1}\right)^{\varepsilon_{2}}\\
&\times s\left(G\left(\omega_{1}\right)\right)^{\gamma_{1}}s\left(G\left(\omega_{2}^{-1}\right)\right)^{\gamma_{2}}\left(\mathtt{x}^{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}\left(\omega_{1},\omega_{2}^{-1}\right)-\mathtt{x}^{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}\left(\omega_{1},-\omega_{2}^{-1}\right)\right),\end{split}

(82)

s\left(G\left(\omega\right)\right)=\omega\sqrt{\omega^{-2}+2c}\hskip 5.69054pt\mbox{and}\hskip 5.69054pts\left(G\left(\omega^{-1}\right)\right)=\frac{1}{\omega}\sqrt{\omega^{2}+2c},

with

(83)

\begin{split}&\mathtt{x}^{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}\left(\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\right)=\frac{G\left(\omega_{1}\right)G\left(\omega_{2}\right)}{\prod_{j=1}^{2}\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}+2c}\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{-2}+2c}}\\
&\times\mathtt{y}_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}\left(a,1,G\left(\omega_{1}\right),G\left(\omega_{2}\right)\right)\left(1-\omega_{1}^{2}\omega_{2}^{2}\right),\end{split}

and
\mathtt{y}_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}
is given in
Appendix

B
.
We will also define

(84)

T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\frac{G(\omega_{1})G(\omega_{2}^{-1})}{G(\omega_{1}^{-1})G(\omega_{2})}.

and notice that
T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=T_{G}(\omega_{2},\omega_{1})^{-1}
.

As given in
[
CJ16
, Lemma 3.2]
, there is a remarkably simple formula for
V_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(\omega,\omega)
which is given by

(85)

V_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(\omega,\omega)=\frac{(-1)^{1+h(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})}a^{\varepsilon_{2}}G\left(\omega^{-1}\right)^{h(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})}+a^{1-\varepsilon_{2}}G\left(\omega\right)G\left(\omega^{-1}\right)^{1-h(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})}}{2(1+a^{2})\sqrt{\omega^{2}+2c}\sqrt{\omega^{-2}+2c}}.

Introduce
\omega_{c}=r_{c}e^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{c}}
,
\theta_{c}\in[0,\pi/2]
for some
r_{c}>0
an let
\Gamma_{\omega_{c}}
be the contour

\Gamma_{\omega_{c}}\,:\,[\theta_{c},\pi-\theta_{c}]\cup[\pi+\theta_{c},2\pi-\theta_{c}]\ni\theta\to r_{c}e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}.

For
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathtt{W}_{\varepsilon_{1}}
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2})\in\mathtt{B}_{\varepsilon_{2}}
we
define

(86)

C_{\omega_{c}}(x,y)=\frac{\mathrm{i}^{(x_{2}-x_{1}+y_{1}-y_{2})/2}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{\omega_{c}}}V_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(\omega,\omega)G(\omega)^{\frac{y_{1}-x_{1}-1}{2}}G(\omega^{-1})^{\frac{x_{2}-y_{2}-1}{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega}{\omega}.

The following lemma collects asymptotics for
K^{-1}_{a,1}(x,y)
when the points
x,y
are close to the diagonal in the third quadrant. It gives a starting point for the asymptotic analysis of
K^{-1}_{a,1}
given in this paper. It is pieced together from various statements in
[
CJ16
]
, which we gather in a short proof in
Section

14
. Note that we do not need an explicit form for
S(x,y)
given below, only what is listed below. We have kept the notation, up to a shift, similar to that used in
[
CJ16
]
.

Lemma 10.3
.

For
x=(x_{1},x_{2})=(\lfloor 4m\eta_{1}\rfloor+\overline{x}_{1},\lfloor 4m\eta_{1}\rfloor+\overline{x}_{2})\in\mathtt{W}_{\varepsilon_{1}}
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2})=(\lfloor 4m\eta_{2}\rfloor+\overline{y}_{1},\lfloor 4m\eta_{2}\rfloor+\overline{y}_{2})\in\mathtt{B}_{\varepsilon_{2}}
with
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
where
-1<\eta_{1},\eta_{2}<0
and
|\overline{x}_{1}|,|\overline{x}_{2}|,|\overline{y}_{1}|,|\overline{y}_{2}|\leq Cm^{5/6}
for some
C>0
constant, we have for a constant
\mathtt{c}>0

(87)

K^{-1}_{a,1}(x,y)=\mathbb{K}^{-1}_{1,1}(x,y)-\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,x+(n,n),y+(n,n))+O(e^{-\mathtt{c}n})

where

(88)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n+x_{2},n+y_{1},n+y_{2})=\frac{\mathrm{i}^{\frac{x_{2}-x_{1}+y_{1}-y_{2}}{2}}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{p}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}}{\omega_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{1/p}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\\
&\times\frac{V_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}}\frac{H_{x_{1}+1,x_{2}}(\omega_{1})}{H_{y_{1},y_{2}+1}(\omega_{2})}\end{split}

where
V_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
is given in (
81
). Moreover, we have

(89)

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,x+(n,n),y+(n,n))=C_{1}(x,y)+\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,x+(n,n),y+(n,n))

where for
\sqrt{2c}<p<1
,

\begin{split}&\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,x+(n,n),y+(n,n))=\frac{\mathrm{i}^{\frac{x_{2}-x_{1}+y_{1}-y_{2}}{2}}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{1/p}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}}{\omega_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{p}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\\
&\times\frac{V_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}}\frac{H_{x_{1}+1,x_{2}}(\omega_{1})}{H_{y_{1},y_{2}+1}(\omega_{2})}.\end{split}

We also have that

(90)

\mathbb{K}^{-1}_{1,1}(x,y)=C_{1}(x,y)+S(x,y)

where
S(x,y)
is defined in
[
CJ16
, Equation (2.18)]
. We have that
S(x,y)=0
if
y_{1}-x_{1}>-1
or
x_{2}-y_{2}>-1
. Also, if
f\in\{\pm e_{1},\pm e_{2}\}
and
x\in\mathtt{W}_{\varepsilon_{1}}
, then
S(x,x+f)\not=0
only if
f=e_{2}
and then
S(x,x+e_{2})=-\mathrm{i}a^{\varepsilon_{1}-1}
.

Finally, we need the following lemma, which contains versions of the asymptotic equation (
87
) when the points
x,y
are close to the anti-diagonal or close to the diagonal in quadrants
1
. It is again essentially contained in
[
CJ16
]
, and we give a brief proof in
Section

14
.

Lemma 10.4
.

For
x=(x_{1},x_{2})=(-\lfloor 4m\eta_{1}\rfloor+\overline{x}_{1},\lfloor 4m\eta_{1}\rfloor+\overline{x}_{2})\in\mathtt{W}_{\varepsilon_{1}}
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2})=(-\lfloor 4m\eta_{2}\rfloor+\overline{y}_{1},\lfloor 4m\eta_{2}\rfloor+\overline{y}_{2})\in\mathtt{B}_{\varepsilon_{2}}
with
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
where
-1<\eta_{1},\eta_{2}<0
and
|\overline{x}_{1}|,|\overline{x}_{2}|,|\overline{y}_{1}|,|\overline{y}_{2}|\leq Cm^{5/6}
for some constant
C>0
, we have for a constant
\mathtt{c}>0

K^{-1}_{a,1}(x,y)=\mathbb{K}^{-1}_{1,1}(x,y)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{a}(-1)^{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}}\mathcal{B}_{1-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n-x_{1},n+x_{2},n-y_{1},n+y_{2})+O(e^{-\mathtt{c}n}).

For
x=(x_{1},x_{2})=(\lfloor 4m\eta_{1}\rfloor+\overline{x}_{1},-\lfloor 4m\eta_{1}\rfloor+\overline{x}_{2})\in\mathtt{W}_{\varepsilon_{1}}
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2})=(\lfloor 4m\eta_{2}\rfloor+\overline{y}_{1},-\lfloor 4m\eta_{2}\rfloor+\overline{y}_{2})\in\mathtt{B}_{\varepsilon_{2}}
with
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
where
-1<\eta_{1},\eta_{2}<0
and
|\overline{x}_{1}|,|\overline{x}_{2}|,|\overline{y}_{1}|,|\overline{y}_{2}|\leq Cm^{5/6}
for some constant
C>0
, we have for a constant
\mathtt{c}>0

K^{-1}_{a,1}(x,y)=\mathbb{K}^{-1}_{1,1}(x,y)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{a}(-1)^{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},1-\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n-x_{2},n+y_{1},n-y_{2})+O(e^{-\mathtt{c}n}).

For
x=(x_{1},x_{2})=(-\lfloor 4m\eta_{1}\rfloor+\overline{x}_{1},-\lfloor 4m\eta_{1}\rfloor+\overline{x}_{2})\in\mathtt{W}_{\varepsilon_{1}}
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2})=(-\lfloor 4m\eta_{2}\rfloor+\overline{y}_{1},-\lfloor 4m\eta_{2}\rfloor+\overline{y}_{2})\in\mathtt{B}_{\varepsilon_{2}}
with
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
where
-1<\eta_{1},\eta_{2}<0
and
|\overline{x}_{1}|,|\overline{x}_{2}|,|\overline{y}_{1}|,|\overline{y}_{2}|\leq Cm^{5/6}
for some constant
C>0
, we have for a constant
\mathtt{c}>0

K^{-1}_{a,1}(x,y)=\mathbb{K}^{-1}_{1,1}(x,y)-\mathcal{B}_{1-\varepsilon_{1},1-\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n-x_{1},n-x_{2},n-y_{1},n-y_{2})+O(e^{-\mathtt{c}n}).

10.3.
Scalings

In this subsection, we give the scalings needed for asymptotic analysis.
Recall that
\xi_{c}=-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1-2c}
. Let
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
and
(\tilde{\xi}_{1},\tilde{\xi}_{2})
be on the rough-smooth limit shape curve (given in (
144
)) with
|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|<m^{-\frac{1}{6}}
and
|\tilde{\xi}_{1}-\tilde{\xi}_{2}|<m^{-\frac{1}{6}}
and
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\tilde{\xi}_{1},\tilde{\xi}_{2}<0
. We consider the following scalings for

(91)

\begin{array}[]{l}x=(\lfloor 4m\xi_{1}+4mX\rfloor+1,\lfloor 4m\xi_{2}+4mX\rfloor),\\
y=(\lfloor 4m\tilde{\xi}_{1}+4mY\rfloor,\lfloor 4m\tilde{\xi}_{2}+4mY\rfloor+1).\end{array}

For this, fix
L>0
,
0<\delta<\frac{1}{12}
and
\gamma>0
.

(Smooth region)

(92)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826ptm^{-\frac{1}{6}+\delta}\leq X,Y\leq-\xi_{c}+\gamma

(Smooth region close to rough-smooth boundary)

(93)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826ptm^{-\frac{2}{3}}L\leq X,Y<m^{-1/6+\delta}

(Rough-smooth boundary)

(94)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-m^{-\frac{2}{3}}L\leq X,Y<m^{-\frac{2}{3}}L

(Rough region close to rough-smooth boundary)

(95)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-m^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}\leq X,Y<-m^{-\frac{2}{3}}L.

We will also consider a refined rough-smooth boundary scaling

(96)

\begin{split}&x=\big(\lfloor 4m\xi_{c}\rfloor+2\lfloor\alpha_{x}\lambda_{1}(2m)^{\frac{1}{3}}\rfloor)e_{1}\\
&-\big(2\lfloor\hat{\beta}_{x}\lambda_{2}(2m)^{\frac{2}{3}}+k_{x}\lambda_{2}\log^{2}m\rfloor\big)e_{2}+f_{x}\end{split}

where
|\hat{\beta}_{x}|,|f_{x}|<C
and
-C<\alpha_{x}<\log^{3}m
for a constant
C>0
,
1\leq k_{x}\leq M
where
M=(\log m)^{2}
. We have a similar scaling for
y
with
\hat{\beta}_{y},f_{y},k_{y},\alpha_{y}
. We always choose
f_{x},f_{y}
so that
x
and
y
indicate will always be white and black vertices respectively.

10.4.
Asymptotics of the Correlation Kernel

We now give the refined asymptotics for
K^{-1}_{a,1}
for the scalings given in the previous subsection. To state our results, we need some details on the saddle point functions, giving a brief description of their origins. In what follows below, we ignore integer parts since these are negligible.

First, if we set
x
and
y
as given in (
91
) we have that

(97)

\log H_{x_{1}+1,x_{2}}(\omega)=2mg_{\xi_{1}+X,\xi_{2}+X}(\omega)-\log G(\omega)

and

(98)

\log H_{y_{1},y_{2}+1}(\omega)=2mg_{\tilde{\xi}_{1}+Y,\tilde{\xi}_{2}+Y}(\omega)+\log G(\omega^{-1})

where

(99)

g_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}}(\omega)=\log\omega-\xi_{1}\log G(\omega)+\xi_{2}\log G(\omega^{-1})

and the function
G
is defined in (
76
). Recalling (
88
) and using (
97
) and (
98
), we have

(100)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n+x_{2},n+y_{1},n+y_{2})=\frac{\mathrm{i}^{\frac{x_{2}-x_{1}+y_{1}-y_{2}}{2}}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{p}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}}{\omega_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{1/p}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\\
&\times\frac{V_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}}e^{2mg_{\xi_{1}+X,\xi_{2}+X}(\omega_{1})-\log G(\omega_{1})}e^{-2mg_{\tilde{\xi}_{1}+Y,\tilde{\xi}_{2}+Y}(\omega_{2})-\log G(\omega_{2}^{-1})}.\end{split}

To understand the asymptotic behvavior of this integral, we need to understand the saddle points of
g_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}}
. By differentiating (
99
) we arrive at

(101)

\omega g^{\prime}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}}(\omega)=1+\frac{\xi_{1}\omega}{\sqrt{\omega^{2}+2c}}+\frac{\xi_{2}}{\omega\sqrt{\omega^{-2}+2c}}

and the equation
\omega g^{\prime}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}}(\omega)=0
has at most four roots in
\mathbb{C}\backslash(\mathrm{i}(-\infty,-1/\sqrt{2c}]\cup\mathrm{i}[-\sqrt{2c},\sqrt{2c}]\cup\mathrm{i}[1/\sqrt{2c},\infty))
; see
[
CJ16
, Lemma 3.7]
.
Note that the equation is symmetric under
\omega\mapsto-\omega
and
\omega\mapsto\mathrm{i}\omega
by the choice of square root. We denote
\mathbb{H}
to be the upper half-plane and set
\mathbb{H}_{+}=\{x+y\mathrm{i}:x,y>0\}
to be the first quadrant.

The critical points of
g_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}}
determine the macroscopic region which
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
belongs to. The analysis in
[
CJ16
]
was performed for
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
to make the analysis explicit, showing that the four roots of (
101
) are given by
\omega_{c},\omega^{-1}_{c},-\omega_{c}
and
-\omega_{c}^{-1}
where
\omega_{c}\in\mathbb{H}_{+}\cup\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}\cup[-\sqrt{2c},1]\mathrm{i}
. In this case, they found that
(\xi_{1},\xi_{1})
depends continously on
\omega_{c}
with

•

if
\omega_{c}\in(\sqrt{2c},1)\mathrm{i}
, then
(\xi_{1},\xi_{1})
is in the smooth region (single critical point),

•

if
\omega_{c}=\mathrm{i}
, then
(\xi_{1},\xi_{1})
is at the smooth-rough boundary,

•

if
\omega_{c}\in\mathbb{H}_{+}
with
|\omega_{c}|=1
, then
(\xi_{1},\xi_{1})
is in the rough region (single critical point),

•

if
\omega_{c}=1
, then
(\xi_{1},\xi_{1})
is at the frozen-rough boundary,

•

if
\omega_{c}>1
, the
(\xi_{1},\xi_{1})
is in the frozen region.

Since we work slightly away from the main diagonal in this paper, we adapt some of our notation accordingly. Moreover, we use
the notation that

(102)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\omega_{c,X}\in\mathbb{H}_{+}\cup\partial\mathbb{H}_{+}&\mbox{such that}&g^{\prime}_{\xi_{1}+X,\xi_{2}+X}(\omega_{c,X})=0\\
\tilde{\omega}_{c,Y}\in\mathbb{H}_{+}\cup\partial\mathbb{H}_{+}&\mbox{such that}&g^{\prime}_{\tilde{\xi}_{1}+Y,\tilde{\xi}_{2}+Y}(\tilde{\omega}_{c,Y})=0,\end{array}

where
X
and
Y
are marking the distance from
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
on the limit shape curve at the rough-smooth boundary with
|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|<Cm^{-1/6}
. The point
\omega_{c,X}
will be a perturbation of
\omega_{c}
at
(\xi_{1}+X,\xi_{1}+X)
, whereas
\tilde{\omega}_{c,Y}
will be a perturbation of
-\omega_{c}^{-1}
at
(\xi_{1}+Y,\xi_{1}+Y)
.

Our choice in notation means that
\omega_{c,0}
, that is where
X=0
, is such that
g_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}}^{\prime}(\omega_{c,0})=g_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}}^{\prime\prime}(\omega_{c,0})=0
meaning that
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
is on the limit shape curve. Similarly
\tilde{\omega}_{c,0}
, that is where
Y=0
, is such that
g_{\tilde{\xi}_{1},\tilde{\xi}_{2}}^{\prime}(\tilde{\omega}_{c,0})=g_{\tilde{\xi}_{1},\tilde{\xi}_{2}}^{\prime\prime}(\tilde{\omega}_{c,0})=0
meaning that
(\tilde{\xi}_{1},\tilde{\xi}_{2})
is on the limit shape curve.

Note that the analysis in
[
DK21
]
holds for the whole Aztec diamond. We could have computed refined asymptotic
formulas found in the latter here, due to the relation found in
[
CD23
]
between the inverse Kasteleyn matrix entries and the particle system used in
[
DK21
]
. For simplicity, we refine the asymptotics of the formulas used in
[
CJ16
]
.

We now state asymptotics for
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}
when
x,y
are scaled as in (
91
). The proof of the upcoming theorem is contained in
Section

14.2
.

Theorem 10.5
.

Assume that
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathtt{W}_{\varepsilon_{1}}
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2})\in\mathtt{B}_{\varepsilon_{2}}
with
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
. There exist positive constants
\mathtt{C}_{1},\mathtt{C}_{2},\mathtt{C}_{3}>0
depending only on
a
, such that

(1)

If
x
and
y
have scaling given by (
92
) and
0<\gamma<\mathtt{C}_{2}
, then

(103)

|\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n+x_{2},n+y_{1},n+y_{2})|\leq\mathtt{C}_{1}e^{-\mathtt{C}_{2}m^{\frac{5}{6}+\delta}}.

(2)

If
x
and
y
have scaling given by (
93
), then

(104)

\begin{split}&|\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n+x_{2},n+y_{1},n+y_{2})|\\
&\leq\mathtt{C}_{1}\bigg|\frac{H_{x_{1}+1,x_{2}}(\omega_{c,0})}{H_{y_{1},y_{2}+1}(\tilde{\omega}_{c,0})}\bigg|e^{-m(\mathtt{C}_{2}X^{3/2}-\mathtt{C}_{3}Y^{3/2})}.\end{split}

(3)

If
x
and
y
both have scaling given by (
94
) and
|\xi_{1}-\tilde{\xi}_{1}|<m^{-1/3}
, then

(105)

\begin{split}&|\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n+x_{2},n+y_{1},n+y_{2})|\leq\mathtt{C}_{1}(2m)^{-\frac{1}{3}}.\end{split}

(4)

If
x
and
y
both have scaling given by (
95
),
|\xi_{1}-\tilde{\xi}_{1}|<Cm^{-1}
, and
|X-Y|<Cm^{-1}
for some
C>1
constant, then

(106)

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n+x_{2},n+y_{1},n+y_{2})=C_{\omega_{c,X}}(x,y)+O(m^{-\frac{1}{2}})

where
C_{\omega_{c,X}}
is defined in (
86
) and
\mathrm{Re}\,\omega_{c,X}
is order
\sqrt{X}
.

Remark 10.1
.

If one of
x
and
y
have scaling (
92
), then we also have the bound given in (
103
). The proof of this follows by the same saddle point analysis for one of the variables in the proof of (
103
) (and another saddle point analysis for the other). We omit this computation.

By relying on the analysis in
[
CJ16
]
, we give a shortened proof of the above result in
Section

14
.

Using the notation from
[
CJ16
]
, let

\mathtt{g}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\mathrm{i}\left(\sqrt{a^{2}+1}+a\right)}{1-a}&\mbox{if }(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=(0,0)\\
\frac{\sqrt{a^{2}+1}+a-1}{\sqrt{2a}(1-a)}&\mbox{if }(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=(0,1)\\
-\frac{\sqrt{a^{2}+1}+a-1}{\sqrt{2a}(1-a)}&\mbox{if }(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=(1,0)\\
\frac{\mathrm{i}\left(\sqrt{a^{2}+1}-1\right)}{(1-a)a}&\mbox{if }(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=(1,1).\end{array}\right.

The following theorem is an extension of
[
CJ16
, Theorem 2.7]
. The extension requires justification, which is done in
Section

14.3
.

Theorem 10.6
.

If
x
and
y
have scaling defined by (
96
), then we have

K_{a,1}^{-1}(x,y)=\mathbb{K}^{-1}_{1,1}(x,y)-\mathbb{K}_{A}(x,y)

where
\mathbb{K}_{A}(x,y)=\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n+x_{2},n+y_{1},n+y_{2})(1+o(1))
as
n\to\infty
. Moreover, we have that as
n\to\infty

\begin{split}&\mathbb{K}^{-1}_{1,1}(x,y)=\mathrm{i}^{y_{1}-x_{1}+1}|G(\mathrm{i})|^{\frac{-2-x_{1}+x_{2}+y_{1}-y_{2}}{2}}c_{0}\mathtt{g}_{\varepsilon_{x},\varepsilon_{y}}\\
&\times e^{\alpha_{y}\hat{\beta}_{y}-\alpha_{x}\hat{\beta}_{x}-\frac{2}{3}(\hat{\beta}_{x}^{3}-\hat{\beta}_{y}^{3})}(2m)^{-\frac{1}{3}}(\phi_{\hat{\beta}_{x},\hat{\beta}_{y}}(\alpha_{x}+\hat{\beta}_{x}^{2},\alpha_{y}+\hat{\beta}_{y}^{2})+o(1))\end{split}

and

\begin{split}&\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n+x_{2},n+y_{1},n+y_{2})=\mathrm{i}^{y_{1}-x_{1}+1}|G(\mathrm{i})|^{\frac{-2-x_{1}+x_{2}+y_{1}-y_{2}}{2}}c_{0}\mathtt{g}_{\varepsilon_{x},\varepsilon_{y}}\\
&\times e^{\alpha_{y}\hat{\beta}_{y}-\alpha_{x}\hat{\beta}_{x}-\frac{2}{3}(\hat{\beta}_{x}^{3}-\hat{\beta}_{y}^{3})}(2m)^{-\frac{1}{3}}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\hat{\beta}_{x},\alpha_{x}+\hat{\beta}_{x}^{2};\hat{\beta}_{y},\alpha_{y}+\hat{\beta}_{y}^{2})+o(1)).\end{split}

Here the notation
\tilde{\mathcal{A}},\phi_{s,t}
for the extended Airy kernel is as in (
23
), (
24
).
Note that the above theorem is stated in a stronger form than needed since it considers different values of
\hat{\beta}_{x}
and
\hat{\beta}_{y}
whereas in the sequel, we only need the case
\hat{\beta}_{x}=\hat{\beta}_{y}
.

11.
Proofs of Lemmas
5.6
and
5.7

In this section, we give the smooth phase coupling resuls, that is the proofs of
5.6
and
5.7
. To prove these results, we introduce notation similar to the one used in
[
BCJ22
]
.

Consider
\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},k}
defined in
5.6
for
k=1
and
5.7
for
k=2
. We denote by
\partial\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},k}
to be vertices which share edges that cross the boundary of the box
\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},k}
. Letting
\mathtt{W}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},k})
denote all white vertices in
\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},k}
, we write
\mathtt{W}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},k})=\{w_{1},\dots,w_{R}\}
. Set
f_{1}=e_{1},f_{2}=e_{2},f_{3}=-e_{1}
, and
f_{4}=-e_{2}
and
[N]=\{1,\dots,N\}
for a positive integer
N
. A
configuration
in
\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},k}
is a set of edges:

(107)

({w}_{1},{w}_{1}+f_{s_{1}}),\dots,({w}_{R},{w}_{R}+f_{s_{R}})

where
s_{j}\in[4],1\leq j\leq R
. We can think of (
107
) as the event that all these edges are covered by dimers. For
\overline{s}\in[4]^{R}
, we let
\overline{s}
denote the configuration (
107
). Let

A_{ij}(\overline{s})=K_{a,1}({w}_{i},{w}_{i})\mathbb{K}^{-1}_{1,1}({w}_{i},{w}_{j}+f_{s_{j}})

and

C_{ij}(\overline{s})=-K_{a,1}({w}_{i},{w}_{i})\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n+x_{2},n+y_{1},n+y_{2})(1+o(1));

where
w_{i}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathtt{W}_{\varepsilon_{1}}
and
{w}_{j}+f_{s_{j}}=(y_{1},y_{2})\in\mathtt{B}_{\varepsilon_{2}}
and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
.

Then,
\mathbb{P}_{a,n}
induces the following measure on
[4]^{R}
:

(108)

p^{a,n}_{\mathrm{Az}}\big(\overline{s}|\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},k}\big)=\det\big(A_{ij}(\overline{s})+C_{ij}(\overline{s})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq R}

while
\mathbb{P}_{a}
induces the following measure on
[4]^{R}

(109)

p_{a}\big(\overline{s}|\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},k}\big)=\det(A_{ij}(\overline{s}))_{1\leq i,j\leq R}.

Note that if
w_{j}+f_{s_{j}}=w_{k}+f_{s_{k}}
for
j\not=k
, then (
108
) and (
109
) both give zero, so configurations with overlaps have probability zero.

Proof of
5.6
.

The proof is the same as
5.5
given in
[
BCJ22
, Proposition 4.5]
but replacing the bound for
|C_{ij}(\overline{s})|
by the bound given in (
103
) for all
i,j
and for all
\overline{s}
such that all faces
x
in
\Lambda_{\mathrm{sm},1}
have scaling (
92
). The result can be equivalently seen as replacing
n^{-1/3}
in
5.5
by the bound given in (
103
) and accounting for the size of the box (which is
o(n^{-1/2})
) in the statement of
5.6
.

The statement on the rotations follows from using
10.4
, the same asymptotic bounds given in (
103
), and the above argument. Indeed, these asymptotic bounds holds for any of the rotations considered here because the saddle point function from
10.4
has the same form as the one considered in
Section

10.4
.
∎

Proof of
5.7
.

Like in the proof of
5.6
we give a shortened proof by relying on the proof of
5.5
given in
[
BCJ22
, Proposition 4.5]
. The main difference to the proof this lemma compared with
5.6
is that we need to adjust for the additional factor seen in the estimate (
104
). We can remove this by showing this additional factor becomes a conjugation factor multiplied by another factor.

Indeed, for
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathtt{W}_{\varepsilon_{1}}
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2})\in\mathtt{B}_{\varepsilon_{2}}
and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
, we can write

\begin{split}&K_{a,1}(x,x)(\mathbb{K}_{1,1}^{-1}(x,y)-\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n+x_{2},n+y_{1},n+y_{2})(1+o(1)))\\
&=\frac{H_{x_{1}+1,x_{2}+1}(\omega_{c,0})}{H_{y_{1}+1,y_{2}+1}(\tilde{\omega}_{c,0})}\bigg(\frac{H_{y_{1}+1,y_{2}+1}(\tilde{\omega}_{c,0})}{H_{x_{1}+1,x_{2}+1}(\omega_{c,0})}K_{a,1}(x,x)\mathbb{K}_{1,1}^{-1}(x,y)\\
&-\frac{H_{y_{1}+1,y_{2}+1}(\tilde{\omega}_{c,0})}{H_{x_{1}+1,x_{2}+1}(\omega_{c,0})}K_{a,1}(x,x)\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n+x_{1},n+x_{2},n+y_{1},n+y_{2})(1+o(1))\bigg).\end{split}

We now proceed as before in the proof of
5.5
, with
A_{ij}(\cdot)
given by the first term on the right side of the above equation inside the parenthesis and with
C_{ij}(\cdot)
given by the second term on the right side of the above equation inside the parenthesis. The result follows by noting the additional factor
\frac{H_{x_{1}+1,x_{2}+1}(\omega_{c,0})}{H_{y_{1}+1,y_{2}+1}(\tilde{\omega}_{c,0})}
outside the parenthesis becomes a conjugation factor, the bound from (
104
) which gives an exponential bound for
C_{ij}(\cdot)
, the fact that
\frac{H_{x_{1}+1,x_{2}+1}(\omega_{c,0})}{H_{y_{1}+1,y_{2}+1}(\tilde{\omega}_{c,0})}=G(\omega_{c,0})^{\frac{1}{2}}G(\tilde{\omega}_{c,0})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{H_{x_{1}+1,x_{2}}(\omega_{c,0})}{H_{y_{1},y_{2}+1}(\tilde{\omega}_{c,0})}
from (
80
),and that
A_{ij}(\cdot)
as chosen above, is the smooth phase correlation kernel with a conjugation factor of the form
\frac{H_{y_{1}+1,y_{2}+1}(\tilde{\omega}_{c,0})}{H_{x_{1}+1,x_{2}+1}(\omega_{c,0})}
. Analogous to the proof of
5.6
, the exponential bound is given by (
104
) (after multiplying by
\frac{H_{y_{1}+1,y_{2}+1}(\tilde{\omega}_{c,0})}{H_{x_{1}+1,x_{2}+1}(\omega_{c,0})}
).
∎

12.
Proof of Expected Height Results

In this section, we give the proofs of the expected height function results, namely, the proofs of
5.1
,
5.3
and
5.4
.

12.1.
Proof of
5.1

To prove
5.1
, we need the following result that is a surprising consequence of the explicit formulas for
K^{-1}_{a,1}
. Once this result is established,
5.1
follows readily.

Lemma 12.1
.

Let
k,\ell
be non-negative integers with
-n+2k+2\ell<n
. Then
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(-n+2k+2\ell,-n+2\ell)-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(-n+2k,-n)
is invariant under the map
a\mapsto a^{-1}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
(-n+2k+1,-n+1)
is an
a
-face. The same computation holds if
(-n+2k+1,-n+1)
is a
b
-face.
Using the relationship between the height function and dimer coverings, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(-n+2k+2\ell,-n+2\ell)-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(-n+2k,-n)=4\sum_{s=0}^{\ell-1}\sum_{\varepsilon=0}^{1}(-1)^{\varepsilon}\\
&\big(\mathbb{P}[\mbox{Dimer covers the edge}\\
&((-n+2k+2s+1,-n+2s+2\varepsilon),(-n+2k+2s+2\varepsilon,-n+2s+1))]-1/4\big).\end{split}

We can write the above formula using the inverse Kasteleyn matrix using
10.1
and (
73
) which gives

(110)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(-n+2k+2\ell,-n+2\ell)-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(-n+2k,-n)=4a\mathrm{i}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell-1}\sum_{\varepsilon=0}^{1}(-1)^{\varepsilon}\\
&K^{-1}_{a,1}((-n+2k+2s+1,-n+2s+2\varepsilon),(-n+2k+2s+2\varepsilon,-n+2s+1)).\end{split}

We substitute in the formula for
K_{a,1}^{-1}
given in
10.2
to get that the above equation equals

(111)

\begin{split}&-4a\mathrm{i}\sum_{s=0}^{\ell-1}\sum_{\varepsilon=0}^{1}(-1)^{\varepsilon}\big(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(a,2k+2s+1,2s+2\varepsilon,2k+2s+2\varepsilon,2s+1)\\
&-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{{a}}\mathcal{B}_{1-\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(a^{-1},2n-2k-2s-1,2s+2\varepsilon,2n-2k-2s-2\varepsilon,2s+1)\\
&-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{{a}}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon}(a^{-1},2k+2s+1,2n-2s-2\varepsilon,2k+2s+2\varepsilon,2n-2s-1)\\
&+\mathcal{B}_{1-\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon}(a,2n-2k-2s-1,2n-2s-2\varepsilon,2n-2k-2s-2\varepsilon,2n-2s-1)\big).\end{split}

In the above equation, we have used

\sum_{\varepsilon=0}^{1}(-1)^{\varepsilon}\mathbb{K}^{-1}_{1,1}((-n+2k+2s+1,-n+2s+2\varepsilon),(-n+2k+2s+2\varepsilon,-n+2s+1))=0.

which follows by a computation (or since the full-plane smooth phase is flat in expectation).

We want to show that (
111
) is invariant under
a\mapsto a^{-1}
. We show that

(112)

-a\mathrm{i}\sum_{\varepsilon=0}^{1}(-1)^{\varepsilon}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(a,2k+2s+1,2s+2\varepsilon,2k+2s+2\varepsilon,2s+1)

is invariant under
a\mapsto a^{-1}
since the other three cases are equivalent and this will show that (
111
) is invariant under
a\mapsto a^{-1}
. Note that each term in the above summand is not invariant under the map
a\mapsto a^{-1}
. We use the formula for
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}
given in (
79
) to get that (
112
) equals

\begin{split}&-\frac{a}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{r}}\frac{\mathrm{d}u_{1}}{u_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{r}}\frac{\mathrm{d}u_{2}}{u_{2}}\frac{F_{s}(\nu(u_{1}))F_{s+k}(\nu(u_{2}))}{u_{1}^{k+s}u_{2}^{s}}\\
&\times\Bigg(\frac{1}{4c^{2}}u_{1}^{2}u_{2}^{2}(2-\mu(-u_{1}\mathrm{i}))^{2}(2-\mu(-u_{2}\mathrm{i}))^{2}\Bigg)^{m}\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}=0}^{1}Y_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{0,0}(u_{1},u_{2})\\
&-\frac{a}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{r}}\frac{\mathrm{d}u_{1}}{u_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{r}}\frac{\mathrm{d}u_{2}}{u_{2}}\frac{F_{s+1}(\nu(u_{1}))F_{k+s+1}(\nu(u_{2}))}{u_{1}^{k+s}u_{2}^{s}}\\
&\times\Bigg(\frac{1}{4c^{2}}u_{1}^{2}u_{2}^{2}(2-\mu(-u_{1}\mathrm{i}))^{2}(2-\mu(-u_{2}\mathrm{i}))^{2}\Bigg)^{m}\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}=0}^{1}Y_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{1,1}(u_{1},u_{2}).\end{split}

We combine both of the above integrals and factor. Since
\mu
and
c
are invariant under the map
a\mapsto a^{-1}
, to show that the above expression is invariant under
a\mapsto a^{-1}
, we only need to show that the following expression

(113)

\begin{split}&-\mathrm{i}aF_{s}(\nu(u_{1}))F_{s+k}(\nu(u_{2}))\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}=0}^{1}Y_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{0,0}(u_{1},u_{2})\\
&-\mathrm{i}aF_{s+1}(\nu(u_{1}))F_{s+k+1}(\nu(u_{2}))\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}=0}^{1}Y_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{1,1}(u_{1},u_{2})\end{split}

is invariant under the map
a\mapsto a^{-1}
. From
[
CJ16
, Lemma 2.2]
, we have that

F_{s}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}\omega}{\sqrt{2c}}\bigg)=\frac{\mathrm{i}^{|s|}}{(1+a^{2})\omega\sqrt{\omega^{-2}+2c}}G(\omega^{-1})^{|s|}

for
\omega\in\mathbb{C}\backslash(\mathrm{i}(-\infty,1/\sqrt{2c}]\cup[1/\sqrt{2c},\infty))
and
\mathrm{i}\omega=\sqrt{2c}\nu(u)
, we see that (
113
) becomes

\begin{split}&-\mathrm{i}aF_{s}(\nu(u_{1}))F_{s+k}(\nu(u_{2}))\Bigg(\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}=0}^{1}Y_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{0,0}(u_{1},u_{2})\\
&-\mathrm{i}^{2}\Bigg(-\frac{\mu(-\mathrm{i}u_{1})}{c(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})}\Bigg)\Bigg(-\frac{\mu(-\mathrm{i}u_{2})}{c(u_{2}+u_{2}^{-1})}\Bigg)\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}=0}^{1}Y_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{1,1}(u_{1},u_{2})\Bigg)\end{split}

which we want to show is invariant under the map
a\mapsto a^{-1}
. We simplify the above equation and use the definition of
\mu
and
s
found in (
77
), we arrive at

\begin{split}&-\mathrm{i}\frac{F_{s}(\nu(u_{1}))F_{s+k}(\nu(u_{2}))}{c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})(u_{2}+u_{2}^{-1})}a\Bigg(c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})(u_{2}+u_{2}^{-1})\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}=0}^{1}Y_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{0,0}(u_{1},u_{2})\\
&+(1-s(-\mathrm{i}u_{1}))(1-s(-\mathrm{i}u_{1}))\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}=0}^{1}Y_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{1,1}(u_{1},u_{2})\Bigg),\end{split}

which we want to show is invariant under the map
a\mapsto a^{-1}
. We use the formula for
Y_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}
given in (
145
) and note that
(1+a^{2})F_{s}(\cdot)
, from (
75
), is invariant under the map
a\mapsto a^{-1}
. By these observations, to show that the above equation is invariant under the map
a\mapsto a^{-1}
, we need to show that

(114)

\begin{split}&ac^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})(u_{2}+u_{2}^{-1})\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}=0}^{1}(-1)^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}}s(-\mathrm{i}u_{1})^{\gamma_{1}}s(\mathrm{i}u_{2})^{\gamma_{2}}\mathtt{y}_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})\\
&-a\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}=0}^{1}s(-\mathrm{i}u_{1})^{\gamma_{1}}s(-\mathrm{i}u_{2})^{\gamma_{2}}(1-s(-\mathrm{i}u_{1}))(1-s(-\mathrm{i}u_{2})^{1})\mathtt{y}_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})\end{split}

is invariant under the map
a\mapsto a^{-1}
. We expand out the above equation using the definition of
s(\cdot)
given in (
77
) and noting that
s(-\mathrm{i}u_{1})^{2}=1-c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})^{2}
. We can then expand out in terms of coefficients of
s(-\mathrm{i}u_{1})
and
s(-\mathrm{i}u_{2})
. The coefficient of
s(-\mathrm{i}u_{1})^{0}s(-\mathrm{i}u_{2})^{0}
in (
114
) is given by

(115)

\begin{split}&a\mathtt{y}_{0,0}^{0,0}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})(u_{2}+u_{2}^{-1})-au_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{0,0}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})\\
&+a(1-c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})^{2})(1-c^{2}(u_{2}+u_{2}^{-1})^{2})u_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{1,1}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})\\
&-a(1-c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})^{2})u_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{1,0}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})\\
&-a(1-c^{2}(u_{2}+u_{2}^{-1})^{2})u_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{0,1}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2}),\end{split}

the coefficient of
s(-\mathrm{i}u_{1})^{1}s(-\mathrm{i}u_{2})^{1}
in (
114
) is given by

(116)

\begin{split}&a\mathtt{y}_{1,1}^{0,0}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})(u_{2}+u_{2}^{-1})-au_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{0,0}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})\\
&-au_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{0,1}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})-au_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{1,0}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})+au_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{1,1}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})\end{split}

the coefficient of
s(-\mathrm{i}u_{1})^{1}s(-\mathrm{i}u_{2})^{0}
in (
114
) is given by

(117)

\begin{split}&a\mathtt{y}_{1,0}^{0,0}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})(u_{2}+u_{2}^{-1})-au_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{0,0}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})\\
&-au_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{1,0}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})+a(1-c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})^{2})u_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{0,1}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})\\
&-a(1-c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})^{2})u_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{1,1}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2}),\end{split}

and the coefficient of
s(-\mathrm{i}u_{1})^{0}s(-\mathrm{i}u_{2})^{1}
in (
114
) is given by

(118)

\begin{split}&a\mathtt{y}_{0,1}^{0,0}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})(u_{2}+u_{2}^{-1})-au_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{0,0}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})\\
&-au_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{0,1}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})+a(1-c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})^{2})u_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{1,0}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2})\\
&-a(1-c^{2}(u_{1}+u_{1}^{-1})^{2})u_{1}u_{2}\mathtt{y}_{1,1}^{1,1}(-\mathrm{i}u_{1},-\mathrm{i}u_{2}).\end{split}

Using computer algebra (or an explicit computation by hand), each of (
115
), (
116
), (
117
), and (
118
) are invariant under the map
a\mapsto a^{-1}
and we can conclude that (
112
) and (
111
) are invariant under the map
a\mapsto a^{-1}
as required. ∎

We are now in the position to prove
5.1
.

Proof of
5.1
.

Let
k,\ell
be as in
12.1
. In the proof of the lemma, we let
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{a}=\mathcal{H}_{n}
to make explicit the height function dependence on
a
.

First, using that the boundary faces have deterministic heights, see
Section

2.2
, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{a}(-n+2k+2\ell,-n+2\ell)&=-n+2k+\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{a}(-n+2k+2\ell,-n+2\ell)\\
&-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{a}(-n+2k,-n).\end{split}

Similarly,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{a}(n-2k-2\ell,-n+2\ell)&=n-2k+\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{a}(n-2k-2\ell,-n+2\ell)\\
&-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{a}(n-2k,-n).\end{split}

Next, by symmetry and a gauge transformation (multiplying all the white vertices by
a
),
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{a}(n-2k-2\ell,-n+2\ell)-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{a}(n-2k,-n)
has the same distribution as
-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{1/a}(-n+2k+2\ell,-n+2\ell)+\mathcal{H}_{n}^{1/a}(-n+2k,-n)
. Since

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{1/a}(-n+2k+2\ell,-n+2\ell)-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{1/a}(-n+2k,-n)\\
&=\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{a}(-n+2k+2\ell,-n+2\ell)-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{a}(-n+2k,-n)\end{split}

by
12.1
, it follows that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}^{a}_{n}(n-2k-2\ell,-n+2\ell)=n-2k-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}^{a}_{n}(-n+2k+2\ell,-n+2\ell)\\
&-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}^{a}_{n}(-n+2k,-n)=-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}^{a}_{n}(-n+2k+2\ell,-n+2\ell).\end{split}

The other three equations follow similarly (or from applying reflection symmetries and gauge transformations). The final statement follows from setting
i=0
or
j=0
in the chain of equalities.
∎

12.2.
Proofs of
5.3
and
5.4

Here, we give the proof of
5.3
and
5.4
. Both of these results involve the refined asymptotic analysis of
K^{-1}_{a,1}
.

Suppose that
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
is on the rough-smooth limit curve (
144
) and that
X
is defined by (
92
), (
93
), (
94
) or (
95
). We denote

J_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},X}=(\lfloor 4m\xi_{1}+4mX\rfloor,\lfloor 4m\xi_{2}+4mX\rfloor)

which is a face in the Aztec diamond graph. We prove the following result in which the proof of
5.3
will follow.

Lemma 12.2
.

Let
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
be on the limit shape curve (
144
) with
|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|<m^{-\frac{1}{6}}
and let
m^{-\frac{1}{3}}\log^{2}m\leq R<m^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}
for
0<\delta<\frac{1}{12}
fixed. Then, for a constant
\mathtt{C}_{1}>0
we have as
m\to\infty

\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(J_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},-R})=-\mathtt{C}_{1}R^{\frac{3}{2}}m+o(m^{2\delta}).

Proof of
12.2
.

First, without loss of generality we may assume that
J_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},-R}
is a
c
-face, since the estimate for other faces can be built from the
c
-face estimate and the fact that height differences on adjacent faces are at most
4
. Next, let
u\in[m^{5/6},m^{5/6}+2]
be such that
\mathtt{J}=J_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},u}
is also a
c
-face. Then, we have that

\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(J_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},-R})=\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(J_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},-R})-\mathcal{H}_{n}(\mathtt{J}))-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{H}_{n}(\mathtt{J}),

where we have an exponential bound on the last term by
5.11
. Note here that
5.11
does not rely on either
5.3
and
5.4
as an input, so this is not a circular argument.

Using the relationship between dimer coverings and height functions as well as
10.1
, similarly to (
110
) we have that

(119)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(J_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},-R})-\mathcal{H}_{n}(\mathtt{J}))\\
&=4\sum_{k=0}^{\mathtt{m}}\sum_{\varepsilon=0}^{1}(-1)^{\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}\big[\mbox{Dimer covers the edge }(x_{k,\varepsilon},y_{k,\varepsilon})\big]\\
&=-4a\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathtt{m}}\sum_{\varepsilon=0}^{1}(-1)^{\varepsilon}K^{-1}_{a,1}(x_{k,\varepsilon},y_{k,\varepsilon}).\end{split}

where

\displaystyle x_{k,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=(\lfloor 4m\xi_{1}-4mR\rfloor+2k+1,\lfloor 4m\xi_{1}-4mR\rfloor+2k+2\varepsilon),

\displaystyle y_{k,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=(\lfloor 4m\xi_{1}-4mR\rfloor+2k+2\varepsilon,\lfloor 4m\xi_{1}-4mR\rfloor+2k+1).

and
\mathtt{m}=2m^{5/6}+2Rm+O(1)
.
We use the formula
K_{a,1}^{-1}
given in
10.2
, noting that the smooth phase is flat in expectation, and the bounds given in the first equation of
10.3
, which gives that (
119
) equals

\begin{split}-4a\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathtt{m}}\sum_{\varepsilon=0}^{1}(-1)^{\varepsilon}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}\big(a,&(n,n)+x_{k,\varepsilon},(n,n)+y_{k,\varepsilon}\big)+O(e^{-\mathtt{C}_{1}n})\end{split}

for some positive constant
\mathtt{C}_{1}
.
We split the above sum into three sums, namely

(120)

\begin{split}-4a\mathrm{i}\sum_{j=0}^{2}\sum_{k=\mathtt{m}_{j}}^{\mathtt{m_{j+1}}}\sum_{\varepsilon=0}^{1}(-1)^{\varepsilon}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}\big(a,&(n,n)+x_{k,\varepsilon},(n,n)+y_{k,\varepsilon}\big)+O(e^{-\mathtt{C}_{1}n})\end{split}

where
\mathtt{m}_{3}=\mathtt{m}
,
\mathtt{m}_{2}=\lfloor 2(m^{1/3}\log^{2}m+Rm)\rfloor
,
\mathtt{m}_{1}=\lfloor 2(-m^{1/3}\log^{2}m+Rm)\rfloor
and
\mathtt{m}_{0}=0
. We consider the sum with respect to
j
separately.

By statements 1 and 2 in
10.5
, we have that the summands when
j=2
in the above equation are at most
O(\exp(-c\log^{3}m))
for some constant
c>0
, while the sum is over a polynomial in
m
, and so the
j=2
sum above is
O(e^{-\mathtt{C}_{2}\log^{3}n})
as
m\to\infty
.
By statement 3 in
10.5
, we have that the summands when
j=1
are of order
m^{-\frac{1}{3}}
and so the second sum is
o(m^{\delta})
as
m\to\infty
.
It remains to consider the sum
j=0
in the above equation. Notice that we can apply Statement 4 in
10.5
and we get that the error term, when summed is
o(m^{2\delta})
as
m\to\infty
. Putting everything together, we get that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(J_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},-R})-\mathcal{H}_{n}(\mathtt{J}))\\
&=-4a\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathtt{m_{1}}}\sum_{\varepsilon=0}^{1}(-1)^{\varepsilon}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}\big(a,(n,n)+x_{k,\varepsilon},(n,n)+y_{k,\varepsilon}\big)+o(m^{\delta})\\
&=-4a\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathtt{m_{1}}}C_{\omega_{c,X(k)}}((1,0),(0,1))-C_{\omega_{c,X(k)}}((1,2),(2,1))+o(m^{2\delta})\end{split}

where the error term is out of the sum, and
\omega_{c,X(k)}
is defined through (
106
) since
X(k)=2k-4R
.
We have that

\begin{split}&C_{\omega_{c,X(\ell)}}((1,2),(2,1))-C_{\omega_{c,X(\ell)}}((1,0),(0,1))\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{\omega_{c,X(k)}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega}{\omega}(\mathrm{i}V_{1,1}(\omega,\omega)-\mathrm{i}^{-1}V_{0,0}(\omega,\omega)(G(\omega)G(\omega^{-1}))^{-1})\\
&=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{\omega_{c,X(k)}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega}{\omega}\frac{1}{2(1+a^{2})\sqrt{\omega^{2}+2c}\sqrt{\omega^{-2}+2c}}(G(\omega)G(\omega^{-1})-(G(\omega)G(\omega^{-1}))^{-1})\\
&=-\frac{\mathrm{i}a}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{\omega_{c,X(k)}}}{\mathrm{d}\omega}\frac{1}{\omega^{2}\sqrt{\omega^{-2}+2c}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega^{2}+2c}}\end{split}

where we have used the formula for
C_{\omega_{c,X(k)}}
given in (
86
) and the formula for
V_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(\omega,\omega)
given in (
85
). The above integral can be computed explicitly, is negative and is bounded below by
-\mathtt{C}_{1}|\Gamma_{\omega_{c,X(k)}}|
, the length of the contour which is approximately equal to
4\mathrm{Re}\,\omega_{c,X(k)}
. The exact value is not important here. We have that
|\Gamma_{\omega_{c,X(k)}}|
is order
\sqrt{X(k)}
and since the sum contains approximately
Rm
terms, we get the desired expansion.
∎

Proof of
5.3
.

Set
\delta=1/30
and
x=Rm
in
12.2
and the result follows for
x\in[-n^{8/15},-\mathtt{C}n^{1/3}(\log n)^{2}]
for some constant
\mathtt{C}>0
. For
x\in[-\mathtt{C}n^{1/3}(\log n)^{2},2n^{1/3}(\log n)^{2}]
, we can follow the proof of
12.2
and get an expected height bound of
O(\log^{2}n)
; see the proof of
12.2
up to (
120
) along with the discussion in the following paragraph for the summands
j=1
and
j=2
.
∎

The next lemma is used in the proof of
5.4
.

Lemma 12.3
.

Recall that
\xi_{c}=-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1-2c}
and let
-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1+2c}<X<X^{\prime}
where
X^{\prime}=-m^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}
for
0<\delta<\frac{1}{12}
fixed.
Then, we have

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}_{n}(J_{\xi_{c},\xi_{c},X})]=\big(p_{1}(\theta_{c})-p_{1}(\theta_{c}^{\prime})\big)2m+o(m^{\frac{1}{2}})

where

p_{1}(\theta_{c})=\frac{2}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\big(g_{X,X}(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{c}})-g_{X,X}(e^{-\mathrm{i}\theta_{c}})+g_{X,X}(-e^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{c}})-g_{X,X}(-e^{-\mathrm{i}\theta_{c}})\big).

where
\theta_{c}
is defined through
g^{\prime}_{X,X}(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{c}})=0
and
\theta_{c}^{\prime}
is defined through
g^{\prime}_{X^{\prime},X^{\prime}}(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{c}^{\prime}})=0
; see (
101
).

Proof.

We compute the expected height difference between
J_{\xi_{c},\xi_{c},X}
and
J_{\xi_{c},\xi_{c},X^{\prime}}
. This will give the expected height at
J_{\xi_{c},\xi_{c},X}
since we have from
12.2
, the expected height at
J_{\xi_{c},\xi_{c},X^{\prime}}
. By following a similar reasoning as in (
119
) and its aftermath, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(J_{\xi_{c},\xi_{c},X})-\mathcal{H}_{n}(J_{\xi_{c},\xi_{c},X^{\prime}}))=-4a\mathrm{i}\sum_{\ell=\lceil mX\rceil}^{\lfloor mX^{\prime}\rfloor}\sum_{\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}}(-1)^{\varepsilon}\\
&K_{a,1}^{-1}((\lfloor 4m\rfloor\xi_{c}+2\ell+1,\lfloor 4m\xi_{c}\rfloor+2\ell+2\varepsilon),(\lfloor 4m\rfloor\xi_{c}+2\ell+2\varepsilon,\lfloor 4m\xi_{c}\rfloor+2\ell+1))\\
&=-4a\mathrm{i}\sum_{\ell=\lceil mX\rceil}^{\lfloor mX^{\prime}\rfloor}\sum_{\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}}(-1)^{\varepsilon}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}\bigg(a,(n,n)+(\lfloor 4m\rfloor\xi_{c}+2\ell+1,\lfloor 4m\xi_{c}\rfloor+2\ell+2\varepsilon),\\
&(n,n)+(\lfloor 4m\rfloor\xi_{c}+2\ell+2\varepsilon,\lfloor 4m\xi_{c}\rfloor+2\ell+1)\bigg)+O(e^{-\mathtt{C}_{1}n})\end{split}

where
\mathtt{C}_{1}
is a positive constant. We next use (
88
) to write

(121)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(J_{\xi_{c},\xi_{c},X})-\mathcal{H}_{n}(J_{\xi_{c},\xi_{c},X^{\prime}}))=\sum_{\ell=\lfloor m\xi_{c}+mX\rfloor}^{\lfloor m\xi_{c}+mX^{\prime}\rfloor}\frac{4a}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{p}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}}{\omega_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{1/p}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\frac{1}{\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}}\\
&\times\bigg(\frac{H_{2\ell+2,2\ell}(\omega_{1})}{H_{2\ell,2\ell+2}(\omega_{2})}\mathrm{i}^{-1}V_{0,0}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})-\frac{H_{2\ell+2,2\ell+2}(\omega_{1})}{H_{2\ell+2,2\ell+2}(\omega_{2})}\mathrm{i}V_{1,1}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\bigg)+O(e^{-\mathtt{C}_{1}n})\\
&=\frac{4a}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{p}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}}{\omega_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{1/p}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\frac{1}{\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}}\sum_{\ell=-\lfloor m\xi_{c}+mX^{\prime}\rfloor}^{-\lfloor m+\xi_{c}mX\rfloor}\frac{\omega_{1}^{2m}}{\omega_{2}^{2m}}T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})^{\ell}\\
\times&\frac{1}{G(\omega_{1})G(\omega_{2}^{-1})}\bigg(V_{0,0}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})+G(\omega_{1}^{-1})G(\omega_{2})V_{1,1}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\bigg)+O(e^{-\mathtt{C}_{1}n})\end{split}

where we have used (
80
) and
T_{G}
defined in (
84
).
Let

V(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\frac{\omega_{2}^{-1}(\omega_{2}^{2}-\omega_{1}^{2})}{1-T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}\frac{V_{0,0}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})+G(\omega_{1}^{-1})G(\omega_{2})V_{1,1}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{G(\omega_{1})G(\omega_{2}^{-1})}.

Then, evaluating the geometric sum in (
121
) and using the above definitions gives

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(J_{\xi_{c},\xi_{c},X})-\mathcal{H}_{n}(J_{\xi_{c},\xi_{c},X^{\prime}}))=\frac{4a}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{p}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}}{\omega_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{p^{-1}}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\frac{\omega_{2}V_{F}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{(\omega_{2}-\omega_{1})^{2}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})}\\
&\times\frac{\omega_{1}^{2m}}{\omega_{2}^{2m}}\big(1-T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})^{-\lfloor mX\rfloor+\lfloor mX^{\prime}\rfloor}\big)T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})^{-\lfloor mX^{\prime}\rfloor}+O(e^{-\mathtt{C}_{1}n})\end{split}

In the above expression, there is a double pole at
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}
since
V_{F}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
is analytic for
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}
.
We split the above integral into two and notice that we need to evaluate

(122)

\frac{4a}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{p}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}}{\omega_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{p^{-1}}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\frac{\omega_{2}V_{F}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})^{-\lfloor mY\rfloor}}{(\omega_{2}-\omega_{1})^{2}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})}\frac{\omega_{1}^{2m}}{\omega_{2}^{2m}}

with
Y=X^{\prime}
and
Y=X
. We proceed to evaluate (
122
).
By using

\frac{\omega_{2}}{\omega_{2}^{2}-\omega_{1}^{2}}\frac{1}{\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}}=\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\frac{1}{(\omega_{2}-\omega_{1})^{2}}+\frac{1}{2\omega_{1}}\bigg(\frac{1}{\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}}-\frac{1}{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}}\bigg)\bigg)

we split up the integral in (
122
) which gives

\begin{split}&\frac{2a}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{p}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}}{\omega_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{p^{-1}}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\frac{V_{F}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})^{-\lfloor mY\rfloor}}{(\omega_{2}-\omega_{1})^{2}}\frac{\omega_{1}^{2m}}{\omega_{2}^{2m}}\\
&+\frac{a}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{p}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}}{\omega_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{p^{-1}}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\bigg(\frac{1}{\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}}-\frac{1}{\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}}\bigg)V_{F}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\\
&\times T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})^{-\lfloor mY\rfloor}\frac{\omega_{1}^{2m}}{\omega_{2}^{2m}}.\\
\end{split}

For the second integral above, we use a change of variables in the second term of
\omega_{2}\mapsto-\omega_{2}
and so we get that (
122
) equals

\begin{split}&\frac{2a}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{p}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}}{\omega_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{p^{-1}}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\frac{V_{F}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})^{-\lfloor mY\rfloor}}{(\omega_{2}-\omega_{1})^{2}}\frac{\omega_{1}^{2m}}{\omega_{2}^{2m}}\\
&+\frac{a}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{p}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}}{\omega_{1}}\int_{\Gamma_{p^{-1}}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\frac{V_{F}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})-V_{F}(\omega_{1},-\omega_{2})}{\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}}T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})^{-\lfloor mY\rfloor}\frac{\omega_{1}^{2m}}{\omega_{2}^{2m}}.\\
\end{split}

Here to simplify the change of variables we have used that
G(\omega)=-G(-\omega)
, which implies that
T(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=T(\omega_{1},-\omega_{2})
.
To compute the asymptotics of the above integrals, we use a saddle point analysis, which we omit the full details as these are given fully in
[
CJ16
, Section 3.6]
. We let
\omega_{c}
be such that
g^{\prime}_{Y,Y}(\omega_{c})=0
with
Y=X
or
Y=X^{\prime}
. We have that
|\omega_{c}|=1
and
0<\arg\omega_{c}<\pi/2
; see (
101
) and
[
CJ16
, Lemma 3.18]
. In each of the integrals, we deform the
\Gamma_{p}
(the
\omega_{1}
-contour) to the contour of steepest descent and
\Gamma_{1/p}
(the
\omega_{2}
-contour) to the contour of steepest ascent, which results in the contours crossing; see
[
CJ16
, Lemma 3.20]
for a precise description of these contours. For each integral, the contour crossing picks up a single integral term from the residue at
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}
which has a double pole in the first integral and a simple pole in the second integral. The contribution from both double integrals (with the contours deformed to that of the steepest ascent and descent) is
O(1)
which follows from standard saddle point analysis.
We obtain that (
122
) equals a sum of single integrals, plus an
O(1)
term:

(123)

\begin{split}&\frac{2a}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{1}\backslash\Gamma_{\omega_{c}}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\bigg[\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}}\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}\frac{\omega_{1}^{2m}}{\omega_{2}^{2m}}T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})^{-\lfloor mY\rfloor}V_{F}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\bigg]_{\omega_{2}=\omega_{1}}\\
&+\frac{a}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{1}\backslash\Gamma_{\omega_{c}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega}{\omega}V_{F}(\omega,-\omega)-V_{F}(\omega,\omega)+O(1).\end{split}

We have that the second term above is also
O(1)
. It remains to compute the first term. We have that (
123
) equals

\begin{split}&\frac{2a}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{1}\backslash\Gamma_{\omega_{c}}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\bigg[\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}}\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}\frac{\omega_{1}^{2m}}{\omega_{2}^{2m}}T_{G}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})^{-\lfloor mY\rfloor}V_{F}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\bigg]_{\omega_{2}=\omega_{1}}+O(1)\\
&=\frac{2a}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{1}\backslash\Gamma_{\omega_{c}}}\mathrm{d}\omega\bigg(-\frac{2m}{\omega^{2}}+\lfloor mY\rfloor\frac{G^{\prime}(\omega)}{G(\omega)\omega}+[mY]\frac{G^{\prime}(\omega^{-1})}{G(\omega^{-1})\omega^{2}}\bigg)V_{F}(\omega,\omega)+O(1)\\
&=-\frac{2a}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{1}\backslash\Gamma_{\omega_{c}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega}{\omega}2mg_{\lfloor Ym\rfloor,\lfloor Ym\rfloor}^{\prime}(\omega)V_{F}(\omega,\omega)+O(1).\end{split}

We can compute that
V_{F}(\omega,\omega)=\omega/a
, which allow us to compute the above integral explicitly. Set
\omega_{c}=e^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{c}}
which means that (
122
) when
Y=X
is equal to
p_{1}(\theta_{c})
. We can also set
\omega_{c}=e^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{c}^{\prime}}
which means that (
122
) when
Y=X^{\prime}
is equal to
p_{1}(\theta_{c})
. The result follows from taking the difference. ∎

We now prove
5.4
.

Proof of
5.4
.

For
x\in(-n^{8/15},2n^{1/3}\log^{2}n]
, the bound follows from
5.3
. For
-\delta n<x\leq-n^{8/15}
and for fixed
\delta>0
small, the bound follows from
12.3
along with a Taylor expansion (in
x/n
), while for
x\leq-\delta n
, the bound follows immediately from
12.3
.
∎

13.
Proof of strengthened result of
[
BCJ18
]

Here, we prove
6.7
. We adopt a similar notation and conventions used in
[
BCJ18
]
to make later comparisons to that paper simpler. This helps to keep our exposition as brief as possible. We translate back to the coordinate system in the remainder of the paper only as necessary.

13.1.
Particle System from
[
BCJ18
]

We give a similar albeit slightly altered particle system to the one used in
[
BCJ18
]
. Let
\hat{\beta}_{m}\to\hat{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}
, and introduce

\hat{\beta}_{m}(k)=2\lfloor\hat{\beta}_{m}\lambda_{2}(2m)^{2/3}\rfloor+2\lfloor k\lambda_{2}\log^{2}m\rfloor.

where
1\leq k\leq M
with
M\to\infty
with
n
and
M=o(m^{1/3}\log^{-2}m)
. For
\ell\in 2\mathbb{Z}
, let

\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\varepsilon}(\ell)=\{v=(2i-\varepsilon+\frac{1}{2})e_{1}-\ell e_{2}:i\in\mathbb{Z},v\in[-n,n]^{2}\},

let
\mathcal{L}_{m}(\hat{\beta}_{m}(k))=\mathcal{L}_{m}^{0}(\hat{\beta}_{m}(k))\cup\mathcal{L}_{m}^{1}(\hat{\beta}_{m}(k))
and let

\mathcal{L}_{m}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{m}(\hat{\beta}_{m}(k)).

For
z\in\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\varepsilon}(\hat{\beta}_{m}(k))
, write
\varepsilon(z)=\varepsilon
. If
\varepsilon(z)=0
, then
z
has
even
parity and if
\varepsilon(z)=1
, then
z
has
odd
parity. This relates to the sign of the height function when crossing the edges in the north-east direction along the diagonal of the Aztec diamond. For a particle
z\in\mathcal{L}_{m}(\hat{\beta}_{m}(k))
, associate the vertex
x(z)\in\mathtt{W}_{0}\cup\mathtt{W}_{1}
and the vertex
y(z)\in\mathtt{B}_{0}\cup\mathtt{B}_{1}
and the edge
(y(z),x(z))
between them, that is the dimer covering the two vertices
y(z)
and
x(z)
. Our convention means that

(124)

\begin{array}[]{ll}&x(z)=z-\frac{1}{2}(-1)^{\varepsilon(z)}e_{2},\\
&y(z)=z+\frac{1}{2}(-1)^{\varepsilon(z)}e_{2},\end{array}

see
Figure

25
.

Figure 25
.
The particles to edge mapping.

This gives the particle to edge mapping:

There is a particle at

\displaystyle z\in\mathcal{L}_{m}\qquad

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\qquad

There is a dimer at

\displaystyle(x(z),y(z))\in\mathtt{White}\times\mathtt{Black}.

We have the following proposition.

Proposition 13.1
.

The particle process on
\mathcal{L}_{m}
defined above is a determinantal point process with correlation kernel
\mathcal{K}_{m}
given by

(125)

\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(z,z^{\prime})=a\mathrm{i}K_{a,1}^{-1}(x(z^{\prime}),y(z))

for
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{m}
.

This proposition follows immediately from the local statistics formula, see
10.1
, and the fact that
K_{a,1}(y(z),x(z))=a\mathrm{i}
for
z\in\mathcal{L}_{m}
. We let
\mathbb{I}\subset\mathcal{L}_{m}
denote the point process in
13.1
.

The particle processes on
\mathcal{L}_{m}
can be used to reconstruct height differences between faces along each of the lines
\mathcal{L}_{m}(\hat{\beta}_{m}(k))
. Indeed, for two distinct faces of the form
f=ae_{1}-\hat{\beta}_{m}(k)e_{2}
and
f^{\prime}=be_{1}-\hat{\beta}_{m}(k)e_{2}
with
a<b
, we can write

(126)

\mathcal{H}_{n}(f^{\prime})-\mathcal{H}_{n}(f)=4\sum_{z\in\mathbb{I}}(-1)^{z(\varepsilon)}\mathbf{1}(z=xe_{1}-\hat{\beta}_{m}(k)e_{2}\text{ for some }x\in(a,b)).

Through (
126
) and averaging, we can then construct differences of the mollified height function
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}
with
\phi_{n}=\phi_{n}=\{2\lfloor k\log^{2}n\rfloor e_{2}:k\in\{0,\dots,M\}\}
from the particle process
\mathbb{I}
.

Following
[
BCJ18
]
, introduce
\tau_{m}=\lfloor\hat{\beta}^{2}_{m}\lambda_{1}(2m)^{1/3}\rfloor
. It will be convenient to use the following parameterization. Given
z\in\mathcal{L}_{m}(\hat{\beta}_{m}(k))
, there is a
t(z)\in\llbracket-2\lfloor m\xi_{c}\rfloor+\tau_{m},4m-2\lfloor m\xi_{c}\rfloor+\tau_{m}\rrbracket
such that

z=(\lfloor 4m\xi_{c}\rfloor+2(t(z)-\tau_{m})-\varepsilon(z)+1/2)e_{1}-\hat{\beta}_{m}(k)e_{2}.

Write for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,

z_{k}(s)=(\lfloor 4m\xi_{c}\rfloor+s-2\tau_{m}+1/2)e_{1}-\hat{\beta}_{m}(k)e_{2}

with
z_{k}(s)\in\mathcal{L}_{m}(\hat{\beta}_{m}(k))
. The function
s\mapsto z_{k}(s)
and
z\mapsto t(z)
are
almost
inverses, with minor differences coming in so as to account for parity. We note that in the
\beta_{n}
-coordinates introduced prior to
5.3
, that

(127)

z_{k}(s)=\beta_{n}(-\hat{\beta}_{m}(k),s+o(n^{1/3})).

where the
o(n^{1/3})
appears only because in the
\beta_{n}
-definition we centered around the limit curve
\xi
, whereas in the definition of
t(z)
above, our centering is around the second-order Taylor approximation to
\xi
at
(\xi_{c},\xi_{c})
(this is where the parabolic correction
\tau_{m}
is coming from).

Next, for
L>0
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\alpha_{1,m}<\alpha_{2,m}^{\prime}\leq\alpha_{2,m}<\alpha_{3,m}^{\prime}\leq\alpha_{4,m}<\dots\leq\alpha_{L,m}<\alpha_{L+1,m}^{\prime}=\log^{3}m

be real numbers such that
\alpha_{i,m},\alpha_{i,m}^{\prime}\to\alpha_{i}
, where the limiting sequence satisfies

\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\dots<\alpha_{L}\leq\alpha_{L+1}:=\infty.

We allow
\alpha_{L}\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, whereas all
\alpha_{i},i\leq L-1
must be real.
Now let
A_{p}=[\alpha_{p},\alpha_{p+1})
for
1\leq p\leq L
and define disjoint discrete intervals

\tilde{A}_{p}=\{s\in\mathbb{Z}:2\lfloor\alpha_{p,m}\lambda_{1}(2m)^{1/3}\rfloor-1\leq s\leq 2\lfloor\alpha_{p+1,m}^{\prime}\lambda_{1}(2m)^{1/3}-4\rfloor+1\}.

Here the separation by
2
is simply to guarantee that the intervals
\tilde{A}_{p}
are disjoint.
The embedding of the interval
\tilde{A}_{p}
as a discrete interval in
\mathcal{L}_{m}(k)
is then given by

I_{p,k}=\{z_{k}(s):s\in\tilde{A}_{p}\}.

We write
\mathbb{I}_{p,k}(z)=\mathbf{1}(z\in I_{p,k})
for the indicator function for
I_{p,k}
. Now, define the random measure
\mu_{m}
by the formula

(128)

\mu_{m}(\tilde{A}_{p})=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{I}}(-1)^{\varepsilon(z)}\mathbb{I}_{p,k}(z).

With the mollifier
\phi_{n}
as above, and notation as in (
25
), (
26
), by (
126
) we have that

\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(\beta_{n},\alpha_{i+1,m}+o(1))-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\phi_{n}}(\beta_{n},\alpha_{i,m}+o(1))=4\mu_{m}(\tilde{A}_{p})

where the
o(1)
terms are coming from scaling translation in (
127
). Through this translation, and since the approximating sequences
\alpha_{i,m}
were arbitrary, we can rewrite
6.7
as the
L=1
case of the following theorem.

Theorem 13.2
.

In the above setup, there exists
R>0
such that for all
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{L}
with
|\omega_{p}|<R
we have

(129)

\begin{split}\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}e^{\sum_{p=1}^{L}\omega_{p}\mu_{m}(\tilde{A}_{p})}=\mathbb{E}e^{\sum_{p=1}^{L}\omega_{p}\mu_{\mathrm{Ai}}(A_{p})}\end{split}

Here
\mu_{\mathrm{Ai}}
is the counting measure for the Airy point process
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}(0):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

The term on the left is a determinant of a finite matrix, while the term on the right is a Fredholm determinant, noting that the interval
A_{L_{2}}
is semi-infinite. The proof is developed over the next few subsections, with heavy reference to
[
BCJ18
]
. Note that we are only considering one fixed time in
13.2
as opposed to multiple time intervals in
[
BCJ18
, Theorem 1.1]
.

13.2.
Using the determinantal point process

Now, it follows from (
128
) that

(130)

\sum_{p=1}^{L}\omega_{p}\mu_{m}(\tilde{A}_{p})=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{I}}\psi(z)

where

\psi(z)=\sum_{p=1}^{L}\sum_{k=1}^{M}\omega_{p}(-1)^{\varepsilon(z)}\mathbb{I}_{p,k}(z).

As for the correlation kernel of the determinantal point process, with
10.6
in mind, define

\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{m,0}(z,z^{\prime})=a\mathrm{i}\mathbb{K}_{1,1}^{-1}(x(z^{\prime}),y(z))

and

\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{m,1}(z,z^{\prime})=a\mathrm{i}\mathbb{K}_{A}(x(z^{\prime}),y(z)).

Define for
z\in\mathcal{L}_{m}(k)
,

\begin{split}&\gamma_{1}(z)=\frac{t(z)}{\lambda_{1}(2m)^{1/3}}\hat{\beta}_{m}-\frac{1}{3}\hat{\beta}_{m}^{3}\\
&\gamma_{2}(z)=\varepsilon(z)+\hat{\beta}_{m}(k)\\
&\gamma_{3}(z)=2(t(z)-\tau_{m})+\hat{\beta}_{m}(k).\end{split}

and let
\mathcal{K}_{m,\delta}
be such that

(131)

\begin{split}&\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{m,\delta}(z,z^{\prime})=\frac{a\mathrm{i}}{2}\sqrt{1-2c}\mathtt{g}_{\varepsilon(z^{\prime}),\varepsilon(z)}|G(\mathrm{i})|^{2\varepsilon(z^{\prime})-2}(-1)^{\varepsilon(z^{\prime})}e^{\gamma_{1}(z^{\prime})-\gamma_{1}(z)}\\
&\times|G(\mathrm{i})|^{\gamma_{2}(z)-\gamma_{2}(z^{\prime})}\mathrm{i}^{\gamma_{3}(z^{\prime})-\gamma_{3}(z)}{\mathcal{K}}_{m,\delta}(z,z^{\prime})\end{split}

for
\delta\in\{0,1\}
. We set

\mathtt{K}_{m,\delta}(z,z^{\prime})=\frac{a\mathrm{i}}{2}\sqrt{1-2c}\mathtt{g}_{\varepsilon(z^{\prime}),\varepsilon(z)}|G(\mathrm{i})|^{2\varepsilon(z^{\prime})-2}(-1)^{\varepsilon(z^{\prime})}\mathcal{K}_{m,\delta}(z,z^{\prime})

and let
\mathtt{K}_{m}=\mathtt{K}_{m,0}-\mathtt{K}_{m,1}.
The above two equations indicate that
\mathtt{K}_{m}
is the correlation kernel of the determinantal point process after conjugation and along with (
130
), we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}e^{\sum_{p=1}^{L}\omega_{p}\mu_{m}(\tilde{A}_{p})}&=\det[\mathbbm{I}+(e^{\psi/M}-1)\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{m}]_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}\\
&=\det[\mathbbm{I}+(e^{\psi/M}-1)\mathtt{K}_{m}]_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}.\end{split}

We have the following proposition, which is an extended version of
[
BCJ18
, Proposition 3.1]
.

Proposition 13.3
.

Let
z\in\mathcal{L}_{m}(k)
,
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{m}(k^{\prime})
and write
t=t(z)
,
t^{\prime}=t(z^{\prime})
. Consider
\mathcal{K}_{m,\delta}(z,z^{\prime})
defined by (
131
).
The asymptotic formulas and estimates below are uniform as
m\to\infty
for
-C_{1}(2m)^{1/3}<t,t^{\prime}\leq C_{1}(2m)^{1/3}(\log m)^{3}
, for any fixed
C_{1}>0
and
1\leq k\leq M
.

1.

We have

\mathcal{K}_{m,1}(z,z^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(2m)^{1/3}}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\frac{t^{\prime}}{\lambda_{1}(2m)^{1/3}};\frac{t}{\lambda_{1}(2m)^{1/3}}\right)(1+o(1)).

where
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(x;y)=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(0,x;0,y)
is given in (
23
).

2.

Assume that
k>k^{\prime}
. Then there are constants
c_{1},c_{2},C_{1}>0
, so that:

(a)

If
|t^{\prime}-t|\leq c_{2}((k-k^{\prime})(\log m)^{2})^{7/12}
, then

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}_{m,0}(z,z^{\prime})&=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(\log m)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi(k-k^{\prime})}}\\
&\times\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4(k-k^{\prime})}\left(\frac{t^{\prime}-t}{\lambda_{1}\log m}\right)^{2}\right)(1+o(1)).\end{split}

(b)

If
c_{2}((k-k^{\prime})(\log m)^{2})^{7/12}\leq|t^{\prime}-t|\leq\lambda_{2}(k-k^{\prime})(\log m)^{2}
, then

|\mathcal{K}_{m,0}(z,z^{\prime})|\leq\frac{C}{(\log m)\sqrt{k-k^{\prime}}}\exp\left(-\frac{c_{1}}{(k-k^{\prime})}\left(\frac{t^{\prime}-t}{\lambda_{1}\log m}\right)^{2}\right).

(c)

If
|t^{\prime}-t|\geq\lambda_{2}(k-k^{\prime})(\log m)^{2}
, then

|\mathcal{K}_{m,0}(z,z^{\prime})|\leq Ce^{-c_{1}(k-k^{\prime})(\log m)^{2}}.

3.

Assume that
k<k^{\prime}
. Then there are constants
c_{1},C>0
so that

|\mathcal{K}_{m,0}(z,z^{\prime})|\leq Ce^{-c_{1}(k^{\prime}-k)(\log m)^{2}}.

4.

Assume that
k=k^{\prime}
. Then there are constants
c_{1},C>0
so that

|\mathcal{K}_{m,0}(z,z^{\prime})|\leq Ce^{-c_{1}|t^{\prime}-t|}.

Proof of
13.3
.

13.3
differs from
[
BCJ18
, Proposition 3.1]
in that it allows the parameters to enter the smooth region (by at most
\log^{3}m
amount). The asymptotics for statements 2, 3, 4 hold from statements 3, 4, 5 from
[
BCJ18
, Proposition 3.1]
. Statement 1 holds by
10.6
. ∎

13.3.
Proof of
13.2

We give a condensed proof of
13.2
by signposting, where appropriate, to the proof of
[
BCJ18
, Theorem 1.1]
. The main difference is that here, the support interval of
\mu_{m}
is no longer compact.

For a set
S
, we write
S^{r}=\{\overline{k}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{r}):k_{i}\in S\text{ for all }i=1,\dots,r\}
. We adopt cyclic notation, that is,
k_{r+1}=k_{1}
.
Let

D_{r}=\{0,1\}^{r}\times[M]^{r}\times[L]^{r}

where
[N]=\{1,2,\dots,N\}
.
Similar to
[
BCJ18
]
,
define

\displaystyle D_{r,0}

\displaystyle=\{(\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})\in D_{r};\delta_{i}=0,k_{i}=k_{i+1},p_{i}=p_{i+1}\mbox{ for }1\leq i\leq r\},

\displaystyle D_{r,1}

\displaystyle=\{(\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})\in D_{r};\delta_{i}=0\mbox{ for }1\leq i\leq r\mbox{ and }p_{i}\neq p_{i+1}\mbox{ for some $i$}\},

\displaystyle D_{r,2}

\displaystyle=\{(\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})\in D_{r};\delta_{i}=0,p_{i}=p_{i+1}\mbox{~for~}1\leq i\leq r\mbox{~and~}k_{i}\not=k_{i+1}\mbox{~for some~}i\},

\displaystyle D_{r,3}

\displaystyle=\{(\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})\in D_{r};\delta_{i}=1\mbox{~for some~}i\}.

Then
D_{r}=D_{r,0}\cup D_{r,1}\cup D_{r,2}\cup D_{r,3}
. Introduce

(132)

\begin{split}T_{j}(m,r,\overline{\ell})=&\sum_{\overline{\varepsilon}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}(-1)^{\ell_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}\sum_{(\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})\in D_{r,j}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{p_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}\\
&\times\sum_{\overline{z}\in(\mathcal{L}_{m})^{r}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathbbm{I}_{p_{i},k_{i}}^{\varepsilon_{i}}(z_{i})\mathtt{K}_{m,\delta_{i}}(z_{i},z_{i+1}),\end{split}

for
j\in\{0,1,2,3\}
where
\mathbbm{I}_{p,k}^{\varepsilon}
is the indicator on
\mathcal{L}_{m}
for the set

(133)

I_{p,k}^{\varepsilon}=\{z\in I_{p,k}:\varepsilon(z)=\varepsilon\}.

By performing a cumulant expansion, see
[
BCJ18
, Equations (4.1)-(4.6)]
for details, we can write

\log\det(\mathbbm{I}+(e^{\frac{1}{M}\psi}-1)\mathtt{K}_{m})=\sum_{j=0}^{3}U_{j}(m)

where we define

(134)

U_{j}(m)=\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{M^{s}}\sum_{r=1}^{s}\frac{(-1)^{r+1}}{r}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{r}=s\\
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{r}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{T_{j}(m,r,\overline{\ell})}{\ell_{1}!\dots\ell_{r}!}.

We have that
U_{0}(m)
,
U_{1}(m)
, and
U_{2}(m)
all tend to zero as
m\to\infty
by
[
BCJ18
, Lemma 4.1 and Lemma 4.2]
, since the same proofs hold as the same estimates are present in
13.3
as
[
BCJ18
, Proposition 3.1]
for
\mathcal{K}_{m,0}
. It remains to consider
U_{3}(m)
.
We introduce some additional notation similar to that of
[
BCJ18
]
. Define

z_{k}^{\varepsilon}(t)=(\lfloor 4m\xi_{c}\rfloor+2(t-\tau_{m})-\varepsilon+\frac{1}{2})e_{1}-\hat{\beta}_{m}(k)e_{2},

and set

A_{p,m}=\{t\in\mathbb{Z}:\lfloor\alpha_{p}\lambda_{1}(2m)^{1/3}\rfloor\leq t\leq\lfloor\alpha_{p+1}\lambda_{1}(2m)^{1/3}-\log^{3}m\rfloor\}.

We these definitions, we have that

I_{p,k}^{\varepsilon}=\{z_{k}^{\varepsilon}(t):t\in A_{p,m}\}.

Next, define

S_{r}(\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}\mathrm{d}^{r}\overline{t}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathbbm{I}_{A_{p,m}}(\lfloor t_{i}\rfloor)\mathcal{K}^{(i)}_{m,\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k}}(t_{i},t_{i+1})

where

\mathcal{K}^{(i)}_{m,\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k}}(t_{i},t_{i+1})=\mathcal{K}_{m,\delta_{i}}(z_{k_{i}}^{\varepsilon_{i}}(\lfloor t_{i}\rfloor),z_{k_{i+1}}^{\varepsilon_{i+1}}(\lfloor t_{i+1}\rfloor)).

With this notation, following
[
BCJ18
, (4.7)-(4.10)]
we have

T_{j}(m,r,\overline{\ell})=\sum_{\overline{\varepsilon}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}P(\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\ell})\sum_{(\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})\in D_{r,j}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{p_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}S_{r}(\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{\ell},\overline{p})

with

P(\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\ell})=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{a\mathrm{i}}{2}\sqrt{1-2c}(-1)^{(1+\ell_{i})\varepsilon_{i}}\mathtt{g}_{\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{i+1}}|G(\mathrm{i})|^{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{i+1}-2}.

Introduce

(135)

d_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\lambda_{1}(2m)^{1/3}&\mbox{if }\delta_{i}=1\\
(\log m)\lambda_{1}\sqrt{|k_{i+1}-k_{i}|}&\mbox{if }\delta_{i}=0,k_{i}\not=k_{i+1}\\
1&\mbox{if }\delta_{i}=0,k_{i}=k_{i+1}\end{array}\right.

and the change of variables
\tau_{i}=t_{i}/d_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq r
.
After this change of variables, we obtain

S_{r}(\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}\mathrm{d}^{r}\overline{\tau}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathbbm{I}_{A_{m}}(\lfloor\tau_{i}d_{i}\rfloor)d_{i}\mathcal{K}^{(i)}_{m,\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k}}(\tau_{i}d_{i},\tau_{i+1}d_{i+1}).

We have the following bound.

Lemma 13.4
.

There is a constant
C>0
such that

|S_{r}(\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})|\leq C^{r}

for all
(\overline{\delta},\overline{k})\in D_{r,3}
and
\overline{\varepsilon}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is the same as the one given in
[
BCJ18
, Lemma 4.3]
, noting the extended ranges given in
13.3
.
∎

Let

D_{s,3}^{*}=\{(\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})\in D_{s,3}:k_{i}\not=k_{j}\text{ for all }i\not=j\}

and let
Q(\overline{\varepsilon})=P(\overline{\varepsilon},(1,\dots,1))
. Then define

U_{3}^{*}(m)=\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{s+1}}{sM^{s}}\sum_{\overline{\varepsilon}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}Q(\overline{\varepsilon})\sum_{(\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})\in D_{s,3}^{*}}\prod_{i=1}^{s}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}\omega_{p_{i}}S_{s}(\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})

Lemma 13.5
.

There is a constant
C>0
such that for
R
sufficiently small and all
|\omega_{p}|\leq R
,
U_{3}(m)
is uniformly convergent and
|U_{3}^{*}(m)-U_{3}(m)|<C/M
.

Proof.

Both results are a consequence of using the bound in
13.4
; see
[
BCJ18
, Lemma 4.4 and Lemma 4.5]
for further details.
∎

The above lemma means that we focus on
U_{3}^{*}(m)
. For
(\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})\in D^{*}_{s,3}
, let
1\leq j_{1}<\dots<j_{r}\leq s
be the indices
i
where
\delta_{i}=1
. Let

\ell_{1}=j_{1}-j_{r}+s,\quad\ell_{2}=j_{2}-j_{1},\dots,\quad\ell_{r}=j_{r}-j_{r-1}.

Observe that
\ell_{i}\geq 1
for all
i
and
\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{r}=s
and that we can uniquely reconstruct
j_{1},\dots,j_{r}
from
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{r}
provided
1\leq j_{1}\leq\ell_{1}
is given. Write
J=\{j_{1},\dots,j_{r}\}
and
J^{\prime}=[s]\backslash J
. Then, we have

\begin{split}&S_{r}(\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}\mathrm{d}^{r}\overline{\tau}\prod_{i\in J}\mathbbm{I}_{A_{p_{i},m}}(\lfloor\tau_{i}d_{i}\rfloor)d_{i}\mathcal{K}^{(i)}_{m,\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k}}(\tau_{i}d_{i},\tau_{i+1}d_{i+1})\\
&\times\prod_{i\in J^{\prime}}\mathbbm{I}_{A_{p_{i},m}}(\lfloor\tau_{i}d_{i}\rfloor)d_{i}\mathcal{K}^{(i)}_{m,\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k}}(\tau_{i}d_{i},\tau_{i+1}d_{i+1})\end{split}

We can use
13.3
(statements (2) and (3)) to write that for
i\in J^{\prime}

(136)

\begin{split}&d_{i}\mathcal{K}^{(i)}_{m,\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k}}(\tau_{i}d_{i},\tau_{i+1}d_{i+1})\\
&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}e^{-\frac{(\tau_{i}-\frac{\tau_{i+1}d_{i+1}}{d_{i}})^{2}}{4}}(1+o(1))\mathbbm{I}_{|\tau_{i}-\frac{d_{i+1}}{d_{i}}\tau_{i+1}|\leq c_{2}(\log m)^{1/6}}\mathbbm{I}_{k_{i}>k_{i+1}}\end{split}

Next we have that on
\lfloor d_{i}\tau_{i}\rfloor\in A_{p_{i},m}
with
i\in J
,

d_{i}\mathcal{K}^{(i)}_{m,\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k}}(\tau_{i}d_{i},\tau_{i+1}d_{i+1})=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\tau_{i};\tau_{i+1})(1+o(1))

by Statement (1) in
13.3
. The fact that the Airy functions decay when
\tau_{i}\to\infty
means that we can write

\begin{split}&\lim_{m\to\infty}S_{r}(\overline{\varepsilon},\overline{\delta},\overline{k},\overline{p})=\prod_{i\in J^{\prime}}\mathbbm{I}_{k_{i}>k_{i+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}\prod_{j\in J}\mathrm{d}\tau_{j}\times\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathbbm{I}_{A_{p_{i}}}(\tau_{j_{i}})\tilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(\tau_{j_{i}},\tau_{j_{i+1}}\big)\end{split}

where we have computed the Gaussian integral from (
136
) and used the indicator functions for the integral with respect to
\tau_{i}
for
i\in J
.
The proof now proceeds exactly as in
[
BCJ18
]
in that one has to use

\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{1}{M^{s}}\sum_{\overline{k}\in[M]^{s}}\prod_{i\in J^{\prime}}\mathbbm{I}_{k_{i}>k_{i+1}}=\frac{1}{\ell_{1}!\dots\ell_{r}!}.

along with
[
BCJ18
, Lemma 4.6]
that states that
\sum_{\overline{\varepsilon}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}Q(\overline{\varepsilon})=(-1)^{s}
, and a resummation to get the right side of the statement of the theorem; we omit these details since they can be found in
[
BCJ18
]
.

14.
Proof of results from Section
10

We now give the proofs of the results given in
Section

10
, including those relating to the refined asymptotic analysis of
K^{-1}_{a,1}
.

14.1.
Proof of
10.3

The next lemma is a restatement of
[
CJ16
, Lemma 3.6]
and so will be stated without proof.

Lemma 14.1
.

Rescale and center the Aztec diamond so that
x=([4m\eta_{1}]+\overline{x}_{1},[4m\eta_{1}]+\overline{x}_{2})\in\mathtt{W}_{\varepsilon_{1}}
and
y=([4m\eta_{2}]+\overline{y}_{1},[4m\eta_{2}]+\overline{y}_{2})\in\mathtt{B}_{\varepsilon_{2}}
for
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
and
-1<\eta_{1},\eta_{2}<0
where
\overline{x}_{1},\overline{x}_{2},\overline{y}_{1},\overline{y}_{2}
are at most order
m^{5/6}
, there are positive constants
\mathtt{C}_{1}
and
\mathtt{C}_{2}
so that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{a}\mathcal{B}_{1-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(a^{-1},n-x_{1},n+x_{2},n-y_{1},n+y_{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathtt{C}_{1}e^{-\mathtt{C}_{2}n},

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{a}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{1},1-\varepsilon_{2}}(a^{-1},n+x_{1},n-x_{2},n+y_{1},n-y_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathtt{C}_{1}e^{-\mathtt{C}_{2}n},

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{B}_{1-\varepsilon_{1},1-\varepsilon_{2}}(a,n-x_{1},n-x_{2},n-y_{1},n-y_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathtt{C}_{1}e^{-\mathtt{C}_{2}n}.

The statement in
[
CJ16
, Lemma 3.6]
differs slightly in that the vertices
x
and
y
are at the same asymptotic coordinate (that is,
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}
) and that

\overline{x}_{1},\overline{x}_{2},\overline{y}_{1},\overline{y}_{2}
is at most order
m^{2/3}
. However, it follows from the proof of the lemma given there that we can immediately extend to the conditions given in the above lemma.

Next, set

(137)

\begin{split}&Q_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}^{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}\left(\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\right)=(-1)^{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}+\gamma_{1}(1+\varepsilon_{2})+\gamma_{2}(1+\varepsilon_{1})}\\
&\times s\left(G\left(\omega_{1}\right)\right)^{\gamma_{1}}s\left(G\left(\omega_{2}^{-1}\right)\right)^{\gamma_{2}}G\left(\omega_{1}\right)^{\varepsilon_{1}}G\left(\omega_{2}^{-1}\right)^{\varepsilon_{2}}\mathtt{x}^{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}\left(a,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}^{-1}\right),\end{split}

where notation is as in (
82
